<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how different beings relate to time and duration. The previous page explored the nature of "the now" and natural motion; here, the author extends these concepts to spiritual beings (angels and souls) before transitioning to a formal definition of eternity and the "aeon" (evum).</meta>

# Physics

<note>original: "Physicorum"; likely a commentary on Aristotle's *Physics*.</note>

...and it is never the case that one is simultaneously under grace and, in some respect, under sin, just as in a change from white to black, there is the interception of a middle color <note>The author is arguing that certain spiritual transitions are absolute, unlike physical changes (like color) which pass through intermediate stages.</note>. And similarly, this concept of time applies to the motion of an angel, insofar as we say that angels move in time and understand in time, according to the discourse of angelic intelligence over things that can be understood in **evening knowledge**. I say "in **evening knowledge**" <note>original: "cognitione uespertina"; in medieval theology, this refers to the knowledge angels have of things as they exist in their own created nature.</note> because, according to **morning knowledge** <note>original: "cognitionem matutinam"; the knowledge angels have of things as they exist eternally within God.</note>, they reach eternity. And this motion of both the soul and the angel is measured by a **successive now** <note>original: "nunc uicissitudinatū"</note>, insofar as the soul or angel moves from one object of understanding to another; and every such act of it is perfect. And this is what *Augustine* says: "That God moves the bodily creature through time and place, but the spiritual creature through time only." For in this kind of time, it is not inconsistent for there to be no immediate successor to the "now" without an intervening gap. It should also be noted that time is more the cause of decay than of coming-into-being <note>original: "plus cā corruptionis q̃ generationis"</note> because of the change it measures, which causes a thing to distance itself from its beginning; for that which grows old and ages is near its end. Let these remarks on time suffice.

->On Eternity. Chapter 15.<-

Since we have spoken of time, which is the measure of temporal things, we must now speak of **eternity**, which is the measure of eternal things, and of the **aeon** <note>original: "euo" or "aevum"; in medieval philosophy, this is a third type of duration specifically for created spirits like angels, sitting between the "time" of the physical world and the "eternity" of God.</note>, which is the measure of "everlasting" <note>original: "euiternoꝝ"</note> things. For there are some things, as it is said in the *Book of Causes* <note>original: "lib. de causis"; a widely studied Neoplatonic text in the Middle Ages.</note>, whose substance and action are in time, such as "lower nature"—that is, that whose being exists through the process of birth and becoming [generation]. Some things, however, are those whose substance and action are above time, such as the "supreme nature"—namely, God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Truly, there are some things whose substance is above time, but whose action is in time, such as "middle nature," whose being exists through creation <note>This refers to human souls or angels, who are created at a specific point but then exist forever.</note>. A fourth category cannot exist, for in that case, the action of a thing would exist before its substance, which is impossible.

Furthermore, these three measures are understood in relation to duration. For there is a type of duration having both a beginning and an end, such as **time**. There is another having neither beginning nor end, such as **eternity**. And there is a third having a beginning but no end, such as the **aeon** [evum]. Again, a fourth category cannot exist, for everything that has an end must also have a beginning.

*Boethius* defines eternity in this way in the book *The Consolation of Philosophy* <note>original: "lib. de cōse." (De Consolatione Philosophiae)</note> near the end: "**Eternity is the total, simultaneous, and perfect possession of an unending life.**" This definition can be explained as follows: "possession" is common to every measure, for every measure is "possessed" by that which it measures as its own proper attribute. By using the phrase "of an unending life," he touches upon the subject of eternity itself—namely, the eternal God, who has neither beginning nor end—though this subject is not distinct from eternity except in our logical understanding <note>original: "secūdū rōnēm"; conceptually distinct but essentially the same in reality.</note>. And he says "of life" rather than "of being" in order to establish a difference from those things which exist in the eternity of punishments, which do not possess a blessed or joyful life. For "life," though it can be used in a general sense for any kind of living, is here specifically used to mean a joy...

<summary>This page concludes the discussion of spiritual motion in time and introduces the definitions of eternity and the 'aeon' (aevum). It classifies beings into three categories based on whether their substance and actions exist within or above time, using Boethius's famous definition of eternity.</summary>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues a scholastic investigation into the nature of time and duration, specifically contrasting the fleeting "now" of the physical world with the "standing now" of eternity. It builds on the previous discussion of angelic knowledge and the movement of spiritual beings.</meta>

# Physics

...blessed and happy. It is called "all at once" to distinguish it from time, which is not all at once, but rather one part has passed and another part is in the future. And it does not use "at once" to imply a **simultaneity of parts** <note>original: "simultate partium"</note>, but rather "at once" meaning there is no distance or interval. It is called "perfect" to distinguish it from that which is not entirely simple, just as that which it measures is not simple; for "perfect" is used here in place of "simple." 

And note that eternity is spoken of **analogically** <note>original: "anologice"; meaning the word is used in related but different ways</note>—that is, in a primary and secondary sense—regarding both the Creator and his creatures. For there are three conditions of eternity properly so-called: namely, that it is **simple**, **invariable**, and **interminable** <note>original: "iterminabile"; meaning boundless or without limit</note>. In this context, "interminable" refers not only to a lack of an end, but also to a lack of a beginning; in this sense, it belongs to the Creator alone. For He truly is the only one who is simple, invariable, and without beginning or end. 

However, certain creatures participate in the name of "interminability" in their own way. For example, angels and blessed souls, and even bodies after the resurrection, participate in the name of interminability because they lack an end, though they did have a beginning. Because of this, angels, blessed souls, and resurrected bodies are said to be **eternal** and to exist **in eternity**. They are called "eternal" because of their lack of an end; they are said to be "in eternity" because of their lack of change or **invariability**. 

Furthermore, some creatures participate in eternity by reason of interminability because they lack an end—such as the **punishments** <note>original: "pene"; referring to the eternal suffering of the damned</note> which are called eternal—but they are not said to be "in eternity" because they do not participate in the state of invariability. For "invariability" or "unchangeableness" denotes a state of well-being. But this condition of **simplicity** is found "simply" in no creature. 

Additionally, eternity signifies a superior measure regarding both its beginning and its end. Thus, some creatures, such as those that are generated and subject to decay, are excluded from eternity; they are neither eternal nor do they exist in eternity. 

Therefore, some things are eternal and in eternity in the proper sense [God]. Some are not so in the proper sense: of these, some are eternal and in eternity through **participation** <note>Angels and souls, who share in God's changelessness</note>; some are eternal through participation but not "in eternity" [the damned]; and some are neither eternal nor in eternity but are excluded from eternity altogether. A fourth category cannot exist: namely, that something could be "in eternity" through participation but not be "eternal." For everything that is unchangeable lacks an end, but the reverse is not necessarily true.

---

->**On the "now" of eternity. Chapter 16.**<-

---

Next follows the "now" of eternity, of which *Boethius* says in his book *On the Trinity* that "the now that stands still and does not move itself makes eternity." This is better understood by making a comparison to the "now" of time. 

Let us imagine a line, **a** to **c**, and let **b** be a point moving on that line from **a** to **c**, distinguishing what is before from what is after. We call the first part "passed," and the latter part "to be passed." Similarly, understand the line **a-c** to represent all of time, and understand **b** to be the **now of time** itself, which moves continuously from **a** to **c**. That which has been passed, we call the "past"; that which is to be passed, we call the "future." And just as on a physical line the moving point **b** always subtracts from the space ahead and adds to the space behind...

<sig>b iii</sig>

<summary>This page defines eternity through the three conditions of simplicity, invariability, and interminability, distinguishing between God's absolute eternity and the "participatory" eternity of angels and souls. It introduces the Boethian concept of the "standing now" (nunc stans) as the foundation of eternity, contrasted with the moving "now" of linear time.</summary>

<keywords>eternity, Boethius, time, now, angels, souls, invariability, interminability, scholastic philosophy, duration</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of eternity versus time, moving from the definition of eternity as a "simultaneous whole" to how God’s eternal "now" interacts with the past, present, and future of the physical world. It concludes by introducing the concept of "aevum," a third category of duration.</meta>

<header>Physics</header>

...for in this way, the **now** <note>original: "nunc"; the present moment</note> always subtracts from the future and adds to the past. If a point *b* were to stay at position *a* and not move, there would be neither a "before" nor an "after." In the same way, if the "now" of time had stood still at the beginning of time, there would have been neither past nor future. 

Along with this "now" of time, let us imagine a certain "now" that has stood still from eternity. In that eternity, there would be no past or future at all, but everything would be present and all at once. Thus, it exists in eternity, but not in time. For all of eternity is a certain **indivisible now** <note>original: "nunc indivisibile"</note>. But now something wonderful appears: that this very "now" of eternity—though it is indivisible—extends itself through all of time, encompassing even infinite time. Furthermore, it encompasses all of time, and yet the whole of it stands alongside any given instant of time. 

A good example of this is that the **soul** <note>original: "anima"</note> is whole in the whole body, and whole in every part of the body. Likewise, although the soul is simple and lacks parts (unlike the body), it nevertheless extends itself through the whole body.

There is another wonder here: that within the "now" of eternity, which is indivisible, time is contained, even though time is infinitely divisible and has parts that are "before" and "after." An example to help understand this is as follows: **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a foundational Christian philosopher</note> says that in the same instant of time, a ray of sun is in the East and the West, suddenly illuminating our entire hemisphere. Here, therefore, it happens that in a single "now" of time, there is a "before" and "after" to infinity; for naturally—even if not according to the measurement of time—the nearest part (the very closest air) is illuminated or changed before the part that is further away. And that further part is illuminated before the part that is even more remote, and thus one finds parts stretching to infinity. 

From what has been said, it is clear what it means to say "God is," "God was," or "God will be." For the meaning is: **God is** in the "now" of eternity, with which present, past, and future time coexist. This also clarifies the solution to a certain logical point which argues that whatever God knows, He will never know its contradictory opposite. For in the same "now" of time, it is impossible to know two contradictory things, such as "sitting" and "not sitting" at once. But the "now" of eternity is infinitely simpler than the "now" of time; therefore, since God knows in the "now" of eternity, it might seem He cannot know two contradictory things—but this is false. He knows me to be while I am, and He knows me not to be when I am not. The solution is clear: because the eternal "now" is present with every part of time.

### On "Aevum" and Everlasting Beings. Chapter 16.

<image-desc>A large decorative rubricated initial 'S' in red ink with swirling extensions, beginning the word 'Sequitur'.</image-desc>
**It follows** <note>original: "Sequitur"</note> to speak of **aevum** <term>aevum: a mode of time for beings like angels who have a beginning but do not change or end</term>, which can be defined in this way: *Aevum* is the measure of those things having a beginning but no end. And it should be noted that some say that just as time is found in one thing—namely, in the **motion of the first moved sphere** <note>original: "motu primi mobilis"; in medieval cosmology, the outermost sphere of the heavens whose rotation causes all other time and motion</note>, by which motion all lower motions are measured as by the smallest and simplest of its kind, and consequently is the measure of the motion of the first moved sphere—so *aevum* is in one simplest of everlasting beings, by which all other everlasting beings are measured. Others...

<sig>iii d</sig>

<summary>This page explains the "standing now" of eternity, using the analogy of the soul in the body to show how an indivisible point can encompass all of time. It introduces "aevum" as the specific measurement of duration for beings that are created but immortal, such as angels.</summary>

<header>On the Heavens and the World.</header>

Others, however, say that this is not the case, but rather that every **everlasting being** <note>original: "eviternum"; beings that have a beginning but no end, like angels</note> possesses its own *aevum* <note>original: "evum"; a state of duration between time and eternity</note>. For no everlasting being is the cause of others, in the same way that the motion of the **First Mover** <note>original: "primi mobilis"; the outermost sphere of the heavens that sets all others in motion</note> is the cause of other motions. From the things said above, it can be gathered that an **angel**, in regard to its substance, exists in *aevum*; but in regard to its simple actions—such as in **evening knowledge** <note>original: "cognitione vespertina"; a Scholastic term for an angel's knowledge of created things as they exist in their own nature, rather than as they exist in God</note>—it exists in time. Yet, in regard to acting well and blessedly—such as in contemplating and enjoying God—it exists in **eternity**. Let these things said concerning the first part suffice.

### The second part begins, on the heavens and the world. Chapter 1.

Since we have already spoken about the "mobile body" <note>a physical object capable of change or movement</note> in itself in the books of the *Physics*, we must now speak consequently of the **specific mobile body**. And first, we must speak of that which is **ungeneratable and incorruptible** <note>original: "ingenerabili & incorruptibili"; things that were not born and cannot decay, which medieval thinkers believed described the stars and planets</note>, which is the heaven. For as **Aristotle** <note>original: "Ari."; the Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose work formed the basis of medieval science</note> says at the beginning of the *Physics*: "One must proceed from universals to particulars." This is why children call all men "fathers" and all women "mothers"; only later do they learn to distinguish each one specifically. First, therefore, it must be shown that there is only **one physical world** and not many. Second, that the heaven is **finite** <note>has a physical limit or edge</note>. Third, that all physical things are contained within the heaven. Fourth, that...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical discussion of duration (eternity and time) to the physical structure of the cosmos, specifically focusing on the heavens as the "First Mover" introduced on the previous page.</meta>

<header>On the Heavens and the World.</header>

The heaven is neither an element nor composed of elements <note>The author distinguishes the celestial "ether" from the four earthly elements: fire, air, water, and earth</note>. Fifth, one must speak concerning the nobility of the heaven in respect to lower corporal things. Sixth, concerning motion.

### That there is only one world. Chapter 2.

Aristotle <note>original: "Arif."</note> proves that there is only one corporal world and not many in this way: Let us suppose that there are two worlds. Either that other world will be similar to this one in its things, or in name only. If it were similar in its things, then that earth would be similar to this earth, and vice versa. And that water would be similar to this water, and so with the others. But things which are of the same nature tend toward the same place <note>In Aristotelian physics, "earth" naturally moves toward the center of the universe; if two earths existed, they would both strive for the same single center</note>. Therefore, this earth and that one tend toward the same place, and this water and that one, and so on for the others. But whatever things tend toward the same place belong to the same world; therefore, all things are of one world. Thus, there is only one corporal world and no other. If it is said that the other world is similar to this one in name only, then since there are no other things than the things of this world, that world would not be real or corporal, but only intellectual, or an archetype <note>original: "architippus"; referring to the Platonic idea of a conceptual "blue-print" world in the mind of God</note>, concerning which there is no doubt that it exists.

### That the heaven is finite. Chapter 3.

Before we prove the heaven to be finite, it must first be noted that, as Aristotle says in the third book of the *Physics*, "infinite" is spoken of in many ways. In one way, "infinite" is said of that which is impossible to cross because it is not by nature intended to be crossed, such as an invisible voice; and thus "infinite" is said negatively, as if it were not finished or not terminated; and in this way God is said to be infinite. In another way, "infinite" is said of something that has a passage but is incomplete, because while it is suited to have a passage, it does not yet have one—just as when someone crosses a certain space but does not reach the end. In these two ways, something is said to be actually infinite. In a third way, "infinite" is said according to addition, as in discrete quantity <note>such as numbers, which can always have another added to them</note>. In a fourth way, "infinite" is said according to division, as in continuous quantity <note>such as a line that can be divided into smaller and smaller parts</note>. In a fifth way, it is said in both ways, such as time. In these latter three ways, something is not called "actually infinite," but only "potentially infinite." According to this, the infinite is defined in the same work: "The infinite is that which, when one takes its quantity, there is always something more to be taken outside of it." It remains to prove that the heaven is not actually infinite.

And first, it is proven generally that no body is actually infinite, thus: Every body has a surface, and every surface is a boundary; therefore, every body is bounded. But everything bounded is finite; therefore, every body is finite. And from this it can be inferred: but the heaven is a body; therefore, the heaven is finite. Likewise, we prove specifically that the heaven is finite. Let us suppose, therefore (as is proven below), that every body is in the heaven and none is outside it. Hence: among all bodies, the heaven is the most noble. For it is ungenerable <note>it was not created through natural biological or chemical processes</note> and incorruptible <note>it does not decay or change</note>, possessing active power in respect to lower things. Moreover, every agent is more noble than the "patient" <note>original: "patiente"; the object that receives the action or is acted upon</note>, as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> says. But a nobler form is owed to a nobler body; and the spherical <note>original: "spica," likely a contraction for "spherica"</note> form is more noble than all other corporal forms or figures because of its capacity and its lack of edges, because it does not have—

<summary>This page discusses two major cosmological questions: why there is only one physical world and why the heavens must be finite in size. Using Aristotelian logic, the author argues that the heaven's "nobility" and physical nature require it to be a finite sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical demonstration of the universe's structure, moving from the unity of the world to the specific shape and boundaries of the heavens.</meta>

# On the Heavens and the World.

...beginning, nor an end, and not contained by more than one surface. For a sphere is a solid figure <note>original: "figura plana"; in this medieval context, the author likely means a "simple" or "uniform" figure, as a sphere is defined by a single, continuous surface rather than multiple faces like a cube</note> contained by a single surface; therefore, a spherical form is appropriate to the heaven; therefore, the heaven is spherical or round. Furthermore, we see that when the moon is half-full, it appears arched. But that shape does not exist except in a circle or from a circle. For a circle is only found in a round body; therefore, the moon is round. By the same reasoning, the sun and all other celestial bodies are round; therefore, the heaven is round. From this, let us proceed further in this way: A spherical body is one in whose middle a point is understood from which all lines drawn to the circumference are equal; therefore, the heaven has a center <term>medium: the center or middle point of a geometric body</term>; and everything that has a middle has extremes; but that which has extremes is terminated or finite; therefore, the heaven is finite.

Likewise, the heaven is proven to be finite in comparison to motion, and the measure of that motion, which is time. For everything that is moved entirely in a certain or finite time is finite; but the heaven is moved entirely in a certain or finite time, namely in one natural day <note>A "natural day" refers to a full 24-hour rotation of the heavens</note>; therefore, the heaven is finite. That an infinite motion cannot be measured by a finite time is demonstrated thus: Let that time be represented by the line *a-b*, and let it be divided into four parts. One part of the time will measure one part of the motion, and another part another, and so on for the rest. But since the motion is infinite, it is not yet entirely measured by a finite time; therefore, infinite motion is not measured by a finite time. In the same way, it is demonstrated that a finite motion is not measured by an infinite time, by dividing the motion into four parts. For motion and time ought to be proportional, just as a subject and its own property <term>passio: in scholastic philosophy, an attribute or characteristic that is "suffered" or possessed by a subject</term>, so that neither exceeds the other. And thus, if the time measuring the motion is finite, the motion is finite; and if the motion is finite, the body which is moved is finite. It remains, therefore, that the heaven is finite.

->That all things are in the heaven and nothing is outside.<-

Now, therefore, we must prove that all bodies are within the heaven and none are outside. And first, we will prove that no body is naturally outside the heaven. Second, that none are there accidentally or by force.

->That no body is naturally outside the heaven is proven thus:<-

There are three kinds of bodies: namely, the heaven, the elements, and that which is composed of elements <term>elementatum: any compound material thing—like a plant or animal—formed from the four elements</term>. That the heaven is not outside the heaven is clear, because nothing is outside itself; therefore, the heaven is not outside the heaven. Similarly, it is clear that no element is outside the heaven, because among all elements, earth is the lowest, above which is water, above which is air; therefore, by the same proportion, fire is above air. Since, therefore, all elements are beneath fire, and fire is beneath the heaven, it follows that no element is naturally outside the heaven. Likewise, heavy things naturally tend downward toward the center. Light things [tend] upward toward the circumference, which is the heaven and not beyond; therefore, no element is naturally outside. Similarly, it is clear that no compound body is outside the heaven. For every compound body consists of the elements...

<meta>This page concludes the logical demonstration that no physical body exists outside the celestial sphere and begins a new chapter arguing that the heavens are distinct from the four earthly elements (earth, water, air, and fire).</meta>

# On the Heavens and the World.

...[proper]ties: but no element is outside the heaven, as has been proven above; therefore no composite body <term>elementatum: a physical body formed by the mixing of the four elements (fire, air, water, and earth)</term> is outside the heaven. And so it is clear that no body exists outside the heaven naturally <term>naturaliter: according to a thing's inherent nature, such as a stone falling toward the center of the earth</term>. In the same way, it is clear that no body exists outside the heaven accidentally or by force <term>accidentaliter: due to an outside force rather than an inner nature</term>. For in whatever place some body can exist naturally, in that same place it is possible for some body to exist accidentally or by force; for example, earth is naturally at the bottom, but fire [can be held there] by force. Similarly, fire is naturally at the top, but earth [can be held there] by force. But since no body is naturally outside the heaven, as has been proven, it follows that no body is there by force. And so it is clear that no body at all exists outside the heaven. And this is what John of Damascus says: That the heaven is the container of visible and invisible creatures <note>original: "Quod celum est continentia uisibilium & inuisibilium creaturarum"</note>.

### That the heaven is neither an element nor composed of elements. Chapter 4.

<margin>1</margin>Now we must show that the heaven is neither an element nor composed of elements. That it is not an element is clear, because it is the property of every element to have within itself a contrariety <note>In medieval science, elements were defined by opposing qualities: hot vs. cold, dry vs. wet</note>, and to act upon its contrary: if it is joined to its contrary, it destroys it if it prevails, and generates its own likeness in that matter. If the heaven were an element, then—since all the elements are in relation to the heaven like a point is to the whole earth—it would have long ago destroyed everything else, and all things would be "heaven," which we do not see. In the same way, it is clear that the heaven is not composed of elements. For every composite body is smaller than the elements from which it is composed <note>This likely refers to the volume of the concentrated elements versus the vast, unmixed expanse of the celestial sphere</note>; but the heaven is greater than all the elements; therefore it is not a composite. Likewise, it is clear at once that the heaven is neither a composite nor an element: for every such thing has a straight motion, that is, downward toward the middle or upward away from the middle. But the heaven does not have straight motion, but circular motion. Therefore, it is neither a composite body nor an element. If it were said that straight motion is natural to it, but circular motion is accidental, we argue against this: accidental motion increases and decreases in speed. If, therefore, circular motion were accidental to the heaven, it would speed up or slow down. However, we do not see this in the heaven, because the motion of the heaven is always uniform. From what has been said, it is now clear that the heaven was not "generated" <note>In this context, "generated" means having come into being through the physical transformation of pre-existing matter</note>. For everything that is generated is generated from a contrary principle or from contrary principles, as Aristotle says in the first book of *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: "Arif. in primo de celo & mūdo"</note>. Plato, however, says in the *Timaeus* <note>original: "Thimeo"</note> that it is generated; but he meant "generated" in the sense of having a beginning or a cause for its existence. From the preceding, it is also clear that the heaven neither heats up nor cools down, because it is neither an element nor composed of elements. For these are the proper qualities of the elements, namely: hot and cold, moist and dry. And since it does not receive these types of impressions, it is certain that it is no more "old" now than it was at the beginning of its creation. And it is also clear that although from the aggre[gate]...

It reconciles the views of Aristotle and Plato regarding the eternal or created nature of the celestial sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the celestial spheres, specifically addressing how they influence the Earthly realm without sharing the same physical qualities, followed by chapters on the "nobility" (the superior status) of the heavens based on their height, shape, and eternal motion.</meta>

# On the Heaven and the World.

...[propa]gation of light that comes from the sun, we feel heat; nevertheless, the celestial bodies <term>corpora celestia: the stars and planets</term> are not themselves hot. Yet they are called "hot," like the sun, or "cold," like Saturn, or "moist," like the moon, because of their effects. For the superior bodies act upon the inferior ones <term>inferioribus: the "lower" world of the four elements (Earth)</term>, and not the other way around, as is evident. We see, for instance, that when the moon has the fullness of its light, the sea rises, and as the moon wanes, the sea recedes. From this it is clear that the moon is the cause and "mother" of moisture; therefore Aristotle says in the book *On the Properties of the Elements* <note>original: "lib. de proprietatibus elementorum"; a work attributed to Aristotle in the Middle Ages, though now considered pseudo-Aristotelian</note> that when all the planets were gathered together in the sign of Pisces, a great flood occurred. When, however, they were gathered in the sign of Gemini, a great mortality <note>A period of mass death or plague</note> took place. Nevertheless, you should always understand this to be the judgment of God, with the divine will ordaining these events. It is also clear from what has been said that the heaven is not colored, because color is caused by the qualities of the elements <note>Since the heavens are made of a "fifth element" or quintessence, they lack the qualities of the four earthly elements that produce color</note>.

### On the nobility of the celestial bodies. Chapter 5.

The nobility <note>In this context, "nobility" refers to the ontological perfection, purity, and superior status of the heavens</note> of the heaven or the superior bodies is manifest from its elevation above all inferior bodies. For the more noble a body is, the higher the place that is owed to it. Likewise, the nobility of the superior bodies is evident from their figure or form, which is roundness. Roundness is more noble than all other shapes, as Aristotle says in the book *On the Heaven and the World*; this is clear from the fact that all other figures have boundaries <note>referring to angles or edges where a line stops and changes direction</note>. A round figure, however, has a circular form, and a circle has neither beginning nor end, but is terminated within itself. That all superior bodies are round was shown above. And because the heaven is round, all inferior bodies are round, and for this reason they are finite. For everything round has a center, and every center has extremes <note>outer boundaries or circumferences</note>, and that which has an extreme is finite. That all inferior bodies are round is manifest. For fire, since it is contained by the heaven which is round, has a round form, as Aristotle says in the book *On the Heaven and the World*; because of this, it is finite, and so it is for the others. Note, however, that the earth is uneven <note>original: "discorporata"; literally "un-bodied" or "disfigured," referring to the fact that the Earth's surface is broken by mountains and valleys rather than being a perfect, smooth sphere</note>, because God willed that humans should inhabit it. Furthermore, the nobility of the superior bodies is clear from their impassibility <term>impassibilitate: the quality of being unable to suffer change, decay, or harm from external forces</term>; for they are incapable of being generated, unchangeable, and indestructible, yet they possess an active power in respect to inferior things.

### On the motion of the heaven. Chapter 6.

And because the heaven is more noble than all inferior bodies, a more noble motion is owed to it: namely, a circular motion that is by nature perpetual, and free from all pain and fatigue. That the motion of the heaven is perpetual is manifest from the fact that the principle <note>the source of power or "mover"</note> moving it cannot be separated from it. For that principle is either inside or outside. If it were outside, then the motion of the heaven would be accidental or forced; but every forced or accidental thing passes away easily, as [it is] sa[id]...

<summary>This page explains that celestial bodies influence Earth (like the tides and astrological events) through their effects rather than shared physical qualities. It further argues that the heavens are "noble" due to their height, their perfect circular shape, and their eternal, effortless motion.</summary>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nobility and physical properties of the heavens, moving from the qualities of the celestial bodies (like light and heat) to the nature of their eternal, circular motion. It addresses why celestial motion differs from the movements we observe on Earth.</meta>

<header># On the Heaven and the World.</header>

Aristotle is cited in the book *On the Heaven and the World*. If, therefore, the motion of the heaven were "violent" <term>violentus: in medieval physics, this refers to "forced" motion that goes against an object's natural inclination, like throwing a stone upward</term>, it would have already passed away long ago. It remains, therefore, that this principle <note>The internal source or "spark" of movement</note> is within the heaven itself; but everything that is within another cannot be separated or removed from it except through "passion" <note>Being acted upon by an external force</note> or some contrariety. But the heaven is impassible <note>Incapable of being changed or harmed by external forces</note>, having no contrariety within it; therefore, that principle of motion is never removed from it. Thus, the motion of the heaven is perpetual.

Likewise, it is clear that circular or celestial motion is perpetual according to its nature because it has no intensification or weakening, but is always uniform; nor does it tend more toward acquiring "this location" than "that location." Furthermore, the motion of the heaven does not tend toward reaching any final destination; therefore, it is perpetual by its very nature.

From the aforesaid, it appears that the motion of the heaven is neither "natural," nor "violent," nor "animate" <note>original: "animalis"; motion caused by a soul, like that of a living creature</note>. That the motion of the heaven is not "natural" <note>In the sense of the four earthly elements</note> nor "violent" is clear from the fact that all such motion is "straight" <note>Rectilinear motion, moving in a straight line from point A to point B</note>. Let us suppose, then, that some body—whether moving naturally, accidentally, or by force—moves through some line or finite space; for indeed, all such space is finite. That body eventually traverses that space since it is finite; therefore, it will either stop there or it will not. If it stops, it is then at rest; therefore, that motion was not perpetual. If it does not stop, it must return, because it cannot proceed further <note>Assuming the universe is finite</note>. Thus, it would return either at the same time or at another time. If at the same time, then it would be going and returning simultaneously, which means two opposites would exist in the same thing at once, which is impossible. If it returns at another time, then there was a period of rest between those two times; therefore the motion had ceased, and thus it was not perpetual. But it has been proven that the motion of the heaven is perpetual according to its nature; therefore, it is neither "natural" <note>in the earthly, linear sense</note> nor "violent."

Also, natural motion either speeds up or slows down; the motion of the heaven does not; therefore, it is not natural in that way. Likewise, Avicenna <note>A famous Persian philosopher and physician whose works deeply influenced medieval European thought</note> proves that the motion of the heaven is not natural because natural motion is a journey from one point to another and always tends toward acquiring a goal; once that goal is acquired, the moving object rests. But the motion of the heaven does not acquire any single place in which to rest; therefore, it is not natural.

This same point is proven by another reason: because those things that move naturally and are of the same species and nature move toward the same direction, just as all "heavy" things <note>Earth and water</note> descend toward the center and all "light" things <note>Air and fire</note> move upward. Therefore, since the heaven is of the same species and nature throughout, if its motion were "natural," it would move toward only one direction, and there would be no diversity of movement there. However, the opposite is proven in astronomy; for Aristotle says in the book *On the Heaven and the World* that the eighth sphere <note>The "Firmament" or the sphere of the fixed stars</note> moves from East to West, while the other seven spheres <note>The spheres of the planets, including the Sun and Moon</note> move against the course of the "first mover" <term>primum mobile: the outermost sphere that gives motion to all others</term>. Therefore, they do not all move toward the same direction, and so the motion of the heaven is not natural.

The same is proven thus: the motion of the heaven is the "principle" or source of nature and of all natural things, because through that motion all natural things are generated, decayed, and changed. That which is the cause of something is stronger and more noble than the thing it causes. And so, since that motion is the source of natural things, it is not subject to the laws of nature; therefore, it is not "natural" but "supernatural."

That the motion of the heaven is not "violent" was proven above: for every violent thing passes away quickly. Similarly, it is shown that it is not "animate" <note>The motion of a living creature</note>, because Aristotle says that animate motion does not occur without pain and labor, nor without a stopping point...

<summary>This page argues that celestial motion is unique because it is perpetual, uniform, and circular, distinguishing it from the "natural" linear motion of earthly elements and the "animate" motion of living beings. The author concludes that because the heavens cause natural change on Earth, their own motion is "supernatural" and not subject to earthly decay.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Avicenna, celestial motion, perpetual motion, circular motion, four elements, eighth sphere, primum mobile, medieval physics, supernatural motion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of celestial motion, moving from the physical properties of the spheres to the metaphysical drivers of their movement—the "desire" of the angels for their Creator. It transitions into a structural overview of the heavens, counting the spheres from the outer stars down to the moon and mapping them according to theological and philosophical systems.</meta>

# On the Heaven and the World.

The motion of the heaven, moreover, is without pain or labor, and is without end; therefore, this motion is not "animate" <note>original: "animalis"; referring to the motion of living creatures, which eventually grow tired or reach a destination</note>. Likewise, animate motion intensifies in the middle [of its path], whereas the motion of the heaven does not intensify; therefore, it is not animate. Beyond these types of motion, it is therefore necessary to propose another motion for the heaven—namely, circular—as Aristotle says in the book *On the Heaven and the World*. Avicenna <note>A Persian polymath whose works were essential to medieval science; he sought to harmonize Aristotelian philosophy with Islamic theology</note> says similarly of this motion that it is not animate in a simple sense, but by way of comparison. This is because, just as the human soul moves the human body and the body obeys it, so too do the angels, because they know and see their Creator, move those spheres out of joy and desire. Thus, the first sphere, which is the greatest of spheres, is moved by the first order [of angels] out of such desire. Hence, that motion is always called "the motion of desire," or simply "desire" <note>original: "motum desiderii sive desiderium"</note>. And the second sphere is moved by the second order, and so on for each in sequence. Hence he [Avicenna] says that the angels are like souls, yet they are not strictly speaking souls. And for this reason, that celestial motion is called "quasi-animate," yet it is not called strictly animate.

## On the number of the heavens. Chapter 6.

<image-desc>Large decorative red initial 'E' set within a circular frame with horizontal lines.</image-desc>
And note that eight celestial spheres are distinguished. The first of these is that in which the fixed stars are, which are innumerable, as Aristotle says. The second sphere is of Saturn, in which there is only Saturn. The third is of Jupiter; the fourth of Mars; the fifth of the Sun; the sixth of Venus; the seventh of Mercury; and the eighth of the Moon. Hence the verse: *There are eight spheres, which you will be able to remember thus: the Starry heaven, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, the Sun, Venus, followed by Mercury, with the Moon being the last.*

Beneath the moon, however, there are only corruptible things <term>corruptible: in medieval cosmology, things in the "sublunary" world made of the four elements that can change, decay, or die</term>, such as Fire and Water and other corruptible things, as Macrobius <note>A 5th-century author whose writings on the soul and the cosmos were foundational for medieval thinkers</note> says. Ptolemy <note>An ancient Greek astronomer whose Earth-centered model of the universe was the standard until the 16th century</note>, however, assigns another sphere above these, in which are the angels and spiritual and incorruptible things; and that sphere is motionless. 

Theologians, however, distinguish seven heavens: namely, the Aerial, the Ethereal, the Fiery, the Starry, the Crystalline, the Empyrean, and the Heaven of the Trinity. Their number can be understood in this way: "heaven" refers either to a created thing or an uncreated thing. If uncreated, it is the Heaven of the Trinity. If created, it refers to either a corruptible or an incorruptible thing. If corruptible, it is either in respect to the extremes or the middle. If in respect to the extremes, it is the Aerial and the Fiery heaven; if in respect to the middle, it is the Ethereal heaven. If, however, it refers to a created incorruptible thing, then again it is either in respect to the extremes or the middle. If in respect to the extremes, it is the Starry heaven and the Empyrean heaven <note>The highest, most luminous part of the heavens, often considered the dwelling place of God and the angels</note>. If in respect to the middle, it is the Crystalline heaven. 

And note that the motion of the eighth sphere is said to be uniform. But the motion of the planets and the other lower spheres is said to be twofold, just as the motion of the sun is twofold: namely, the "tropic" or daily motion, and the "immobile revolution" <note>The planet's own orbital path</note> in the opposite direction of the first mobile, which is its "proper motion" <term>proper motion: the individual movement of a planet along the zodiac, distinct from the daily spin of the whole sky</term>. The tropic motion is called "turning" or "consensual." Hence all planets in astronomy are called "tropic" because they move backward [from east to west] following the motion of the first mobile <term>Primum Mobile: the outermost moving sphere that drags all other spheres in a 24-hour cycle</term>. This motion is driven by such great desire that it revolves all other spheres along with it. But the motion called the "immobile revolution" is the proper and natural motion of the planets. Wherefore...

<meta>This page transitions from the celestial physics of the upper spheres to the terrestrial physics of the four elements. # On Physics

...or whether it is moved by the hope <note>original: "spe"; likely referring to the influence or aspectual relationship between the planets</note> of the planets, as was said, against the progression of the first mover <note>original: "primi mobilis"; the outermost sphere in medieval cosmology that moves all others</note>: various reasons are assigned by various people. For some say that this happens to slow down the motion of the first mover. For unless, as they say, that motion were slowed down, the whole system would be broken because of its excessive speed, or would have been shattered long ago. Others, however, say that this is for the sake of humans and other things that are upon the earth. Because if the motion of the first mover, which is driven by such great desire <note>Referring to the "desire" of the celestial spheres to emulate the perfection of the Divine, as discussed on the previous page</note>, did not have some slowing down, nothing could ever grow upon the earth, nor could humans be able to live. And let these things said about the heaven suffice.

->**Here begins the third part, namely, concerning the elements.**<-

After having spoken of the mobile body that is un-generable and incorruptible <note>The "quintessence" or ether that makes up the heavens, which medieval thinkers believed never decayed or changed</note>, we must now speak of the mobile body that is generable and corruptible <note>The physical matter of the earthly realm which can be created and destroyed</note>: and first concerning the simple, afterward concerning the composite. Simple generable and corruptible bodies are the elements. Whence now we must speak of the elements, which are arranged below the heaven in their respective positions. This is done so that that which is more subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" means less dense or more "refined" and spiritual</note> than the others is closer to the heaven: just as fire, because it is more subtle than air, is closer to the heaven than air; and air closer than water; and water closer than earth. 

First, therefore, let us set forth the opinions of the ancient philosophers concerning the elements. Second, we shall speak of the primary qualities of the elements. Third, we shall discuss the generation and corruption <note>The process of one element turning into another, such as water turning into air through boiling</note> of the elements into one another. For the sake of this, we shall also speak of alteration and other species of their motion. <margin>Note</margin> Before we set forth the opinions concerning the elements, it must be noted that the term "element" is used in many ways. 

For in one way, it is called a complex principle of an art <note>An "axiom" or foundational rule</note>, such as: "Every whole is greater than its part." And that rule: "Concerning any subject, there is either an affirmation or a negation, and never both at the same time," and similar logic. Whence Aristotle says in the book *Physics* <note>original: "lib. physico"</note>: "The arrangement of truth and science is found in all those things which are principles or elements," where he calls foundational principles "elements." 

It is also called an "element," as the Philosopher <note>A standard medieval title for Aristotle</note> says in the fifth book of the *Metaphysics* <note>original: "v. methaphifice"</note>, as something common to all things, which is the starting point of each thing, and the first part within each; just as letters are called the "elements" of speech, which signify meaning by convention; and similarly in other things. 

It is also called an "element" according to a more specific understanding: the starting point of any sensible mixed body. According to this, an element is defined in the fifth book of the *Metaphysics*: An element is that from which a thing is first composed, and which exists within it, and which is not divided according to its form (that is, its species) into another form. For example: in a mixture <note>A "mixed body" is any physical object made of the four elements, like a plant or a stone</note> there is water, of which every part is still water; and similarly in the other elements. And according to this, four elements are distinguished: **Fire, Air, Water, and Earth**. These all share the same underlying matter, but they differ according to their forms, namely: **Hot, Cold, Moist, and Dry**. And therefore they are called "contraries" by reason of these forms; for while in their underlying matter nothing is contrary, in the form of their substance, one thing is the opposite of another.

->**Opinions of the Ancients Concerning the Elements.**<-

<meta>This page transitions from the general study of the heavens to a specific debate regarding the "elements"—the fundamental building blocks of the physical world. # On Physics

Concerning the elements, there are various opinions among various thinkers. For some posited that there were many elements, while others posited only one. And among those who posited many elements, there was still diversity. **Empedocles** and **Aristotle** posited that there are four elements, which have been mentioned already. However, Empedocles says nothing certain about this; for he posited that the four elements were the material principles of all bodies, and he posited two formal principles, namely: **Strife** and **Friendship** <note>original: "litem & amicitia"; these represent the forces of repulsion and attraction</note>. And he said that when those qualities or differences—namely: **hot**, **cold**, **wet**, and **dry**—were separated from them, they were stuck together into one mass because of "Friendship." Afterward, when those differences or qualities arrived, they were separated because of "Strife"; and this happened after a **Great Year**, that is, after ten thousand years. From the saying of Empedocles, therefore, it was not known whether those elements were the principles of that mass, or if the mass was the principle of the elements.

**Democritus** and **Leucippus** posited that **indivisible bodies** were the principle of all bodies. Whence they also called them **atoms** <note>original: "athomos"; from the Greek "atomos," meaning "uncuttable"</note>, which they said were infinite. They proved their position thus: It is possible for a body to be divided infinitely; let it be granted, therefore, that some body is divided infinitely. Either something remains, or nothing. If nothing remains, then some body would consist of nothing, which is impossible. If something remains, it is either a body or it is not a body. If it is not a body, then something would consist of a non-body; this again is impossible. If, however, it is a body, it is either divisible or indivisible. If it is divisible, this is contrary to what was granted, for it was granted that it would be divided infinitely until it could not be divided further. If, however, it is indivisible, I have reached the proposition: namely, that indivisible bodies are the principles of all bodies.

Responding to this proof, **Aristotle** says that a body is divisible into infinity in **potential**, but not in **actuality** <note>Aristotle argues that while you can mathematically divide something forever, you cannot physically perform an infinite number of cuts to reach a "final" piece</note>. They, however, understood a body to be divisible into infinity in actuality—that is, divisible until the very end, so that it could not be divided further.

**Anaxagoras**, however, posited infinite elements, and said the opposite of Aristotle and Empedocles. For he said that those four elements—**fire**, **air**, **water**, and **earth**—were "elemented" <note>original: "elementata"; meaning they are composite things made of something more basic</note>, and all other things were the true elements, namely: **wood**, **stone**, **flesh**, etc. And that it was so, he proved in this way: You say that those four are the elements and that flesh and similar things consist of them. Therefore, either that flesh which consists of them is in them, or it is not. If it is not in them, then a "being" would consist of "non-beings," which is false. If, however, flesh is in them, then by the same reasoning so are **bone** and **marrow**, etc. Therefore, those things that are inside are the elements, and those four—namely: fire, air, water, and earth—are composed of elements. Likewise, Anaxagoras said that **anything is in anything** <note>original: "quodlibet esse in quolibet"</note>. For he said that in flesh there was bone, and again in that bone there was flesh, and again in that flesh there were nerves, and again something else in the nerves, and so on unto infinity. Aristotle, however, disproves his opinion at the beginning of the *Physics*, saying: Some flesh is the "minimum flesh," such that if that flesh were divided, it would no longer be flesh as you say. In that minimum flesh, therefore, is bone; and in that bone is another ca...

<summary>This page reviews ancient theories on the fundamental elements of matter, debating whether they are finite (Aristotle/Empedocles), indivisible (Democritus's atoms), or infinite and all-encompassing (Anaxagoras's "everything in everything").</summary>

<meta>This page continues the refutation of Anaxagoras’s theory of "homoeomery" (the idea that every substance contains tiny parts of every other substance) before transitioning into a discussion of the primary qualities—hot, cold, wet, and dry—that define the four elements.</meta>

...therefore, that flesh which is in the bone is smaller than other flesh; for everything that is contained is smaller than that which contains it. Therefore, that flesh would be smaller than the "smallest" possible flesh, and thus something would be smaller than the minimum, which is impossible. It follows, then, that the opinion of **Anaxagoras** <note>Anaxagoras (c. 500–428 BC) believed that everything contains a portion of everything else; for example, we grow hair because there are "hair-parts" in the food we eat.</note> is false. 

Regarding those who said there was only one element, some said that thing was mobile. Others said it was immobile, such as **Parmenides** and **Melissus**, who said there was only one principle: one continuous and immobile being. And they prove it is immobile in this way, saying: "Either something moves, or it does not," etc., as was said above in the first part concerning motion. **Heraclitus**, however, said there was only one material and mobile element: **fire**. And he said there were two formal principles: the **rare** and the **dense**. And he proved it in this way: Every body is mobile, and all motion is from fire, for no body is as mobile as fire; therefore, fire is the elementary component of all inferior bodies. According to how it is condensed, it becomes **air**; and when it is condensed further, it becomes **water**; and when it again becomes more thick, it is called **earth**. Wherefore, fire is the only true element.

### On the primary qualities.

<image-desc>A large, decorative red capital letter R featuring geometric line-work and circular patterns within the body of the letter, with simple flourished extensions at the top and base.</image-desc> It remains now to speak of the primary contrary qualities of the elements, which are: **hot** and **cold**, **moist** and **dry**, by which the elements are determined. For **Fire** is hot and dry; **Air** is moist and hot; **Water** is cold and moist; and **Earth** is dry and cold. 

For the four aforementioned qualities make six combinations. Of these, the four mentioned above are possible, but two are impossible: namely, **hot and cold** or **moist and dry**. Therefore, no element is determined by those two combinations, for it is impossible for contraries to exist in the same thing at the same time. These four are called "primary qualities" because they do not flow from others; rather, all other qualities or contrarieties come from these. Whence heat does not come from nor depend on dryness, nor vice versa, and so with the others. 

And that this is true is proved thus: because if heat depended on dryness, then whatever was at the highest degree of heat would also be at the highest degree of dryness. And thus Fire, which is supremely hot, would be supremely dry—which is false, as will be proved below in the definition of dryness, because **Earth** is the supremely dry element. The same reasoning applies to the others; and thus it is clear that one contrariety does not depend on the other, which is why, as was said before, they are called primary qualities. **Secondary qualities**, however, are those caused by these, such as: **hard** and **soft**, **sweet** and **bitter**, **white** and **black**, and similar things. 

Let us return, therefore, to the properties and definitions of these qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry. **Hot** is defined by **Aristotle** in the book *On Generation and Corruption* <note>original: "de generatione & corruptione"; one of Aristotle's major scientific works on how things are born and decay</note>. Heat is that which gathers things of the same kind <note>original: "homogeneorū"</note> and separates things of different kinds <note>original: "etherogenorū"</note>. For it gathers gold with gold and silver with silver, but it separates gold from iron and lead from silver. And they are called "homogeneous" from *homos* <note>original: "homos," Greek for "same"</note>, which means "one," and *genus* <note>kind or category</note>. Thence things of the same genus or species are called homogeneous. "Heterogeneous" <note>original: "Etherogena"</note> are named from *heteros* <note>original: "etheros," Greek for "other" or "different"</note>, which is "different," and *genus*. Thence...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, and dry) that define the four elements. It specifically addresses a potential contradiction in the definition of "heat" as a force that separates different substances, and begins to define "moist" and "dry" based on their ability to be contained.</meta>

# On the Elements.

...geneous <note>original: "rōgena", completing "heterogena" (heterogeneous) from the previous page.</note> things are said to be those that are of diverse kinds or species. However, an objection will be raised against the aforementioned definition: for we see that when the heat of the sun arrives, it gathers straw with mud, which are heterogeneous things; therefore, heat does not always separate them. To this it is responded: the **principal act** of heat is to heat. Its **secondary acts** are to condense, to separate, to thin out, and so on. And these secondary acts do not vary because of a change in the agent <note>The "agent" is the thing performing the action—in this case, heat.</note>, but because of the diversity of the materials. For in certain materials heat thins them out, as in wax; and in others it condenses them, as in mud. Therefore, the aforementioned definition was given in relation to the secondary act as it applies to certain materials, but not to all. 

**Cold**, however, is that which gathers both **homogeneous** <note>Things of the same kind.</note> and **heterogeneous** <note>Things of different kinds.</note> things. Thus it gathers gold with gold and gold with silver, which heat does not do. Indeed, we see in summer that flesh becomes loose, the pores open, and natural moisture and sweat exit. In winter, however, the flesh is constricted, so that the natural moisture does not exit; and it is for this reason that digestion is more active in winter than in summer. Note, however, that the principal act of cold is to make things cold. Its secondary acts are to gather homogeneous and heterogeneous things, and many others; for of one thing there is one principal act (as **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose medical and philosophical works were standard texts in Medieval Europe.</note> says), but many secondary ones. Whence **Aristotle** in the eighth book of the *Physics* says that heat can make things cold, and cold can make things hot; for example: with the arrival of heat, the pores are opened and natural humors exhale, so that the body becomes cold inside. Similarly, cold constricts the body and keeps the humors inside, so that the body becomes hot. These effects, however, happen <term>by accident<gloss>Latin: "per accidens"; a secondary or indirect result rather than a primary function.</gloss></term>. 

The **moist** is that which is difficult to contain within its own boundaries, but is easily contained by the boundaries of another thing. Whence it is clear that air is more "moist" than water <note>In Medieval science, "moist" refers to fluidity and the inability to hold a shape, rather than just wetness; thus, air is considered more "moist" (fluid) than water.</note> because it is more fluid than water. From this, the error of those who say that every body is porous is clear; because if air were porous, it would have holes and cavities where it would be bounded, and thus it would be easily contained by its own boundaries—which is false. 

The **dry**, however, is that which is easily contained by its own boundaries, but difficult to contain by the boundaries of another, such as earth, stone, and the like. And note that, as **Aristotle** says, moisture is the cause of thickness, because it brings about the thickness of the humors. Dryness, however, is the cause of thinness, because thinness comes from dryness. Likewise, note that as **Aristotle** says, of the aforementioned qualities, two are **active**—namely hot and cold—and two are **passive**—namely moist and dry. These two are called passive because they do not have such forceful action as the other two; otherwise, it would not happen that water is generated from earth, nor vice versa. For those elements agree in coldness; unless, therefore, there were a relationship of "suffering" (passion) and action between the dryness of the earth and the moisture of the water, they would not be generated from one another. For **generation** <note>The process of one substance transforming into another.</note> does not occur except in things having a contrariety of action and passion. The same is to be understood regarding air and fire. Similarly, the other two—namely hot and cold—are called active: not because they are not passive at all, but because they are less passive than the moist and the dry.

It explains that heat and cold are "active" forces, while moist and dry are "passive" properties related to how a substance holds its shape.</summary>
