<header>Meteorology</header>

Otherwise, air would not be made from water, because they agree in moisture; nor would fire be made from earth, because they agree in dryness.

### On the generation of the elements.

And because all elements are generated from one another: for instance, from one handful of earth, ten handfuls of water are made; and from one of water, ten of air; and from one of air, ten of fire. <note>This 1:10 ratio was a common medieval Aristotelian idea regarding the "thinning out" or density of the elements as they move upward from the center of the earth.</note> We must now speak of their generation, investigating first whether they were **generated** or **created**. Regarding this, Aristotle, in the book *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: "lib. de celo & mundo"</note>, sets forth an opinion which he says was Ptolemy's: that the elements were first made through the **motion of the heaven**. Against this, it is objected thus: either that motion acted upon some existing body, or upon **bare matter** <note>original: "materiam nudam"; matter without any form or qualities.</note>. 

If it acted on bare matter, this is impossible because matter is never "bare," which is shown as follows: Suppose that air acts on fire by corrupting it <note>In this context, "corrupting" means transforming one thing into another.</note>. Either the matter will be bare, or it will not. If it is not bare, I have proven my point. If it is bare, then it is not assigned to any specific place; for if it were in a place, it would possess **corporeity** <note>The quality of being a physical body.</note>. Therefore, there is no reason why "fire-ness" should be given to it in that place more than in another. But we see that wherever that action occurs, there is the placement of "fire-ness"; therefore, it is already assigned to a certain place and thus possesses corporeity. Therefore, matter is never bare. 

If, however, the motion acts on some body other than an element, then that body must have existed before the elements. Thus, the elements would not have been the "first" things, and so they would not be "elements" <note>The basic building blocks of the universe.</note> at all, which is false. It remains, therefore, that the elements are not **generated**, but **created**. This agrees with Augustine, who says that all things—that is, all the major parts of the world—were created at once in both **matter and form**.

After this, it must be known that there are two kinds of generation: namely, **simple generation** and **non-simple generation**. Simple generation is **substantial generation** <note>The creation of a whole new substance, like a plant growing from a seed.</note>. Non-simple generation is any **transmutation** or **alteration** <note>A change in quality, like something getting hotter, rather than becoming a new thing entirely.</note>. Hence, "to be generated simply" can be defined thus: **To be generated** is for matter to be stripped of one substantial form and to put on another substantial form. Since every "stripping" occurs from a contrary quality, it is necessary for generation and corruption to occur by means of the properties of the primary qualities. 

From this it is also clear that **primary matter** <note>The fundamental "stuff" of the universe before it takes any specific shape or form.</note> is ungenerated and incorruptible; for if it were generated, it would have to come from some other matter. Thus, matter would have its own matter, and one would proceed to infinity, which is impossible. Therefore, primary matter is not generated, but created. 

And note the difference between **to generate**, **to create**, and **to make**. 
- **To generate** is to make or produce something from something else. 
- **To create** is to make something out of nothing. 
- **To make** is a general term that applies to both.

From the things said above, it is clear that those elements are more easily transformed into one another if they share a quality, rather than those that share none. For it is easier to overcome one contrary quality than two. Hence, earth is more easily transformed into water than into air. For it agrees with water in one quality <note>Both earth and water are "cold."</note>, but it agrees with air in none <note>Earth is cold and dry, while air is hot and moist.</note>; and so it is with the others. And this is what Aristotle says in the first book of *On Generation and Corruption*: that whatever things have a symbol... <note>The text breaks off here; "symbol" likely refers to "symbolum" or "symbola," meaning shared characteristics or "tokens" between the elements.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the origins and transformations of the four elements, concluding that they were created by God rather than generated by celestial motion. It defines the differences between generation, creation, and simple making, while explaining that elements transform more easily when they share a common quality.</summary>
<keywords>generation, creation, primary matter, Aristotle, Augustine, four elements, substantial form, transmutation, primary qualities</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of elemental transformation, moving from the shared qualities of elements to the specific types of physical change: generation, alteration, growth, and local motion.</meta>

<header>Meteorology</header>

...agreement with one another <note>original: "convenientiam ad invicem"</note>, their transformation is swift. However, whichever elements do not share a quality are slow to change, because it is easier to transform one quality than many. And note that the **generation** of one element is the **corruption** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "corruption" simply means the passing away or destruction of one form to make way for another.</note> of another, and vice versa. Hence, some say that generation and corruption are the same in their underlying subject or substance, yet they differ according to their endpoints; just as the road from Thebes to Athens is the same as the road from Athens to Thebes, yet they are described differently according to their starting and ending points. Others say they are not the same, and they explain the previous statement like this: the generation of one is the corruption of another, and vice versa—meaning that the generation of one element is followed by the corruption of the other, and vice versa.

## On the motions of the elements.

**Alteration** follows. Alteration is a movement from one quality to another quality. By using the word "quality," I refer to an **accidental quality** <note>An "accident" is a property that can change without the thing itself turning into a different kind of substance, like a person getting a tan.</note> such as health and sickness, roundness and angularity, sweetness and bitterness, heat and cold, and the like. Alteration differs from generation because alteration is a change from one accidental quality to another accidental quality. In generation, however, there is a change from one **substantial form** <note>The essential nature that makes a thing what it is (e.g., the "fleshiness" of flesh).</note> to another substantial form. Furthermore, in alteration, the same **sensible subject** <note>A physical thing we can perceive with our senses.</note> must remain; but in generation, this is not so, for the transformation occurs according to the whole. I say "sensible subject" because of **prime matter** <note>term: <term>Prime Matter</term> (materia prima); the most basic reality of the universe that has no qualities of its own but can receive any form. It is the "clay" of the universe.</note>, which, although it remains the same during generation, is not sensible in itself. 

Next follows **growth** <note>original: "augmentum"</note> and **diminution**. Growth is usually defined as a movement from a smaller quantity to a larger quantity, and conversely, diminution is movement from a larger quantity to a smaller. And note that the "grower" and the "grown" must be both similar and different. They are similar in matter, but different or even contrary in their forms. The reason for this is that **agents** and **patients** <note>In medieval science, the "agent" is the thing doing the action, and the "patient" is the thing receiving the action.</note> must be both similar and different. The grower and the grown act upon and are acted upon by each other. It is manifest that growth is a motion. For it is necessary that nourishment or food be transformed and enter into the form of the flesh—namely, **fleshiness**—or of the bone—namely, **boniness**. Because of that "entering into," the flesh is dilated, or the bone is elongated or thickened. Such acts as dilation and the rest are motions; therefore, it is clear that growth is a motion. Conversely, it is clear that diminution is also a motion. Growth and diminution differ from alteration because alteration is concerned with **quality**, whereas growth and diminution are concerned with **quantity**.

## On change of place.

**Change of place** follows. Change of place is movement from one location to another. Such motion is threefold: **natural**, **violent**, and **animal** <note>Natural motion (like a stone falling), violent motion (like a stone thrown up), and animal motion (voluntary movement by a living soul).</note>. Since these were discussed above, we must move on to speak of **mixture**. And because **contact** <note>original: "contactus"</note> precedes mixture, we must first look at contact. To touch, or contact, as it is defined...

<sig>c ii</sig>

<summary>This page defines the different types of Aristotelian change: generation (substantial change), alteration (change in quality), growth (change in quantity), and local motion (change in place). It uses the famous analogy of the road between Athens and Thebes to explain how the creation of one thing is simultaneously the destruction of another.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotelian physics, generation and corruption, alteration, prime matter, substantial form, accidental quality, growth, local motion, accidental change</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of physical interactions, specifically the nature of physical contact and the definition of a "mixture" as understood in Aristotelian physics, transitioning from general principles to the specific study of meteorology.</meta>

<header>Meteorology</header>

Aristotle says in *On Generation and Corruption* that "contact" is spoken of in many different ways. In one way, contact is said to occur when one thing touches another without any intermediary: just as in contiguous things where their extremities are together, like a surface touching a surface. According to this view, whatever touches is itself touched. In a second way, "touch" is described by similarity: according to which we say that a distressing thing "touches" us, yet we do not touch it; and according to this view, not everything that touches is itself touched. In a third way, touch is described in continuous things whose parts are joined at a single common boundary: just as parts of a line touch each other at a connecting point, according to what Aristotle says in the *Physics*, that a point exists in order to touch. Contact of the first kind—which is the application of two surfaces without an intermediary—is necessary for "action and passion" <note>In this context, "action and passion" refers to the process of one thing acting upon another and the other being acted upon.</note>. Hence it is necessary that whenever things act and are acted upon, they touch each other: just as fire acts upon the air and heats it, and the air heats a finger and acts upon it; and in this way, it is through contact. Hence it is said that contact is the "material cause" <note>The underlying condition or requirement.</note> adapting actions and passions, just as clarity is the material cause adapting the changes of color.

### On Mixture.

Next follows the subject of mixture. Concerning this, it must first be noted that there are two types of mixture. One is according to the senses: such as when barley is placed with wheat; and this indeed appears to be a mixture, but it is not. There is another type according to truth <note>A true chemical or substantial union, rather than a mere physical heap.</note>. And this Aristotle defines in the first book of *On Generation and Corruption*: "Mixture is the union of altered mixables" <note>original: "Mixtio est miscibilium alteratorum unio"</note>. And note that all things that are "mixable" must be both active and passive. They are active in that they divide others, and passive in that they are divided. Hence it is clear that those things which do not possess contrariety <note>Opposite qualities, such as hot and cold.</note> are not mixable. It is also clear that the elements can be mixed and can make one thing out of themselves, since every element is contrary to another. However, the elements in a mixture are not divided down to "indivisibles" <note>A reference to the theory of atoms, which Aristotle rejected.</note>. For every body is divisible; but they are divided into certain tiny parts or "smallest particles" determined by nature—that is, down to the "minima according to species" <note>The smallest possible unit that still retains the identity of that specific substance.</note>. Also note that a property of a mixture is that every part of the mixture must be "homogeneous"—that is, of the same species as its whole. If it were otherwise, then it would not be a mixture according to truth. And note the difference between **composition**, **confusion**, and **mixture**. **Composition** <note>Also known as juxtaposition.</note> is when each of the components remains in its own power, and part is placed next to part, as when barley is composed with wheat. **Confusion** <note>Or "muddling."</note>, however, is when the powers of the parts are not proportioned, nor are they drawn into a new species. **Mixture**, indeed, is when the powers are proportioned and drawn into a species. Let these things said regarding the elements suffice for the present.

### Here begins the Book of Meteorology.

Having spoken of the simple, generatable mobile body, we must now speak of the composite generatable body, or "elemental compounds." Concerning these, it must first be noted that some compounds are of the "first composition": such as vapor released from both water and earth by the heat of the sun;

<meta>This page continues the transition from the general principles of mixture and contact into the specific study of meteorology. It begins by explaining how "vapors" or exhalations act as the fundamental building blocks for everything in the natural world, from weather patterns and minerals to the different levels of the soul.</meta>

<header>Meteorology</header>

...whether it remains enclosed in the bowels of the earth, or is raised into the air according to a greater or lesser degree near the triple region of the air <note>Medieval science divided the atmosphere into three layers: the lower, middle, and upper regions.</note>, and according to how diverse "passions" or impressions of the air are caused by it. For that which is raised above the earth is the principle of those things that occur in the air, according to the different regions. These include Comets, Shooting Stars <note>original: "Assub", a term derived from Arabic for shooting stars or meteors</note>, "Anaysel" <note>original: "Anaysel", likely referring to generic atmospheric impressions or luminous lances/meteors</note>—that is, a general impression—thunder, lightning bolts, flashes of light, clouds, rain, hail, snow, frost, and dew.

That which truly remains in the bowels of the earth warms the springs in winter. It is also the principle of earthquakes, and from it mineral bodies are generated, such as sulfur, from whose burning hot springs—that is, baths—are heated. Also generated are stone and quicksilver <term>quicksilver: the historical name for the element mercury</term>, which is the principle of all metals. These metals are seven, namely: Lead, Tin, Bronze, Orichalcum <note>A gold-colored alloy of copper and zinc</note>, Copper, Silver, and Gold. Electrum, however, is not a simple metal, as it is produced by both art and nature; but that produced by nature is superior. These are the four "elemented" things of the second composition <note>In this system, the "first composition" refers to the four basic elements; the "second composition" refers to homogeneous substances like minerals and metals.</note>.

There are certain "elemented" things in the middle that are neither solely above the earth nor beneath the earth. Some of these are fixed in the earth, such as the things born of the earth like trees and herbs; and some are not, like animals. Yet some posit things to be in the middle between animals and plants, such as the sea sponge, which is nourished by roots like plants and yet feels by touch like animals. But the principle of these is vapors, whether they are generated together by themselves—first, as plants from seeds—or through the mediation of rain or nutrition. For the things born of the earth are nourished from a mixture of earth and water. These are of the "third composition," in which the opposition <note>The conflict between the four qualities: hot, cold, wet, and dry.</note> is already somewhat broken, which previously prevented the elements of the first and second compositions from having a soul. Therefore, plants have a vegetative soul.

And because vapors are the principles of things born of the earth, and from these things are generated the humors of nourishment from which composite animals are nourished, and the seed or eggs are formed from which animals are generated, it is clear that vapors are the principles of irrational animals, which are of the fourth composition, and of rational ones—that is, humans—which are of the fifth composition. And because in irrational animals the opposition of the elements is more broken than in things born of the earth, they therefore receive a nobler soul, namely the sensitive soul <note>The soul that allows for movement and perception.</note>.

In man, however, because there is greater temperance <note>A perfectly balanced mixture of the elements.</note> and less actual opposition, the human body is disposed toward a soul more noble and worthy than others; and therefore it receives the most noble soul, namely the rational one. And among men, Christ <note>original: "Xps", a common medieval abbreviation for the Greek 'Christos'</note> had the most noble and worthy soul, who from the instant of his conception possessed all the treasures of the wisdom and knowledge of God—not by measure, but in fullness, of whose fullness we have all received. Therefore, because man holds the highest temperance among all other creatures and for this reason receives a nobler soul, man is rightly called the "end" or goal of all creatures. Hence Aristotle says in the second book of the *Physics*: "For we also are an end."

<meta>This page transitions from the theological and philosophical justifications for the study of the natural world to the specific classification of elements and vapors. It uses Christology—the study of Christ’s nature—as a model for understanding "union" versus "mixture," before moving into the hierarchical division of all things from inanimate minerals to rational humans.</meta>

<header>Meteorology</header>
<sig>iii b</sig>

In a certain way, the end of all men, or the end of all things, is Christ. And because man is the end of all other creatures, Christ is the end of all men. It is rightly said in the Psalms: *Christ is the end of all perfection* <note>original: "Christus finis omnis consummationis." Referring to the teleological goal or purpose of creation.</note>. For in the union of the divine and human natures, the order of the universe is completed. There was, from eternity, a union of three persons in one nature: namely, the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. And in the beginning of the creation of the angelic nature, there was one nature in one person, as is evident in an angel. Afterward, there was a union of two natures in one person, as can be seen in man; and so that the beginning might correspond to the end, it was necessary for the completion of the universe that, just as at first there was a union of three persons in one nature, so finally there should be three natures in one person, which was completed in Christ <note>Medieval theologians often described Christ as having three "natures" or components: the divine nature, a rational human soul, and a physical human body.</note>. And since he himself is united to divinity, he unites us to God through faith and charity, so that he may bring man back to the principle from which he was made. 

And just as the divine nature is everywhere essentially—which is attributed to the divine essence of the Father—so also it is in the saints through grace, which is attributed to the Holy Spirit; and it is in the man Christ through union, which is attributed to the Son. For the divine nature is united to the human through the Son. Thus, the nature of Christ existing locally in heaven corresponds to that which is "to be everywhere"; and [existing] sacramentally on many altars, it corresponds to that which is "to be in the saints through grace"; and [existing] essentially in the divine nature, it corresponds in a third way through union. 

Yet it should not be said that Christ is of a "sixth composition" just as pure humans are of a "fifth composition" <note>In this hierarchical system, the "fifth composition" refers to the human being, the highest level of natural complexity. The author argues Christ’s divinity does not add a "sixth" layer of physical complexity because the union is spiritual and perfect, not a physical mixing.</note>. This is because such a union does not give him any new composition that would oppose simplicity, and because one nature is not made out of two. Therefore, there is no mixture there, nor confusion, nor composition, but union, as was said above. 

**Composition** is when one thing is made from many while the principal and secondary parts remain, such as a house being made from a wall, a roof, and a foundation. **Confusion** is when one thing is made from many while the secondary parts remain but not the principal ones, as when a bushel of wheat and barley becomes one. **Mixture**, however, is when one thing is made from many with neither the principal nor the secondary parts remaining, such as when a mixture is made from water and honey, and it is called **mead** <term>mead: a honey-water drink; the text uses the Latin "hydromel"</term>. **Union** is when many things, in their own nature, come together in something; thus all humans are united to make one human species, or many men are united in the pulling of a ship. 

And so it is with two natures in one person. Thus, regarding the composition of elements, some are of "proper composition," such as vapor which is dissolved by the power of the sun, and so on, as was said above. And note that this fivefold division of elements can be reduced to a two-part division as follows: 

For of elements, some are **inanimate** and some are **animate**. Furthermore, of the inanimate, some are caused immediately by the elements, like vapors, and some mediately, like mineral bodies, etc. Likewise, of the animate, some do not change their place by "progressive motion," such as the plants of trees and herbs, and some do change place. And of those [that move], some act by nature, like irrational animals, while some are moved by will, like rational beings. Therefore, we must first speak of the inanimate elements.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical and theological foundations of the universe into the specific physical sciences, specifically Meteorology. The previous page discussed Christ as the "end" of all creation; here, the text applies Aristotelian principles of "cold" and "heat" to explain the physical transformation of vapors into rain and hail.</meta>

# Meteorology

It is first required to know what the "converter" is, and how you are able to convert vapor into water. It must also be known what is "constricting," and how you are able to constrict vapor into ice. Therefore, to convert vapor is to bring it back to the nature of water by thickening it <term>thickening: <note>original: "inspissando." This refers to the process of condensation where a gas becomes a more dense liquid.</note></term>. Since it belongs to cold to thicken—just as it belongs to heat to disperse—it is evident that every conversion of vapor occurs through cold. Moreover, freezing occurs through that same cold being intensified in the corners, and it constricts the water which has been converted. 

Note also that a certain cloud is converted in its parts—that is, it has been made into water—and another is in the process of conversion—that is, it is becoming water. It must be said, then, that none of those things generated from moist vapor are inflammable. Also, because water is a thinner element than earth, the heat of the sun, which attracts such vapors, is not much retained by the vapors themselves; instead, it is driven out by the cold which constricts the vapor itself. Therefore, none of these vapors reaches through the region of cold to the region of heat <note>In the medieval cosmos, the "region of heat" or "sphere of fire" sat above the air; the author argues that the cold of the middle atmosphere forces vapor to turn back into water before it can reach the fire above.</note>, but instead, they are raised more and more into the region of extreme or temperate cold, according to whether they are warmer or less warm. 

If, therefore, there is a vapor that is very moist and very hot, that vapor will be very rare <term>rare: <note>original: "rarus." In this context, it means thin, porous, or less dense, allowing the cold to penetrate it easily.</note></term> as it ascends up to the region of cold. Since it is rare, the surrounding cold in that place will penetrate into its depths; because it is rare in all its parts, it gives entry to the cold everywhere. When the cold enters and the heat simultaneously exits, the cold begins to convert, compress, and thicken the converted parts of the vapor. And because the cold is strong, one part begins to flow quickly toward another part. Because the cold is greatly dominant, it constricts the converted water and generates from it hail—either large or small, depending on whether the cold is more or less powerful over the vapor itself. 

There are two signs of this process. One of them is that hail does not fall except in warm seasons, especially in the spring and in the time of fruits <gloss>that is, in autumn</gloss>, and more rarely than in the spring. This is because at those times, the burning heat begins, drying and igniting the vapor and raising it high into the midst of the cold. Because of this, the rare vapor is surrounded on all sides by cold; it gives entry to the cold and is very strongly constricted. This is just like heated water exposed under the open sky in winter, which freezes more than cold water placed in the same spot, because warm water is more "rare" and gives more entry to the cold <note>This is a reference to the "Mpemba effect," an observation attributed to Aristotle that warm water can sometimes freeze faster than cold water due to density and evaporation.</note>. 

The other sign is that hail is not perfectly round or spherical, but rather in the shape of a lens, surrounded or enclosed by frozen drops. This happens because when the vapor was expanded in width, the contracting cold pulled more of the matter from the sides than from the upper or lower part. And when the mass was large, the cold froze one part before another part could flow to it; therefore, as the other converted part flowed toward it, it adhered to it like a frozen drop. If, however, hail is sometimes found to be round, this is by accident <note>original: "per accidens." A logical term meaning it is not the essential nature of hail to be round, but a result of secondary circumstances like friction during its fall.</note>. For the same reason, hail is also always melting and not dry, because falling from the region of cold, it passes through the lower air, which from the reflection <note>The text likely ends with "reuerberatione radiorum solis," referring to the sun's rays reflecting off the earth to warm the lower atmosphere.</note>...

<summary>This page explains the meteorological process of condensation and hail formation using the Aristotelian theory of qualities (hot, cold, rare, dense). It argues that hail forms when intense heat pushes thin vapor into the cold upper atmosphere, where it is rapidly constricted.</summary>
<keywords>meteorology, condensation, hail, Aristotle, density, vapor, cold and heat, atmospheric regions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the physical explanation of atmospheric phenomena, moving from the general principles of condensation to specific descriptions of how snow and spring ice pellets (graupel) form. It maintains the Aristotelian framework of heat and cold acting upon vapors.</meta>

# Meteorology

...due to the reflection <note>original: "reberatiōe"</note> of rays from the earth and water, and through mixing, the air is temperately warm. Therefore, because of the motion and the warmth of this air, the falling moisture melts on the outside and is found falling in liquid form. **Snow**, however, behaves in the opposite way to **hail** regarding the time of year it appears. Yet in certain ways, they are similar in terms of their material. For snow is generated from warm vapor, though not in the same way as hail. 

A sign of this is the forecasting of peasants: who in winter, when the cold slackens, expect snow, unless the clouds are dark. Another truer sign is that the vapor of future snow is bright and pale, and this is seen by many. This is a sign of the vapor's thinness <term>thinness: <note>original: "raritas." In medieval physics, this refers to a substance being less dense or more "airy."</note></term>. Likewise, this vapor sometimes remains in the air for eight days, which is a sign of heat; yet it is not raised up except in the winter time. This is because the sun is then weak, and so with the help of the moon or another star (or several), it has enough power to raise the vapor, but not enough to intensify and heat it as happens in summer. 

It happens here, therefore, that the vapor that becomes snow does not reach all the way to the "region of cold" <note>the middle layer of the atmosphere where hail forms</note>, but remains nearby in a temperately cold region—one that is closer to the cold region than to the earth. Because the cold is not so intense upon it, and because the vapor is not so thin that it allows the cold to enter it from every side, it happens that the vapor of snow is transformed into water and frozen at the same time. It is not transformed into liquid first and frozen afterward. During this transformation, the vapor is diffused in a misty way, like *carded wool* <term>carded wool: <note>original: "lana carminata." Wool that has been combed and fluffed up.</note></term>. 

Therefore, snow falls in broad, thin flakes, as if they were pieces of scattered wool. Because the cold was not strong upon it, it freezes at the very moment the heat leaves it; the exiting heat slightly melts that which was weakly frozen. This is why snow falls soft and is easily compressed, which would not be the case if it were entirely ice like hail. The lower region cannot produce this in summer because the reflection of the sun’s rays makes it too warm for freezing; however, the vapor is still transformed into liquid, as happens with rains, as will be discussed later. 

From these two causes, we can determine the cause of the **small grains** <note>This refers to "graupel" or soft hail—snow pellets that are common in early spring.</note> which are accustomed to fall in March and April in our climates—namely in the sixth and seventh climes <note>Medieval geography divided the earth into seven "climes" or zones; the 6th and 7th were the northernmost inhabited regions like Northern Europe.</note> and further into the western parts, which are more distant from the path of the sun than others. We see, therefore, these round, thin grains, which frequently have the shape of a chickpea. These are generated from a cause mixed from both of those mentioned before: namely, from a vapor that is warmer and raised higher than that of snow, but less so than that of hail. It forms at the boundary of those two named regions. 

Therefore, these grains have the thinness of snow on the inside, but not the softness, because the cold overcomes them more. On the outside, however, they resemble hail. When that vapor is transformed, it is suddenly dilated by its own weight, and it draws in some of the warmer, subtle vapor from above, which enacts the process. A sign of this is that these grains are frequently generated in the transitional seasons, namely in spring. This happens more at the beginning of spring than after its midpoint, while the sun is in the constellations of **Pisces** and at the beginning of **Aries**. Likewise, water vapor is frequently not thin, subtle, and warm, but rather thick, cold, moist, and coarse...

<summary>This page explains the physical formation of snow and spring ice pellets (graupel), attributing their texture and timing to the specific balance of solar heat and atmospheric cold.</summary>
<keywords>snow formation, graupel, medieval meteorology, Aristotelian physics, climes, March and April, Pisces and Aries</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric moisture from the previous page, finishing the explanation of rain formation and transitioning into the secondary phenomena of dew and hoarfrost. It relies on the medieval understanding of "vapors" (moist) and "exhalations" (dry) reacting to varying levels of heat and cold.</meta>

# Meteorology

...is spread out. And therefore it does not ascend high to the region of great cold, but remains in the lower region, the cold of which is sufficient for conversion <note>the process of vapor turning into liquid water</note> but not for freezing <note>original: "congelationi"</note> except by accident. And thus such vapor returns as falling water. I say "by accident" because, due to accidental cold existing in the air, the drops of rain themselves—before they reach the earth—are frozen and fall as ice pellets <note>This refers to sleet or graupel rather than large summer hail</note>. Regarding rains, Isidore says in his book *Etymologies* <note>original: "lib. ethimologiaꝝ"</note> that rains [pluviae] are so called because they flow [fluunt] as if they were "flowings" [fluviae]. For they are born from the breath of the earth and sea; when these have been elevated higher, they are either dissolved by the heat of the sun or pressed down by the force of the winds and drip onto the lands. A **storm cloud** <note>original: "nimbus"</note> is the lowering of a cloud during a dark storm, and the word *nimbus* comes from *nubes* [cloud]. 

In summer, however, because the heat is then stronger, it impetuously raises a thick vapor that is not tightly bound together, which, divided into large parts, is raised only slightly. But the cold parts found in the air are divided by the heat which raises the vapor (or parts of the thick vapor) in separate portions. While a cloud is standing, there is some cold in one spot and some in another; and so that fleeing cold touches parts of the thick vapor standing in the air and suddenly converts them. Thus, large drops are formed, falling in summer without wind. They are seen to pass through the light of the sun like small candles, and they are very warm and quite moistening. 

That this is the way rain and drops are generated is proven by a sign: in rainwater, because the heat that turned it to vapor has not been entirely removed, there remains a power that constricts the belly <term>constricts the belly: <note>original: "vis constipativa ventris." Medieval medicine viewed rainwater as an astringent treatment for digestive issues.</note></term>. For rainwater constricts the bowels and is useful for dysentery. 

Consequently, we must speak of **dew** <note>original: "ros"</note>, which has something in common with rain, and of **hoarfrost** <note>original: "pruina"</note>, which has something in common with snow. The generation of dew comes from a vapor thicker than that of rain and less warm, because it has something of the earth mixed into it. And therefore it is not raised except a little, and it is not formed in the time of great heat <note>original: "caumatis"</note> nor in the time of great cold. Its conversion happens near the earth, sometimes at the height of one cubit <note>about 18 inches</note>, or a palm, or a tower, or such like. 

The conversion of this vapor happens in two ways: naturally and by accident. Naturally, it occurs through the cold of the air at night when there is no reflection of the sun's light in the air itself; and also because it contains little moisture, it is quickly consumed by the sun. The other way is its conversion from the revolution of much vapor back upon itself toward something reflecting it—just as in a covered pot, the reflected steam generates water, or as happens in the instrument by which **rose water** <note>a reference to an alembic or still used in medieval pharmacy</note> is made. Hence, when dew is created in the grass in the evening, at the evening twilight, dewing occurs even under a table due to the reflection of the vapor back toward the table. 

**Hoarfrost**, however, is made from vapor of the same nature, but the dense cold in the air near the earth freezes it as it converts it, just as was said regarding snow. Because cold is more concentrated in solid bodies, such as wood and stones, the cold emerging from them and touching the surrounding vapor as it converts, forces the vapor to stay fixed around them. That this is true is shown by a familiar enough sign: from those breathing in winter onto their clothes and hair, for the breath in winter becomes more moist than in summer...

# Meteorology

...on account of the generation of phlegm <note>one of the four bodily humors, associated here with cold and moisture</note>, the proper generation of which occurs in winter. For the thick vapor exhaled, being frozen during its conversion <note>the process of turning from gas to liquid or solid</note>, surrounds hair and clothing like **hoarfrost** <note>original: "pruina"</note>. The cause of the hardness and rigor of hoarfrost compared to snow is this: the dense cold around the earth, resulting from its own condensation within terrestrial bodies, universally expels the heat from the vapor. And therefore, although it freezes during that very conversion, it nevertheless becomes rigid because none of the heat that elevated the vapor remains to soften it by resolving it <note>melting or loosening it</note>. There also occurs a certain generation of **dew** <note>original: "roris"</note> that was discovered more by shepherds than by philosophers. 

It is a very thick vapor, much like the smoke of oil, or of tallow or fat, generated from a mixture of three elements: namely earth, water, and air. The heat of fire within it "digests" the mixture <note>medieval science viewed heat as a "cooking" or "digesting" force that transformed matter</note>, and a greasy and very sweet humidity is produced, much like **honey** <note>original: "mellis"</note>. When it falls upon plants, they first appear as if drenched with honey; but afterward, as the subtle, airy moisture evaporates, the thick, earthy part remains. Because it is well-mixed and "digested," it looks like mill-flour flying and sprinkled over the leaves of olive trees and herbs. From its dryness, it sometimes causes them to curve and contract, inducing sterility in them. 

Sometimes, as the heat evaporates from it toward the surface, it draws the moisture along with it. Because a "skin" or pellicle is generated on the outside by the cold of the air, the enclosed air is converted into a sensible animal spirit, and from this, **worms and small flies** <note>original: "uermes & musce parue"</note> are generated <note>This refers to the theory of spontaneous generation, where life was thought to arise from decaying matter and trapped moisture</note>. 

Shepherds frequently predict two accidents from these conditions: namely, the death of cattle or of sheep, but especially of sheep. Two causes can be assigned for this. One is that the animals eat so much of it because of its sweetness that the **gallbladder** <note>original: "Kistis felis"</note> is enlarged by an excess of **choler** <note>yellow bile, the hot and dry humor</note>. For honey is hot and dry and nourishes choler to such an extent that the gallbladder bursts; the bile is then diffused through the intestines and cuts through them, and the animals die from this. This kind of evidence has been seen in slaughtered cattle. 

The other cause is that by consuming the sweet substance too greedily, the **liver** <note>original: "epar"</note> becomes obstructed. Much nourishment is drawn in, but because the veins are overfilled, the liver cannot direct it further to the limbs. It therefore putrefies in the liver, and from this **leeches** <note>original: "sanguisuge"; here likely referring to liver flukes</note> are generated and fill the entire liver. This has often been seen in sheep. 

Let these things therefore be said concerning phenomena generated from moist vapor in the upper air. In both types previously discussed—namely, those generated from moist vapor and those generated from dry, inflammable vapor—there is one occurrence that should not be overlooked: sometimes they descend suddenly and tempestuously, and sometimes they fall gently. There is a twofold cause for this. One is the cold expelling the heat, which is its contrary. This happens in the falling **meteor** <note>original: "assub"; a term for a shooting star</note> and the **fiery dragon** <note>original: "dracone"; a long-tailed meteor or fireball</note> which moves to the side rather than directly downward. For the vapor is not equally light and heavy, curving itself because of its uncertain motion; it is ignited by friction, where the meeting cold drives it to one side and the similar heat attracts it to the other. The *assub* falls due to the impulse of its contrary. Because it inclines itself toward one region of the world, sailors say that a subtle wind blows or "drips" toward that part, and I believe this to be true. Another...

# Meteorology

...or the cause is the strongest expulsion from a double-expelling force. One is the cold of the place, as has been said. And the sign of this is that when fire is extinguished in water, a sudden and impetuous expulsion of one from the other occurs here. Another is the dry vapor expelling the moist, or vice-versa. And therefore a stormy rain is always preceded by a wind, involving and thickening the cloud. And let these things said suffice concerning things generated on high from dry inflamed vapor, and from moist vapor.

### On winds and their cause in general. Chapter 5.

We must now speak of the winds: and of the **material cause** <note>the substance from which something is made</note> of winds; and the **efficient cause** <note>the force that acts to create it</note>; and of the place of their generation. Wind, therefore, is naturally made from a dry, thick, terrestrial vapor. Its efficient cause, however, is the heat of the sun elevating this vapor and not consuming it. And the sign of this is that strong winds occur especially in autumn, with the sun being near the winter equinox <note>the author likely refers to the period leading into winter, where the sun’s power is waning</note>: because then the sun, by burning, has dried the elements; and therefore the vapor then elevated is more dry and earthy. For this reason, in autumn the earth gives its "complexion" <note>original: "complexio"; the specific temperament or mixture of qualities</note>, namely cold and dry. 

The place of its generation is near the middle region [of the air], which is of the greatest cold. For the earthy vapor ascends to that place. And since it is by nature heavy and hot, the cold of the place meets it and thickens and weighs it down, and makes it descend a little bit. However, the sun’s heat within it is not easily separable from it, because heat is more strengthened in dry terrestrial matter than in moist vapor. And therefore it raises it up again, and from this occurs a tangled impulse of the air, which is called the blowing of the wind. 

Thus, having determined the material and efficient causes, and the place in general, it is necessary to pass more to the specific kinds of winds so that it may be known: Why does wind not come from on high like rain, but always from the sides? The reason is that the place of the wind’s generation, according to where it begins to blow, is at the extremities <note>the horizons or outskirts</note> of the earth and not directly above it. The cause of this is because the reflection of the sun’s rays at the extremities of the earth is weak, while in the middle it is strong. And this is because of the angle of the ray, which at the extremities is **tangential** <note>original: "contingentie"; a very shallow angle of contact</note>. But the angle in the middle is right, or acute, or obtuse. Since, therefore, the efficient cause of the wind is a weak heat—yet one that calls it forth—it is strengthened by the matter of the wind at the extremities of our habitable earth, and it does not blow directly over it.

The cause, however, why the wind blows discontinuously—making one gust and then ceasing for an hour, or blowing weakly—is because in that vapor, the cold of the place (and not of the vapor itself) presses its upper part down very vehemently. And when the very broad vapor is thickened by the cold, it agitates the air greatly. It gives a gust until the conversion descends: from its upper extremity down upon the lower extremity of the earth. Then the heat rises again from the ray near the earth, with its warmth elevating it and supplying it with matter by elevating more vapor into it. And therefore, strengthened, it gives another strong gust, rising...

<summary>This page explains the medieval scientific theory of wind, identifying it as a dry, earthy vapor driven by the sun's heat and the air's cold. It explores why winds blow horizontally from the horizon rather than vertically like rain, attributing this to the angle of the sun's rays.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of atmospheric phenomena, specifically moving into the physical properties and movements of wind (ventos) as theorized in medieval Aristotelian science. It follows a discussion on the general causes of wind—heat and dry vapor—and now examines the specific mechanics of its motion and cessation.</meta>

# Meteorology

...ending. <note>original: "dere", likely the conclusion of "pendere" or a similar word from the previous page</note> For it must be known that **earthy vapor** <note>a dry, smoky substance believed to be the material of wind</note> never ascends equally in all its parts: because there are opposing powers within it, namely the very light and the very heavy. It is not equally subtle or capable of being thinned because of the hardness of the dry vapor; and therefore it rises like a stretched skin from the lower to the higher parts, with its width turned toward us.

Having seen these things, it is easy to see why one blast of wind is stronger than another, and which of them it is. For when the upper extremity, thickened by the cold of that place, descends and pushes the thick air more forcefully, the blast of the descent will be both rarer and stronger than that of the ascent. The **philosophers** <note>likely referring to Aristotle and his commentators</note> call this rising and falling of the vapor the **tortuous motion** <note>winding or twisting movement</note> of the terrestrial vapor; just as they also call the vapor itself "smoke" because of its earthy nature, while they say that watery vapor is more properly called by its own name, "vapor."

Thus it is also necessary to know that air is an element that easily obeys every impulse. Its motion occurs easily in every direction, contrary to its own nature. A sign of this is the shooting of arrows into water; with a light or violent impulse, air or water is pushed very strongly and floods the area being pushed until the force of the violence ceases. If, therefore, such a small thing as a person pushing can give such a strong motion to it, it is no wonder if a very wide cloud of smoke produces a gust of this kind. 

This is also the cause of the **sound of the wind**, which is like waves clashing together, because one flood of air precedes another and pours itself over it. From these points, a **threefold cause** for the "cutting off" <note>the ceasing or stopping</note> of winds can be clearly seen: namely, intense cold, intense heat—and these cut them off simply—and following rain, which cuts them off in a certain way. 

*   **Intense cold** does not allow the pores of the earth to be opened so that the smoke can be raised. 
*   **Intense heat**, however, burns up the smoke. 
*   **But rain**, because it cools the air, makes the vapor heavy, so that it descends so much that it cannot suddenly be raised by a weak heat. It also over-moistens the earth and blocks the pores of the surface through which the smoke used to exit. 

Thus, the material that increases the wind is cut off. And because whatever is not continuously generated quickly fails, the wind ceases after great rains. But because when the rain ceases, heat rises again in the air and in the earth—drying and elevating the vapor and smoke—the wind usually rises again after an hour or a few days, unless the rain is very long, which by great coldness gains victory over the smoke of the wind. 

From these sayings, it is also clear why the vapor of the winds is not converted into something that falls upon us as **earthy rain**, just as watery vapor is converted and falls as rain. According to what has been said, there are two causes. One is the more principal: the material of the wind, which is dry and not easily made continuous, but only has something of the nature of the moist which is continuous like smoke; and therefore, when dissolved by cold, it does not appear to the senses but is dispersed like **atoms** <note>original: "athomor̃"; referring to tiny, indivisible particles or specks of dust</note>. 

The other cause is that the vapor of the wind does not float above us but is directed to the extremes of our habitable land, or to the **convex arc** <note>the curvature</note> of one land, and does not fall upon us. I say the "convex arc of one land" because the **earth is round** <note>original: "terra rotūda ē"; a reminder that the sphericity of the Earth was standard knowledge in medieval academic texts</note>, and sometimes a wind is in one land and not in another. And then the vapor, although it is not simply at the edge, is nonetheless in the convex arc with respect to the land into which it blows.

<summary>This page explains the physical mechanics of wind, describing it as a "smoky" earthy vapor that moves in a tortuous path between the earth and the cold upper atmosphere. It accounts for the sound of wind and identifies why extreme heat, cold, or rain causes wind to cease, while also affirming the Earth's spherical shape to explain wind patterns.</summary>

# Meteorology

### On the winds in particular. Chapter 6.

Next, we discuss the winds in particular: their place of origin, the **influence** <note>original: "impressione," referring to the physical or astrological effect of each wind</note> of each individual wind, and the effects they produce upon our habitable earth.

### On the four principal simple winds and the others between them.

->Circius and Aquilo are said to hem in the North wind.<-
->Africus and Nothus are collateral to the South wind.<-
->And Eurus, Corus, and Favonius follow from there.<-

</image-desc>

There are, therefore, four **simple winds** <note>fundamental or primary winds</note> which are born or generated toward the four corners of the sun, distinguished above our horizon. According to **Aristotle** <note>the Greek philosopher whose "Meteorologica" was the standard textbook for this subject</note>, these are the **North** <note>original: Septentrio</note>, the **South** <note>original: Meridies</note>, the **East Wind** <note>original: Subsolanus, literally "under the sun"</note>, and the **West Wind** <note>original: Favonius</note>. Between any two of these, however, there are two others. And thus there are eight intermediate winds. 

Between the North and the East winds, **Boreas** <note>a cold, dry northern wind</note> is closer to the North, and **Vulturnus** is closer to the East. Likewise, between the East and the South: **Eurus** is closer to the East, and **Nothus** <note>the Greek name for the South wind</note> is closer to the South or **Auster**. Likewise, between the South and the West: **Africus** is closer to the South, and **Zephyrus** is closer to the West. Likewise, between the West and the North: **Circius** is nearer to the West, and **Corus** is closer to the North. 

It must be noted that the names of the **collateral winds** <note>the side or intermediate winds</note> are not named by the **Agent** <note>likely referring to the "First Mover" or the primary celestial influence</note> but according to common Northern usage. Also, two besides...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of wind systems, moving from the geometric classification of the twelve winds to their specific physical properties (temperature, moisture, and strength) and their relationship to the daily cycle of the sun.</meta>

# Meteorology

**On Meteorology**

Some identify these as uncertain and wandering gusts. If they exist, they do not derive from the main winds, but are instead generated in the same places. This occurs because the rising of vapor in a circular motion is nearly continuous, appearing now here, now there. These matters that have been discussed concerning the twelve winds are clear in the accompanying figure.

It remains to determine why one of these winds is characterized by great blasts, pure rain, and a cold breeze. Another, however, has light blasts and much rain, with a corrupting air. Two others are characterized by many frequent, but not strong, blasts. One of these cuts off the rains—namely, the East wind <note>original: "subsolanus"</note>. The other brings the rains—namely, the West wind <note>original: "occidentalis"</note>. I say, therefore, that the North wind <note>original: "aquilo"</note> has the strongest blasts; it cuts off the rains and purifies the air. All these effects are caused by the coldness of its vapor. Furthermore, when the vapor emerges, it is very dry, and the wind begins to blow higher in the air. For this reason, there is a common proverb that "high winds blow in autumn." The East wind <note>original: "subsolanus"</note> is a dry, hot wind that cuts off rain, but not as much as the North wind; this is because it arises from the vapor following the sun, which is dry. The movement of the firmament <note>The "firmament" refers to the celestial sphere; the author suggests the wind's motion is reinforced by the daily rotation of the heavens.</note> assists in this, because the movement of that wind is the same as the daily motion. Therefore, the vapor is dissolved and dried out.

The West wind <note>original: "fauonius"</note>, however, brings rains for the opposite reason. The intermediate winds should be understood according to their mixed nature. It should also be noted that there is often a wind in the morning, usually before daybreak. Sometimes it blows by day and ceases at night; sometimes the reverse occurs. The cause of this is that in the morning, the sun's rays are cast upon vapor that was compressed in the East by the coldness of the night. Therefore, the rays lift the vapor, and then the vapor itself descends again due to the coldness of the location, which creates a wind. Sometimes the wind ceases when the sun is shining, and sometimes it is strengthened—namely, when the sun is not enough to overcome the vapor, but is sufficient to lift it and continually resist its sinking. Again, cold vapor is pushed down at night, lifted by day, and creates wind. Conversely, a stronger vapor is lifted by day and is slightly depressed by the night's cold; then, being lifted again by its own heat, it creates wind at night. Let these points be determined concerning the phenomena generated from simple vapor—whether moist or dry—in high places.

### On the causes of the effects of composite vapor. Chapter 7.

Next, we must discuss the causes of the effects <note>original: "impressionum," referring to atmospheric phenomena or "impressions" made on the air</note> produced by composite vapor: dry and moist together; or dry with dry; or moist combined with the light of the sun and stars; or dry with the light of the stars. In this way, we cover everything generated in the heights from vapor. The first composition is moist vapor with dry, where the vapors are not mixed but remain distinct; this composition causes thunder and lightning. Second, we will assign the cause of the earthquake, which is caused by dry vapor that is not mixed but is trapped within a solid and dry element, which is the earth. Third, we will give the cause of the whirlwind <note>original: "vento turbinis"</note>, which...

# Meteorology

**On Meteorology**

...is caused by a dry wind encountering an opposing wind. Fourth, we shall speak of the cause of the rainbow <note>original: "yridis"</note> and the colors of the clouds and mists; and concerning comets and the Milky Way <note>original: "galasia"</note>. We must first state what the material of thunder is. There is one material, and another place or vessel where it is generated. Its material is a very dry and hot vapor. For such vapor is easily and entirely ignited. 

A comparison for this, though it may seem crude, is nevertheless appropriate: consider the gases of the belly <note>The author uses a digestive analogy to explain how trapped gas under pressure can become volatile.</note>. Because these are dry and earthy, if they are even slightly touched by fire, they immediately flare up and crackle. If they are released through a fine cloth and a candle is held near, they all ignite with a broad, thick flame. 

The "vessel" or place of generation is a watery and concave cloud. The strong heat of the sun lifts both types of vapor simultaneously <note>Medieval science distinguished between "moist" vapors from water and "dry" exhalations from the earth.</note>. Since the terrestrial vapor is not as expandable as the watery vapor, it is constricted in the middle of it, as if in a womb; and the other vapor expands around it like a tent. Because the exterior is moist and the interior is dry, the interior flees its opposite <note>Following the principle that "contraries" like heat/dryness and cold/moisture repel each other.</note> into the depths of the outer cloud. The exterior cloud also contracts due to the cold of its location and further squeezes the interior. This agitated interior rubs part against part and against the sides of the outer cloud. From this motion and friction <note><term>confricatione</term>: the rubbing together of elements, which Aristotelian physics argued was a primary cause of heat and ignition</note>, it happens to ignite, and the fire is expelled by its opposite. By the force of the fire, it splits the watery cloud, and in that split, there occurs lightning <note>original: "corruscatio"</note> and sound. These happen at the same time, but they are not perceived at the same time because sight is faster than hearing. 

An example is a woman washing clothes <note>This refers to the visual delay observed when watching someone beat laundry against a stone from a distance; you see the strike before you hear the slap.</note>. This is the opinion of Aristotle. It should be noted, however, that sound and thunder occur in three ways, and there are three ways for the emergence of one vapor from another, as can be proven in its proper place. Therefore, six things are to be noted here. In the first way, the emergence is sometimes violent through the expulsion of one by another, as Aristotle says. 

Sometimes, however, it is not only thus, but the vapor, cutting through gradually and flying out of the cloud in which it is held, finds another cloud that is constricted, dry, and thick, and strikes against it. From this collision, it makes a sound. Sometimes this happens with lightning—specifically when it is ignited by this impact—and sometimes without lightning, when they only collide. 

In the third way, a small, rare, ignited vapor emerges from a thin cloud that is not very watery. Then that fire is seen like a white flame through much smoke, and in that case, it does not make a sound in its own cloud. But it encounters another cloud that is very watery and thick. Because the flame is weak, it is extinguished by that watery cloud, hissing and smoking rather than sounding, just as glowing iron hisses and smokes in water. Vapor also emerges from its own cloud in three ways: namely, before ignition, because sometimes it begins to be compressed into the depths of its own peak, and from the commotion—because it is dry—it penetrates the cloud and emerges into the air, moving it strongly or weakly according to the diversity of its power. And these...

### On Meteorology ###

### Header: On Meteorology ###

...is the reason why hot and strong winds occur before thunder. However, in the ignition itself, sometimes both are rare and weak; and then, because it finds no resistance, it exits without sound or wind that might reach our hearing, just as happens in summer during small evening lightnings. After it has been ignited, it is sometimes small, and then by striking the sides of the cloud, it makes a sound; but before it passes through, it is extinguished, and then a sound is heard—namely, a whistling sound—just as when a fierce wind blows through holes, whistling without lightning. These variations have been considered by few, but all are found through experience if one observes the air at night in summer. 

We must now speak of the diversity of the vapor according to the heat or the color of the fire within the cloud itself. But first, concerning the diversity of the cloud containing the thunder, and finally about the diversity of its descent to us. A cloud that contains strong thunder is frequently of a fourfold disposition: namely, black, red, green, and somewhat white. 

A **black** cloud is indeed thick due to its own cold; and therefore, it does not give way to the sun's rays so that they might penetrate and dilate it and make it white. Therefore, being thick, it strongly compresses the vapor and ignites it, and gives a strong thunder, if the internal vapor is plentiful and thick. A cloud **turning red** comes from black, and is of itself very thick; but the ignited vapor now shines through <note>original: "resultat per ipsam"</note> it, and therefore it frequently gives a stronger strike than the black one. 

A **green** one <note>original: "viridis"</note> with blackness and a little redness is the worst of all clouds, and is to be feared if it is directly over any city; because it has much fiery vapor due to the redness, much material of vapor due to the blackness, and much water due to the greenness; and thus a very strong battle occurs there of hot with cold and moist with dry. To such an extent that it frequently emits thunderbolts <note>original: "fulmina"</note> of great stones <note>The author refers to the medieval belief in "thunderstones" or "cerauniae," believed to be physical projectiles falling from the sky during storms, or perhaps very large hail.</note> which cast down towers and buildings, and even burn men. 

But one need not fear a **white** one, because it has little of either material; and so, if its flame falls upon buildings, it is immediately extinguished before it sets them on fire, unless they are very dry or flammable. 

For the colors of lightning <note>original: "corruscationis"</note> are very much to be observed, as they are manifold: namely, Red, Clear, White, Flame-like, and Reddish-wine. A color is called **ruddy** <note>original: "rubicundus"</note> like red wine in a glass illuminated by the sun. This first kind is caused by a vapor that is not very dry. For moisture gives it a red color, just like the flame of green wood. And this color is to be feared, because it contains moist material which is not scattered; and therefore, it ignites more easily and strongly. 

But the **flame-like** color of a white hue comes from very dry vapor, like the flame of dry wood, and therefore it is quickly scattered and does not harm unless it meets easily flammable things like flax or such things. Moreover, the **reddish wine-color** <note>original: "ruffus... uinosus"</note> is noteworthy because that color comes from vapor that is very much stuck together <note>original: "conglutinati"</note> and earthy; and it frequently splits and burns at the same time. Its fall from the cloud upon lower things is whenever...

### On Meteorology ###

...plex or nearly so. The first of these modes is when a red ignited vapor falls from a deep green cloud; and this generates the stone called a <term>thunderbolt<note>Latin: *telum tonitrui*, literally "thunder-weapon." In medieval science, it was believed that lightning strikes sometimes produced a physical stone or "celt."</note></term>. In this way, the vaporous mixture of earthly vapor is ignited. And when it touches a green cloud that is already in the process of turning into water, it is bound together like flour into dough. But a very strong ignition meets it such that the moisture of the cloud is not extinguished. And therefore the vapor, bound very strongly by the moisture, is "cooked" by the heat. And because heat hastens to flee from moisture, the stone is sharpened at its upper part, where the vapor first begins to drip, and it is thick in its other part. And this stone splits and ignites whatever it finds. 

The second mode of the same vapor occurs when it is less bound together and is in a less watery cloud. And then it is not bound together, but being ignited it exits with force; and again it splits and ignites, but it splits only wood and not stones, except rarely. It splits by impact and ignites by flaming. 

The third mode is of a bright red vapor which never makes a stone, yet its flame is not separated from the vapor; and this never splits anything except very compact bodies that are not of great size. For if they were of great size, they would resist it. And this kills men, and a smell of burning <note>original: *odor combustionis*</note> appears in them, yet no wound is seen. Because it is subtle, it enters within without making a wound. And if it finds gold in a purse, it crushes the gold and does not harm the purse, because the purse, being a porous thing, allows entry easily, whereas the compact gold resists. And thus the gold is sometimes broken into pieces, and sometimes into powder. And in this way it also happens that it pierces a sword and does not harm the scabbard. 

There is another mode of the same vapor, more rare and exiting from a less watery cloud. And that enters and does not harm by dividing nor by openly igniting, but it is a red flash <note>original: *corruscatio*</note> that is very harmful. And it harms vineyards, as the <term>vinedressers<note>Latin: *vinitores*</note></term> say, and especially those <term>grape clusters<note>Latin: *botris*</note></term> that are covered by many leaves. And yet through some opening, the flash casts its vapor there with its ray, because those damaged grapes cannot evaporate. And this flash is not only harmful to bodies when it falls directly, but its ray is also harmful to whatever it touches and corrupts, because it is of the nature of the vapor. Hence, for this reason, some who look at it are swollen in the face, and some are blinded in their eyes. 

The fourth mode is of a white vapor which exits in a dry flame, not gathered together; and therefore it is scattered in the air. But yet it exits with great force because of the deep cloud in which it is, which is very watery; and this one splits things, and in the splitting it is extinguished. Thus, the things it splits appear white, and no sign of burning appears in them. And sometimes it kills, and sometimes it does not. And this happens when its impulse is quite strong. And sometimes, because of its scattering, it strikes one person in one place and does not harm his companion standing next to him, and strikes him who stands far off. Sometimes...

# Meteorology #

...it even proceeds from some wall by reflection to another and strikes it. And sometimes it is weaker. And sometimes it consumes a garment or a shoe without injury to the feet and is extinguished; and the violence ceases and does not harm the person. The fifth mode of egress of the same vapor is from a weakened cloud; and that one does no harm at all because as soon as it touches the air, it is extinguished and blunted. Thus, both its burning and the violence of its impulse cease at once. 

But it should be added here what the proper time for thunder is. It is one time in cold regions—namely, in the fifth and sixth <term>climates<note>In medieval geography, "climates" were seven latitudinal zones of the Earth. The fifth and sixth climates refer to Northern Europe and colder regions.</note></term> and beyond—and another in hot ones—namely, in the fourth, third, second, and first climates. In cold regions, it rarely thunders loudly except when the sun is in the summer tropic, which is at the beginning of Cancer, or approaching near it or receding; because then, being strong, the sun raises the vapor of water and earth, from which, joined together, thunder is caused. 

In other regions, namely hot ones, thunder is caused during the equinoxes—that is, when the sun is at the head of Aries and Libra <note>The spring and autumn equinoxes.</note>. For when the sun is in Cancer <note>the summer solstice</note>, it burns up the vapor rather than raising it. Nor is this contrary to what we said above—namely, that the sun in summer cuts off vapors by burning them away—for this is true of weak vapors. But when the heat is strong, it cannot consume thick vapor but instead raises it, just as a large fire raises large burning logs and makes them fly through the air. 

From this, the country-folk have observed something that seems to be of no small consideration: namely, that when they hear thunder in the winter signs, such as in Capricorn or Aquarius, then at the beginning of spring—and perhaps throughout the whole year—it will be windier than in another year. And the cause of this is that it signifies there is much material of the winds in the air, which is not easily cut away even through the whole year. 

Concerning thunder, <note>Saint Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an influential scholar whose *Etymologies* was the standard encyclopedia of the Middle Ages.</note> Isidore says in his book of *Etymologies* that thunder <note>Latin: *tonitruum*</note> is so named because its sound terrifies <note>Latin: *terrat*</note>. For "total sound" is what it is called; and for this reason, it sometimes shakes all things so severely that the sky seems to have split apart. For when a storm-blast of a most violent wind suddenly thrusts itself into the clouds with a growing whirlwind, and seeking an exit, it tears through the cloud it entered with great force, and carries its dry sound to the ears with a horrid crash. No one should wonder at this, since even a small bladder, however tiny it may be, emits a great sound when burst. Along with thunder, lightning is also expressed at the same time. But the latter is seen more quickly because it is bright, whereas the former reaches the ears more slowly. Let these things said for the present concerning thunder suffice.

The cause of this is because these impressions are made from thick, dense, and moist vapors, from which black, dense, or dry clouds are made, because they are from ignited earthly vapor. **Vocabulary: thunder, lightning, Jupiter, Mars, vapor, pestilence, meteorology**

...ses raised from the surface of the earth where the earth is thin and the water is thin, which descend to the earth through rains. But that <term>vapor<note>In medieval science, "vapor" was a technical term for moist exhalations from the earth, while "smoke" referred to dry ones. Together they formed all weather phenomena.</note></term> is emitted from the depths of the earth where the water is impure, earthy, thick, and dense; it is a sticky substance from the regions near the earth’s core. Properly speaking, it is from these materials that the vapor of these <term>impressions<note>A term used by historical scientists to describe any atmospheric phenomenon, such as rainbows, meteors, or storms.</note></term> is made. For this reason, it cannot occur unless the heat is very great, and therefore it cannot happen except during the summer time. Furthermore, thunder does not occur except from a scorched vapor, which begins to ignite during its own inflammation or burning, as we have said before. Since burning heat only exists in the summer time, the vapor for thunder and lightning only forms when the strongest cold drives against a watery cloud and compresses that vapor. Such an effect is not found in the air unless it is expelled from all sides by the surrounding air; however, this impression is not strong except in the time of great heat <note>original: "caumatis," referring to the sweltering heat of midsummer</note>, as is clear from what has already been said. 

The actual generation of thunder and lightning does not occur by itself in the summer. Rather, it is known that their generation occurs after a period of clear weather. Although the sky may become clouded over during the event itself, this is because in cloudy weather, the heat is dispersed throughout the air and cannot perform a strong operation in a single place. But because these phenomena only exist within clouds, it is necessary for the sky to be clouded over when they descend. Even though at times there seem to be no clouds, lightning still flashes—just as in the evening during summer, lightning is seen in a clear sky, especially at twilight. Nevertheless, clouds are indeed present there; it is from them that the lightning flashes, and only the change in brightness reaches us. 

From what has been said, the two-fold location of these impressions is easily recognized: namely, the place where they are generated according to their forms, and the place from which their material is extracted. It is necessary that they be generated in a place higher than other moist impressions, because that upper region is where the cold is repelled by the heat from below. A sign of this is the great distance between our hearing the sound and seeing the lightning flash, especially when the thunder is loud, because that distance is greater then than when the thunder is small. The place from which the material is extracted is moist and thick, containing a watery fluid and an earthy stickiness that is not easily separated, because it is further compressed and thickened within the cloud by the burning fire.

As for why **Jupiter** (which is one of the seven planets) possesses the power and property of thundering: there is no other cause than that it has the power and nature to raise the strong material of dry winds. This is especially true when it is joined in some influence with **Mars** during the summer time and in the northern <term>signs<note>The zodiac signs associated with the northern hemisphere's spring and summer, such as Aries, Taurus, and Gemini.</note></term>. At such times, it can be predicted with great certainty that many "drippings" will occur in the air. This can even lead to future danger for mankind due to **pestilence**, resulting from the great corruption that follows when poisonous, burning vapors are mixed into the air. Because Jupiter...
