...rising with the Sun, and in its movement it corrupts the air: and thus the air becomes **heavy and tainted** <note>original: "uino fus." likely intended as "vinosus" (wine-like/heavy) or a typo for "vitiosus" (corrupted/faulty)</note>. Let these things said concerning time and place suffice.

### Chapter 10: On Earthquakes.

An earthquake has as its <term>material cause<note>In medieval science, the "material cause" refers to the physical substance that makes up an object or phenomenon.</note></term> a dry, thick, and very earthy <term>vapor<note>Ancient and medieval scientists believed earthquakes were caused by "exhalations" or winds trapped in underground caverns.</note></term>. Its <term>efficient cause<note>The "efficient cause" is the force or agent that triggers an event; here, the sun acts as the catalyst.</note></term> is the heat of the sun penetrating deep into the belly of the earth. It takes place where the earth is <term>clogged<note>original: "opilatam." This term refers to the blocking of the earth's "pores" or natural vents.</note></term> at the surface. A place is clogged for three reasons: first, because it is not sandy but is near the sea, such that the ebb and flow of the tide are at its very boundaries, and thus the place is clogged by the moisture of the sea and the vapor is shut in, unable to exhale because the moisture fills the pores; or the pores are clogged by continuous rains filling them; or because the earth is solid and rocky with unbroken stone. In these places, the vapor is dried out or stirred up by the heat of the sun from below, and it cannot exit because of the clogged pores at the surface. Therefore, the vapors multiply there in the belly of the earth.

This material, being an earthy vapor, is distinguished by whether there is much or little of it, whether it is thick or <term>subtle<note>meaning "thin" or "refined"</note></term>, and whether it is burning hot or temperately cold. From these two factors—place and material—the time of its breakout and its effects are determined. The time of breakout, as is frequently the case, is the time of the <term>equinox<note>The times in Spring and Autumn when day and night are of equal length.</note></term>, because then the material of the winds is multiplied both inside and outside the earth; once this material has multiplied, the earth is split, stretched, and the vapor breaks out.

Nevertheless, a great earthquake was seen lasting for a long time in **Lombardy**, which occurred around the time of the **winter solstice** <note>original: "tropici hyemalis," literally the winter tropic</note>. This happened because the material was abundant and deep in the earth, and the pores were heavily clogged in the earth at that time. Thus, it could not evaporate little by little, and so it moved often. That vapor was very hot and spread throughout the width of that province. Because it was very hot, it usually grew quiet during the day, because it was then thinned by the sun so that it could not move the earth. By night, however, it was thickened by the cold of the night, so that it then had the strength to move. For this reason, it came especially in the middle of the night and lasted in this way for almost forty days.

The time during the day is more often in the morning and at noon than at other times, and more in the night than in the day, unless it occurs otherwise by accident. For when the vapor is hot, as it frequently is, it becomes too thinned out during the day, but once thickened, it moves the earth at night. If, however, it is cold and scarce, then it stays still at night because it is scarce. At the first sight of the sun in the morning, it is thinned and moves. But if it is cold, abundant, and thick, then it is not thinned enough to move until the sun has strengthened over it at noon. Its breakout, when it is scarce and subtle and the pores of the earth are loose, is without sound and induces only a small earthquake or tremor. If, however, it is subtle and scarce but the pores of the earth are constricted, then in...

or a hill; and if it is thrown to another place by the force, then it settles back down again
once the vapor has escaped, either entirely or in part. But if there is a great deal of cold vapor in a

sometimes, by obstructing rivers in one part, they force them to appear or open up
in another. However, if it is a vapor that is opposed by another, this is very dangerous,
because then it creates deep whirlpools; and sometimes one moves the water
coming from one direction, and the other moves the water coming from the other direction; and from their
collision, they bring about a local flood, especially when they block the mouths
of rivers from their accustomed flow. An example of an earthquake is
given in the case of a chestnut and an egg: which, if they are placed in a fire without their shells being cracked,
the vapor that is stirred up inside by the external heat begins to become thin and, wanting
to exit, since it finds resistance, it breaks the shell with great force.
So much so that it frequently hurls the egg or the chestnut out of the fire. The signs preceding
earthquakes are cold from the cold vapor, and a sound from the subterranean
movement. The following signs are mist and darkness around the sun or stars, because of the
arrangement of the clouds. And let these things said concerning earthquakes suffice.

### On things generated from vapor: and first concerning the rainbow. Chapter 12.

It remains to speak of those things generated from <term>vapor<note>The text uses "vapor" to cover both moist exhalations (leading to rain) and dry exhalations (leading to wind/fire).</note></term> which is joined with the light of the Sun, the Moon, and the stars, such as the rainbow, the halo of the sun and moon, comets, the colors of clouds and mists, and the <term>galaxy<note>original: "galasya." Refers to the Milky Way, which medieval scholars often categorized as an atmospheric phenomenon rather than a stellar one.</note></term>. It should be known, therefore, that according to Aristotle, the rainbow is caused by a ray of the sun returning <note>i.e., reflecting</note> to a vapor opposite the cloud—that is, standing before the cloud—just as when a ray shining into water reflects back onto a wall. And this happens when it begins to descend in a drizzle during a time of rain. However, the cloud must be smooth and still. For in stillness, the parts flow together since they are similar; and thus the outer surface becomes flat to receive the impression of the rays. And thus the ray reflects back to the vapor, just as a luminous mirror reflects back to a mirror similar to itself placed opposite its face. Aristotle provides a sign of this kind in his book on <term>Metaphysics<note>The text cites "Metaphysics," but this anecdote is found in Aristotle’s *Meteorology* (III.4). Medieval manuscripts occasionally misattributed quotes between major works of the same author.</note></term>, saying that a certain man’s vision was weakened; and while he was walking, he saw a light before his face. Because much thick and turbid moisture was flowing into the pupil of his eyes, his image was confusedly impressed—especially that of his eyes—into the moisture as if into a mirror. Thus, he thought another person similar to himself was walking opposite his face. And although the sun, whose image the rainbow is, is round: because it is nevertheless much higher than the clouds, touching the upper part of the cloud, it impresses upon it a bow-like shape. Therefore, the closer the sun is to rising or setting, the larger the arc appears on the opposite side.

Moreover, the colors of the rainbow are caused by the fact that the cloud irradiated by the sun has some parts that are higher and others lower. Likewise, it has some parts thicker so that they transmit the sun, and others thinner so that they exclude the sun. This inequality, making a variation, mixes light and shadow and represents that wonderful appearance of the bow. According to Aristotle, however, the colors of the bow are three or four: namely, wine-colored or red on the outside upon the curvature of the bow; after which follows green, which is both interior and exterior. But the middle color between these is <term>blue<note>original: "ceruleus."</note></term>; wherefore in the upper part, varied between white and red, there is a kind of pale color. But in the lower part, varied between white and green, there is a kind of <term>citrine<note>original: "citrinus." A yellow-green color resembling the skin of a citrus fruit.</note></term>. And painters cannot make a color similar to the rainbow, and especially the composite color in the middle between the inner and outer. Some, however, say the rainbow has four principal colors from the four elements, from which they also say the cloud is composed: red from fire.

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 12 regarding the formation and colors of the rainbow, drawing on Aristotelian and Isidorian theories, before transitioning to Chapter 13, which describes the phenomenon of halos (circles) around the sun and moon.</meta>

# Meteorology

[The rainbow takes] purple from the air, gray-blue from the water, and green from the earth because of the green plants. This bow signifies different things at different times. For if it rises at midday, it signifies that there is a great force of water in the air which can dominate at noon. If, however, it appears around sunset, it will thunder and rain lightly. But if it rises at sunrise or thereabouts, it promises clear weather. Regarding the bow, <term>Aristotle<note>original: "Ari." referring to Aristotle's *Meteorologica*.</note></term> says that after the autumnal <term>equinox<note>The time of year (around Sept 22) when day and night are of equal length.</note></term>, a rainbow can form at any hour of the day. In summer, however, it occurs only at the beginning of the day or when the day is already declining. He gives this reason for the matter: in summer, the sun around midday is at its hottest and burns up the clouds, so that it cannot imprint its image upon them. But in the morning time, or turning toward sunset, because it is less hot, it can be sustained by the clouds and can imprint its image upon them. Likewise, the sun does not usually create a bow unless it is opposite those clouds in which it creates it. When the days are shorter and the sun is obliquely opposite above the clouds, it can therefore imprint its image on the clouds at any part of the day, even when the sun is at its highest. In summer times, however, it is carried above our <term>vertex<note>The highest point in the sky directly above the observer; the zenith.</note></term>, and then at midday it looks directly at the clouds and does not imprint its image upon them. Regarding the <term>Iris<note>The Greek name for the rainbow and the goddess who personified it.</note></term>, <term>Isidore<note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), whose *Etymologiae* was a standard reference for medieval scholars.</note></term> says in his book of *Etymologies* that the "heavenly bow" is so named for its likeness to a curved bow; "Iris" is not its proper name. And he says "Iris" is as if it were "of the air" <note>original: "quasi aeris." A medieval etymological pun linking the Greek name *Iris* to the Latin word for air, *aer*.</note>, because it ascends from the earth to the air, or because it descends through the air to the earth. This shines back from the sun while hollow clouds receive the sun's ray from the opposite side and form the appearance of a bow. Various colors are given to it by this fact: because thin water, bright air, and dark clouds, when irradiated, cause these various colors.

## Concerning the circle that appears around the sun and moon. Chapter 13.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial C, colored in red and outlined in black, begins the word Circulus.</image-desc>

The circle which is sometimes seen around the sun, or a **crown**, is caused by moist vapor ascending and multiplying in the air, upon which when the sun shines, its splendor reflects in the air beyond that vapor or within it. Then that light is reflected back from the air, returning upon the ascending vapor itself, and thus a circle or crown is seen around the sun. And although this circle may seem to touch the sun, this image is not made far from the earth. But our sight, deceived by its own weakness, thinks it is around the sun. And a similar thing happens with the moon and other stars that have light. These circles appear more frequently at night around the moon and stars, but more rarely around the sun during the day, because of the strength of the sun's heat dissolving the vapor. If these circles, having slipped away evenly, vanish into themselves, they signify tranquility in the air. If, however, they cease as if broken in one part, they signify that wind will blow openly from that direction. If they are broken in many places, sailors expect a storm at sea. It also seems sometimes that the light of the sun is of a slight or more intense redness, which...

## On the Comet. Chapter 14.

<term>Comets<note>From the Greek 'kometes', meaning 'long-haired'. In the Middle Ages, comets were often considered atmospheric phenomena occurring in the upper air rather than distant celestial bodies.</note></term> are stars that have "hair." This occurs because of the inflamed air contained by the stars, or rather by the five planets: namely, Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Mercury, and Venus. For these planets, on account of their swift motion, ignite the air which is in their circuit; and thus, because of the inflamed air surrounding these aforementioned stars, combined with their light, those stars are seen as comets. I say, however, that a comet is nothing other than a <term>thick, earthy vapor<note>original: "uapor grossus terreus". Aristotelian science taught that comets were made of dry, smoky exhalations from the Earth.</note></term> whose parts lie close together. As it gradually ascends from the lower part of the <term>heat<note>original: "estus"; referring to the hot, upper region of the atmosphere near the sphere of fire.</note></term> to its upper part, it touches the concavity of the fire, where it becomes diffused and inflamed. For this reason, it frequently appears long and spread out. 

I say that it is an "earthy vapor" so that it may have the material for vapor; and I say "thick" because if it were thin, it would necessarily evaporate and be dissipated. And I say "whose parts lie close together" because it is a well-mixed, viscous substance. This rises to the region called the "heat." Because its parts have a great deal of uniformity, it is first spread out by the heat of the fire and afterwards ignited. In the middle, it always remains thick where it is nourished by its <term>storehouse<note>original: "thesauro"; refers to the reservoir of vaporous material feeding the flame from below.</note></term> situated beneath it. Therefore, the flame is very high and thick. However, that part which is far from the bottom and spread to the sides is thin; it has a thin flame like a white cloud, which is seen as its "hair." It lasts for the entire time that its "storehouse" evaporates toward it. 

I myself, along with many others, in the Year of the Lord's Resurrection <term>1240<note>original: "M.cc.quadragesimo".</note></term>, saw a <term>beam<note>original: "trabem"; a classification of a comet or meteor that appears long and rectangular like a wooden beam.</note></term> in Saxony almost next to the North Pole. It cast rays toward the south and west, directing its point more toward the east; and it is certain that no planets are located there.

## How the Colors of Clouds are Caused. Chapter 15.

The colors of clouds or mists are caused by the joining of light with them, according to whether they are more or less <term>transparent<note>original: "dyaphane".</note></term>. Regarding this, <term>Averroes<note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), a prominent Al-Andalusian philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to medieval science.</note></term> says: "It is necessary that color be produced from the joining of a shining body with a transparent one." Therefore, a white color in a cloud is caused by the joining of bright light with it, if that cloud possesses a high degree of transparency. A black color in a cloud, however, is caused by the joining of light with it if it has very little transparency. The intermediate colors between white and black vary according to the diversity of these two things: namely, the shining body and the transparent body, according to the greater or lesser degree of each. Therefore Averroes says: "White and black are the elements of colors." The evening redness in a cloud is caused by its thickness...

It also introduces the optical theory of how light and transparency interact to create the colors of clouds, citing Averroes.</summary>

# Meteorology

[...the cloud appears] because of the scorching heat of the sun; and for this reason, it is more or less <term>transparent<note>original: "dyafanū" (diaphanum). Medieval science used this term to describe how easily light could pass through a substance.</note></term>. When the sun's light is joined to it, it produces a red color. Hence, it is also a sign of future clear weather. But morning redness in a cloud is caused by its thickness, due to the humid, watery vapors ascending. When the sun's light is joined to these, it produces a red or "wine-like" color, just as happens with smoke released from green wood when combined with the light of fire. Hence, it is also a sign of future rain.

### On the Milky Way. Chapter.

Regarding the <term>Milky Way<note>original: "galaſia". From the Greek "galaxias," meaning "milky."</note></term>, <term>Aristotle<note>The OCR reads "Arp.", a scribal abbreviation for Aristoteles. His work "Meteorologica" was the standard textbook on this subject.</note></term> says that it is pure fire, located very close to the celestial sphere, inflamed and bright. Within the regions of the Milky Way, there are many small stars—that is, dense ones—as well as large, bright stars close together. From the combination of the light of these stars (which receive their splendor from the sun) and the additional light of the pure, inflamed fire, the light appears elongated. This is what the philosophers have called the "Galaxia." And because these aforementioned stars are fixed in the heavens, the Milky Way is seen in one specific place in the sky and does not move away from it. Let these words suffice for the present concerning things generated from vapor when it is joined with the light of the sun, moon, and stars.

->The End.<-

### Here begins the fifth part, namely on the Soul. Chapter 1.

Following the order of this treatise, having spoken of inanimate things made from the elements, I must now speak of <term>animate things<note>original: "elementatis aĩatis". In the medieval worldview, all things were made of the four elements, but "animate" things possessed a soul or life-force.</note></term>. But since the principle of animate things is the <term>soul<note>original: "aĩa" (anima). In this context, the soul refers to the internal principle of life, growth, and sensation.</note></term>, and since things are more easily understood once their principles are known, we propose to treat the soul. First, we will set down some definitions of the soul itself. Second, we will subjoin the divisions of the powers of the soul.

Note, therefore, that the soul is sometimes defined as a **spirit**. Sometimes as the **mover and ruler of the body**. Sometimes, however, as the **act and perfection of the body**. The soul, insofar as it is a spirit, is defined by a certain wise man in the book *On the Motion of the Heart* as follows: "The soul is an incorporeal, intellectual substance, receptive to the illuminations that come from the <term>Beginning<note>original: "a principio". Here referring to God as the First Principle or source of all light and knowledge.</note></term> by a final revelation."

In this definition:
*   **Substance** is used as the general category (genus).
*   **Incorporeal** is used to distinguish it from physical bodies.
*   **Intellectual** is used to distinguish it from the **vegetative** soul (found in plants) and the **sensitive** soul (found in animals).
*   **Illuminations that are from the Beginning** is used to distinguish it from illuminations that come from created things.
*   **Receptive by a final revelation** is used to distinguish it from **angels**, because angels receive revelations from the Beginning—that is, from God—by a *first* revelation. 

To understand this, note that just as we observe three things in the physical sun—as <term>Augustine<note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD). His "Soliloquies" explore the nature of the soul and God through reason.</note></term> attests in his book of *Soliloquies*—namely, that it *is*, that it *shines*, and that it *illuminates*; so too in God we consider these three: that He *is*, that He *understands*, and that He causes all other things to understand by His illumination. An angel perceives this illumination by a "first relation," but the human soul perceives it by a "final relation," as can be shown by an analogy. Fire and air [receive light] from the <term>fifth essence<note>original: "quinta eſſentia". The celestial material (aether) believed to make up the stars and planets, distinct from the four earthly elements.</note></term>...

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on weather signs and the Milky Way before pivoting to a philosophical and theological study of the human soul. It defines the soul as an incorporeal substance that receives divine light, contrasting the human intellect's "final" reception with the "first" reception of angels.</summary>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the final observations on meteorological phenomena to the philosophical study of the soul (De Anima). It establishes the nature of the soul by comparing how different beings receive "light" and defining the soul's unique relationship to the human body.</meta>

# Meteorology

They receive light, but in different ways. For fire receives light as something natural to itself, co-equal or co-eternal to its very existence. Air, however, receives light not as something natural, nor co-eternal to itself or its essence, but for its <term>well-being<note>original: "bene esse." In scholastic philosophy, this refers to a state of perfection or flourishing that goes beyond mere existence.</note></term>. In the same way, an angel receives illumination from God immediately at its creation; and this is not understood as the illumination of grace or glory, but as a <term>connatural illumination<note>A type of knowledge or light that is part of an angel's natural makeup from the moment of its creation.</note></term>. The soul, although it receives illumination at its creation, does not yet perceive it because it does not comprehend it through sight. Similarly, a puppy, before nine days have passed, receives some light in its pupil because it is naturally suited to see at a determined time, yet it does not perceive the light before the ninth day because it does not yet comprehend through sight. Thus, it can be understood that the soul is at the "final relation" of those illuminations that exist from the beginning, while the angel is at the "first."

Likewise, the soul—insofar as it moves and directs the body, like a mover of the movable, or the nature of a ship—is defined by its "rowing" <note>This refers to the classical analogy of the soul being to the body what a pilot or rower is to a ship.</note>. The soul is an **incorporeal substance ruling the body.**

Regarding this definition, it must first be noted that "incorporeal" is said in two ways. In one way, it is that which is not extended by size or dimension, nor circumscribed by place, even though it is circumscribed by its power and exists in a place by definition; in this way, the soul is incorporeal. In the other way, something is incorporeal which is neither extended by dimension or size, nor circumscribed or defined by place or power; as <term>Augustine<note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose theological writings heavily influenced medieval definitions of the soul and God.</note></term> says: "God alone is incorporeal," because He is entirely uncircumscribed. The soul is therefore incorporeal in the first sense, not the second.

Also, note that although an angel, when it takes on a body, is united to it as a "mover," it is not its "ruler." An angel relates to the body it assumes as a voluntary mover; hence it can also set it aside. The human spirit, however, relates to the body to which it is united as a natural mover, bound by a certain necessity of natural inclination. Because of this, it relates to the body as a king and ruler, bound to its governance by a necessity of love and management. For this reason, the aforementioned definition [ruler of the body] belongs to the soul alone, not to an angel.

Furthermore, the soul, insofar as it is the "act" and "perfection" of the body, is defined by <term>Aristotle<note>The text uses the abbreviation "Aris." Aristotle's "De Anima" (On the Soul) provided the standard definition used here.</note></term> in his book *On the Soul*: **The soul is the first act of a physical organic body potentially possessing life.** This definition is common to the vegetative, sensitive, and rational soul, insofar as the rational soul is capable of being united to a body. 

To explain this definition, it must be known that "act" here is called "perfection." Perfection, moreover, is twofold: perfection according to "habit" <note>In this context, "habit" (habitus) means a stable quality or capacity, like having the ability to speak a language even when silent.</note>—as we say a child is "perfect" in their rationality—and perfection according to "use"—as we say a man is "perfect" in his rationality because he can actually use his reason. Perfection according to habit is the **first act**. Perfection according to use is the **second act**. Since the soul is the perfection, form, and act of the body, it is the "first act," not the second. Concerning this double perfection, Aristotle says in the second book of *On the Soul*: "One is like knowledge; the other is like the act of considering." Of a physical body...

<summary>This page defines the human soul using Aristotelian and Augustinian philosophy, distinguishing it from angels by its natural, "ruling" bond to the physical body. It explains the soul as the "first act" or essential capacity for life in an organic body.</summary>
<keywords>soul, anima, Aristotle, Augustine, incorporeal, first act, second act, illumination, philosophy of mind, scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the nature of the soul, moving from general definitions of how life is received to a technical Aristotelian definition of the "organic body" and the specific powers that characterize the soul.</meta>

-># Meteorology<-

The term **natural** is used to distinguish it from an **artificial body**, such as a statue, a house, or similar things whose perfection is not a soul. **Organic** is used to distinguish it from a seed <note>original: "seminis." The text likely contrasts the fully formed body with the potentiality found in reproductive seeds.</note>, or a newly formed birth; for even if such a thing is a physical body "potentially having life," it is not yet an **organic body**. An organic body is one that has divisible parts, or rather, distinct and separated parts that have specific **operations** <term>operations<note>In scholastic philosophy, an "operation" refers to the specific function or activity a part of a body is designed to perform, such as sight for an eye.</note></term>, as is the case with the bodies of animals and plants. Hence, **Aristotle** says in the second book of *On the Soul*: "The organs and parts of plants exist, such as the leaf, which is the covering of the bark <note>Aristotle actually suggests the leaf protects the fruit/pericarp.</note>." The bark, moreover, protects the fruit—that is, the part which is edible. The roots, however, are similar to mouths; for both draw in nourishment. 

And note the difference between an **organ** and an **instrument**. An **organ** is that which has a motor joined to it from within, such as the eye, the hand, the foot, and the like. For the eye has its motor—the visual power—within; the hand has the power of operation; the foot has the power of progression; and so it is with the others. An **instrument**, however, is that which has its motor joined to it from without, such as a sword, a staff, an axe, and the like. 

The phrase **potentially having life** refers to a natural potentiality. It is said to have life to distinguish it from a **corpse**; for although a corpse may be a physical and organic body, it no longer has the natural or "proximate" potential for life. I say "proximate" because the matter of a corpse, through many stages of decay <note>original: "resolutiones." Refers to the breaking down of physical matter.</note>, may eventually have the potential for life again, but not in a direct way, and it will no longer be that same body. Or, the phrase **potentially having life** is used to show how the body relates to life: namely, as a potentiality, or as a **passive** or receptive principle. The soul, however, acts as the **active** principle of life.

### On the powers of the soul: how many they are, and what they are. Chapter 2.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'S' in red ink, featuring simple line work and scroll-like flourishes.</image-desc>
There follows the division of the powers of the soul. Regarding this, it must first be known that, as **John Damascene** says: "It is impossible for any substance to be devoid of some natural operation." Between a substance and its operation, there stands the **power** as a medium. **Power** is that by which any being is "able" to do something, and through which the substance itself acts. That thing through which a substance goes into action is called a "power." To this point, the blessed **Dionysius** writes in the eleventh chapter of *The Celestial Hierarchy*, where he says: "Every intellect, except for the Divine, is divided into three parts: **essence, power, and operation**." By "power," he understands the capacity for action; for this reason, powers are the intermediaries between a substance and its actions. 

Sometimes, a power is shaped by nature for doing and acting, and then it is called a **natural habit** <term>habit<note>From the Latin "habitus," meaning a stable disposition or state of being that allows one to act in a certain way.</note></term>. At other times, the same power is shaped by the exercise of many actions, and then it is called an **acquired habit**, of which the Philosopher <note>Aristotle.</note> says that a habit is left behind by frequent acting. At still other times, the same power is shaped by grace, and then it is called a **gratuitous** or **infused habit**. According to **Averroes**, a habit is that by which one acts when one wishes; and **Augustine** says that a habit is that by which one acts with ease. It is our purpose, therefore, to speak of the powers of the soul according to the tradi-

<meta>catchword: tradition (tradi-)</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "organic body" as the necessary vehicle for the soul, distinguishing internal organs from external instruments, and begins a classification of the soul's powers based on substance, power, and operation.</summary>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's definition and moves into a scholastic debate regarding whether the "powers" of the soul (like reason or sight) are identical to the soul's essence or distinct from it.</meta>

-># Meteorology<-
<meta>catchword: second-</meta>

...the teaching of learned philosophers and theologians. So that we may not seem to invent, but rather to recite what is more excellent and more true, it should be known that it was the opinion of many—and still is—that the **powers of the soul** are the same as the soul itself. They do not differ except in a certain relation to an action; hence, according to these thinkers, the "power of reasoning" is simply the soul in relation to the act of reasoning. The "irascible power" <note>The "irascible" power refers to the soul's capacity for assertive emotions like anger or courage, used to overcome obstacles.</note> is the soul related to its own act, and thus the soul is its own powers. 

And if it is objected to them that, according to this view, there would be only one single power related to various acts, or that one power would be predicated of another—at least accidentally—they respond that it does not follow. This is because a "power" does not refer to the essence of the soul in **rectitude** <term>rectitude<note>In scholastic logic, "in rectitude" (in rectitudine) means the direct, primary meaning of a term (the "what"), while "in obliquity" refers to its secondary or relational meaning (the "how" or "to what").</note></term>, but in **obliquity**. Indeed, it describes the relation of the soul to this or that act, not the soul itself as related to the act. And because this relation is not that one, there is not just one single power, nor is one predicated of the other if the power is spoken of in the abstract; for then it describes the relation of the soul to the act, not the soul itself as related to the act. 

Those who hold this opinion have much in their favor, for **Augustine** says in the book *On the Spirit and the Soul* <note>original: "de spiritu & anima." This work was widely read in the Middle Ages but is now considered pseudo-Augustinian.</note> that the soul is its own powers. He indeed says this there. But others say that Augustine did not compose this book, and their reason is that in his book of *Retractions* <note>original: "libro retractationum." This was Augustine’s own catalog and correction of his life's work.</note>, where he enumerates all his books, he says nothing about that volume. 

Furthermore, many points stand against them. Hence, others say that the soul is not its own powers, but that there are many powers differing in **essence**, though not in **substance**. This opinion is more celebrated and seems truer. For just as "that by which it is" and "that which it is" differ in the soul, 

so too do "that by which it is able" and "that which it is able" differ. Hence, although the powers are the same in substance and in **subject**, they nevertheless differ in essence. For **essence** is that by which a thing is or is not—that is, that by which it is rational, or by which it is concupiscible <note>The "concupiscible" power is the soul's capacity for desiring what is perceived as good or pleasant.</note>, etc. **Substance**, however, is that which underlies the properties from which it is inseparable. The **subject** is the same thing compared to acts, insofar as acts are present in it or absent from it, and without which it can well exist. Therefore, because of this, they say that the powers of the soul and the soul itself agree in subject and substance, but differ, as has been said, in essence. 

Now let us come to the division of the powers of the soul. It should be known that **Aristotle**, in the second book of *On the Soul*, divides the powers of the soul into five differences: the **vegetative**, the **sensitive**, the **appetitive**, the **motive** according to place, and the **intellective**. What is called "motive according to place" here refers to that from which comes progressive movement in the whole body, not that movement which is of expansion in the parts according to growth, because such movement is also found in plants. 

**Avicenna**, however, in the sixth book of *On Animals* <note>original: "sexto de animalibus." This likely refers to the psychological section of Avicenna's vast encyclopedia, the *Shifa*.</note>, posits only three powers of the soul: the **vegetative**, the **sensible**, and the **rational**. He includes the appetitive and the motive according to place under the "sensible." Why, then, did Aristotle divide the "sensitive" against the others—namely, the appetitive and the motive according to place—since these three are all sensible powers of the soul? The reason for this was that "sensible," in terms of the force of the name and its property, refers to a **power of apprehension**, and thus a passive power. And because of this, he sets the appetitive and the motive according to place in opposition, as they are active and motive. Avicenna, however, divided second-

<summary>This page explores the scholastic debate on whether the soul's powers (like reason or desire) are identical to its essence or distinct qualities, ultimately favoring the view that they differ in essence. It also compares how Aristotle and Avicenna classify these various psychological powers.</summary>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the soul's powers, moving from the definitions of Augustine to the categorizations of Aristotle, John of Damascus, and medical authorities like Johannitius. It bridges the gap between theology, philosophy, and medieval physiology.</meta>

-># Meteorology<-

...while there are three distinctions of the soul, which are: the **vegetative**, the **sensible**, and the **rational**. When these are present in the same being—as in a human—they should not be called "three souls," but rather three **powers**. However, insofar as they exist in different beings—namely, in a plant, a beast, or a human—they can be called three souls. But more on this will be discussed below. 

**John of Damascus** <note>original: "Damascenus"; a 7th-century monk and theologian influential in medieval scholasticism.</note>, however, first proposes a two-part division: namely, the **rational** and the **irrational**. He divides the irrational into two: that which is "obedient" (or persuadable by reason) and that which is "disobedient" (or unpersuadable by reason). He calls the "obedient" part that which belongs to the plant-like or natural power. He uses the terms "plant-like," "natural," or "**vegetative**" <term>vegetative<note>The "vegetative soul" refers to the most basic level of life responsible for nutrition, growth, and reproduction, shared by plants, animals, and humans.</note></term>. Under the disobedient or unpersuadable part, he also includes the **vital** <note>original: "zotica"</note> or **pulsing** power, which doctors call "spiritual." 

Indeed, the "disobedient" or "unpersuadable" category encompasses more than the vegetative or natural power described by **Aristotle** <note>original: "Are."</note>. Aristotle, however, does not mention the "pulsing" power in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: "libro de anima"</note>, perhaps because he would have had to descend too far into anatomical detail. Damascus, however, acting as both a theologian and a physician, treats all these matters. Therefore, he includes this category. 

A certain distinction must be noted here: for the "**zodiacal**" <note>original: "zodiacha"</note> and the "**vital**" <note>original: "zotica"</note> powers are not the same. The zodiacal power is the same as the "**animal**" power <note>In this context, "animal" refers to the *anima* or soul's capacity for movement and sensation.</note>. This is why the circle in the middle of the heavens containing the twelve signs is called the "Zodiac," because those are the figures of living animals. However, "vital" is not the same as "animal"; rather, it is distinguished from it in the book *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: "li. de celo & mundo"</note> in the chapter beginning "It is necessary to know," etc. There it says that of the powers, some are animal, some are natural, and some are vital (that is, spiritual). 

This same division is made in medicine in the book of **Johannitius** <note>Johannitius (809–873) was a translator and physician whose "Isagoge" was a fundamental medical text in the Middle Ages.</note> where he says that the power is a three-part division: natural, spiritual, and animal. The spiritual power is the same as the vital or pulsing power. This is clear because the function of the spiritual power is to dilate the heart and the arteries and then to constrict them again. And this is the function of the pulsing power. For, as **Philaretus** <note>original: "philotetus"; refers to a 7th-century physician whose work on the pulse was a standard medical text.</note> says, it is the motion of the heart and arteries which, through **systole** and **diastole** <term>systole and diastole<note>The contraction and expansion of the heart and arteries used to regulate heat and "vital spirits" in the body.</note></term>, occurs for the cooling of innate heat and the expulsion of excess smoky vapors. The zodiacal power, however, is the same as the animal power. 

It follows from the things said above that there are five distinctions of the powers of the soul: according to Aristotle there are three; according to **Avicenna**, Damascus, and the medical naturalists, they agree in the literal text and in their opinion. All, however, agree in their general meaning. The question remains whether in a human the vegetative, sensitive, and rational powers are three souls or only one.

### Chapter 3.

<image-desc>Large ornamental initial D with internal floral/acanthus leaf patterning and a red outer border.</image-desc>

Let us now say what we promised above: namely, to declare that the **vegetative**, **sensible**, and **rational** in a human are not to be called "three souls." Some have indeed doubted this and held a different view; for they wanted to prove that there are three different souls in a human according to their **substance** <term>substance<note>The essential nature or underlying reality of a thing.</note></term>, in this manner: 

**John of Damascus** says: "Those things whose substances or natures are different, have different operations. And those whose substance is the same, have the same operation." Therefore, those things which do not have the same operations do not have the same substance. But in a human, the operations of the vegetative, sensible, and rational soul are not the same; therefore, the substance is not the same. 

To the same point: for one immediate cause, there is one effect; and for many causes, there are many effects. But proper and essential **accidents** <note>In scholastic philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that belong to a thing but are not part of its essential definition.</note> are the effects of substance; therefore, since the vegetative, sensible, and rational souls have diverse properties and...

<summary>This page analyzes the classification of the soul's powers (vegetative, sensible, rational, and vital), comparing the views of Aristotle, John of Damascus, and medieval medical authorities. It argues whether these functions represent distinct substances or a single unified soul in humans.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, John of Damascus, Johannitius, Philaretus, vegetative soul, rational soul, vital power, systole and diastole, zodiacal power, scholastic psychology, medieval medicine</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of whether the soul's different functions (vegetative, sensitive, rational) represent distinct substances or merely different powers of a single soul. The previous page ended by establishing the three distinctions of the soul across different life forms.</meta>

### On the Soul.

...innate accidents: it is necessary to posit that the soul is divisible and composite, or that these are diverse substances. Furthermore, a power does not exist without that to which the power belongs; but the power of the **vegetative soul** is in the embryo. For **Aristotle** <note>original: "Ari."</note> says both that the embryo lives and that the limbs are formed in the embryo according to the vegetative power that is in the embryo; therefore, it is necessary for the vegetative soul to be present there. 

And if it is said that a power can exist without that to which the power belongs—because, as the **Philosopher** <note>Aristotle</note> holds, the "power of fire" can exist in the air <note>This refers to heat residing in air even when the flame is gone.</note>, yet fire is not actually there—nevertheless, the same point can be maintained by the same Philosopher, who says that although the power of fire is there, it does not operate unless it is "excited" or activated by fire itself. Since, therefore, the vegetative power operates in the embryo, it is necessary that it does this having been activated by that to which the power belongs. And therefore, it is necessary for the vegetative soul to be there in actuality. But the **rational soul** is not yet there; therefore, they are not the same in substance. 

To the same point: whatever things are the same according to **substance** <term>substance<note>In medieval philosophy, "substance" refers to the essential reality of a thing that exists in itself, rather than a property that exists in something else.</note></term>, if one is separable, then the rest must be as well. But the rational soul is separable <note>meaning it can exist apart from the body after death</note>. The vegetative and sensible souls are inseparable from the body; therefore, they are not the same according to substance. 

But on the contrary: whatever an inferior power can do, a superior power can also do. Since the rational is the superior power, it can do whatever the other two can do, either in itself or through its own potencies. Since, therefore, nature does nothing in vain, and the rational soul is sufficient for all these operations, there will not be in a human being but one soul—namely the rational—which possesses the power of "vegetating" (living) and "sensing." 

Also to this point is what the **Commentator** <note>original: "cōmentator"; this refers to Averroes, the 12th-century Islamic philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to medieval university teaching.</note> says on the book *On the Soul*, where he rebukes **Plato** for positing three separate substances, and commends Aristotle for positing only one. Likewise, **Augustine** in the book *On the Spirit and the Soul* says: "That one and the same soul sees in the eye, hears in the ear, imagines, reasons, and understands." 

Also, in a dying person: when the rational soul departs, the power of living and sensing does not remain. Yet they *would* remain if they were diverse in substance from the rational soul, according to the order they would have had in their beginning or creation. For that which is first in "generation" (coming into being) is the last in "resolution" (falling apart/death). 

To the same point: to one "perfectible" thing corresponds one **perfection** <term>perfection<note>In this context, 'perfection' refers to the 'form' or soul that completes a physical body and makes it a living thing.</note></term>. And if the thing to be perfected is not different in substance, neither will the perfection be different in substance and essence—for substance and essence are the same. But the vegetative, sensible, and rational aspects are the same according to substance. Therefore, the perfection of those three will be the same according to substance. Therefore, in a human being, the vegetative, sensible, and rational do not differ substantially. 

To the same point, a theological reason can be brought forward: when the soul is occupied with carnal desires, it is hindered from the contemplation of higher things. Therefore, the soul must be one and the same; for if they were diverse in substance, the act of one would not depend on the act of the other, nor would one be drawn away from its own act by the act of the other. But since it is false that they act independently, it is manifest that there is but one soul in a human being. 

This is the position we concede. As for the opposing arguments, they must be answered. Regarding the first objection—"Those things whose substances are diverse," etc.—it can be answered that for one subject, there is...

<meta>catchword: ...one is.</meta>

<summary>This page debates whether the human soul consists of three distinct substances (vegetative, sensible, and rational) or a single substance with multiple powers. The author concludes that the soul is a single unit, citing Aristotle, Averroes, and Augustine to argue that all biological and mental functions cease simultaneously at death.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Averroes, Augustine, vegetative soul, rational soul, embryo, unity of the soul, substance, medieval psychology, embryology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding whether the human soul is a single substance with multiple powers or if the vegetative, sensitive, and rational functions arise from distinct souls. The previous page established the problem of how an embryo can have life before the rational soul is present.</meta>

# On the Soul.

...principal operation: nevertheless, many can be secondary. For example: this material Sun, which is one and the same in substance, has the shining as its principal operation, yet it has many secondary ones, such as illuminating, refining, condensing, liquefying, contracting, and the like. Similarly, the principal action of the soul is to animate—that is, either to give life or to infuse life. Its secondary actions, however, are to vegetate, to sense (or to give life), and to reason. Or it can be said regarding the statement: *Those things whose operations are not the same*, etc. <note>Aristotle often argues that distinct operations imply distinct natures.</note> This is understood to apply when the operations are so diverse that one is not ordered toward the other. But the **vegetative soul** <term>vegetative soul<note>The level of the soul shared by plants, responsible for growth, nutrition, and reproduction.</note></term> and its operation are ordered toward the **sensitive soul** <term>sensitive soul<note>The level of the soul shared by animals, responsible for perception and movement.</note></term>, and the sensitive toward the **rational soul** <term>rational soul<note>The uniquely human capacity for intellect and will.</note></term>; indeed, the material functions are directed toward the higher one. Thus, the conclusion does not follow. Or it can be said that "vegetating" belongs not only to the vegetative soul, but also to the higher souls. And "sensing" belongs not only to the sensitive soul, but also to the rational. Thus, the rational soul vegetates and provides sensation to the body, though not insofar as it is rational. It can also be said that this proposition has no place here because it is true only when the operation emerges immediately from the substance without a mediating power—if indeed such a thing is found in any creature.

To the second point, it must be said that it is true that one effect comes from one cause; however, the operation and the effect are not the same thing. For one effect is sometimes left behind by several different operations.

To the third point, it must be said that the "power" of the vegetative soul is in the **embryo**, but the vegetative soul itself is not there, nor is the sensitive or rational soul. And if it is objected: "Therefore an **accident** <term>accident<note>In philosophy, a quality or property that exists only within a "subject" (like color in an apple), rather than existing on its own.</note></term> exists without a **subject**," it is not a valid objection. For a power is an accident, but a power is not always an accident of the thing to which it belongs as a power. And therefore I say that it is an accident of the embryo and is in it as in a subject; however, it is not, nor is it called, the "power of the embryo." This is evident in the case of "place," for it is the place of one thing and the accident of another; specifically, it is an accident of the container. Thus, it is in the container as in a subject, yet it is not the quantity, or measure, or place of the container; rather, it is the quantity and place measuring the thing contained. And regarding the final objection—that such a power does not operate unless it is activated—we concede this. Hence it is said that it is activated by the vegetative soul or power that is in the one receiving the seed. For this reason, it is immediately bound to the womb. Thus, that bond must be broken before the child is born. An example is seen in a branch that is grafted onto a trunk: the branch is nourished and grows through the vegetation of the trunk, as an external benefit occurring to it.

To the fourth point, it must be said that since the vegetative, sensitive, and rational are the same soul, the vegetative and sensitive are "separated"—that is, that which is vegetative or sensitive is separated, but not under the specific definition of being vegetative and sensitive. If someone wished to infer that they are separable *insofar as* they are vegetative and sensitive, it is a fallacy similar to this: "Whatever things are the same according to substance, if one grows, the other does too. But Socrates as a boy and Socrates as an old man are one according to substance; therefore, etc." <note>This is a logical trap; while the person is the same, the properties of "growing" change over time.</note> If a saying is found by **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristo."</note> that the vegetative and sensitive are prior to the rational, it is to be understood as being in different beings, or "prior" meaning more universal, or even prior in the human being because the vegetative operation occurs first. There would be many more arguments for both sides, but let us set them aside for the present.

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the soul's powers. Having previously discussed whether the soul is one or many, the text now systematically divides the functions of the vegetative and sensitive souls.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

### Chapter 4: On the division of the powers of the vegetative soul.

Let us return, then, to the division of the **vegetative soul** <term>vegetative soul<note>The most basic level of life, found in plants and all higher living things, governing growth and survival.</note></term> into its powers, which are three: namely, the **nutritive**, the **augmentative**, and the **generative**. 

The act of the **nutritive power** is to nourish, or to use food for the restoration of what has been lost. The vegetative soul always possesses this power and its acts, because this was given to it for the preservation of the individual. For the food by which a living thing is nourished assists the **radical moisture** <term>radical moisture<note>original: "humidum radicale"; in medieval medicine, this was the vital fluid present from birth that acted as the "fuel" for life.</note></term> itself, so that it is not consumed too quickly by the **radical or natural heat**. There is an example of this in a burning lamp: the wick would be quickly consumed if there were not external moisture in the oil, on which the fire feeds. 

The nutritive power has four parts: namely, the **attractive**, the **digestive**, the **retentive**, and the **expulsive**. 
*   The **attractive** is that which draws in food, which is very necessary for plants. 
*   The **digestive** is that which digests and alters the food that has been drawn in. Hence it is called "alterative" by Damascene <note>John of Damascus (c. 675–749), a theologian whose works on nature and the soul were widely used in the Middle Ages.</note>. 
*   The **retentive** is that which retains from the digested food that which is fit for nourishing. 
*   The **expulsive** is that which expels waste: whether through excretion, through urine, through sweat, or in some other way. 

The act of the **augmentative power** is to increase, or to bring whatever is growing to its proper size. I say "proper," because, as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle (384–322 BC), usually referred to simply as "The Philosopher" in these texts.</note> says: "For all things established by nature, there is a limit and a proportion of magnitude and growth." 

The act of the **generative power** is to reproduce. However, the generative power is for the preservation of the self within the species, which could not be preserved by itself since it is perishable. Because of this, it is preserved in another through the continuous multiplication of individuals. And thus, in some way, it participates in divine—that is, perpetual—being. Therefore, even in incorruptible individuals, such as the sun and the moon and things of this sort, there is no reproduction, because the species is sufficiently preserved within them.

### Chapter 5: On the division of the powers of the sensitive soul.

After these things, we must speak of the powers of the **sensitive soul**. Of these, this is the first division: some are **apprehensive** <note>original: "apprehensiue"; referring to the powers of perception and gathering information.</note> and some are **motive** <note>original: "motiue"; referring to the powers that cause physical movement.</note>. 

These distinctions do not properly apply to the vegetative soul, because every apprehensive power is cognitive, which in no way exists in plants. Likewise, the apprehensive power always perceives the thing to be perceived existing at some distance or separation from the perceiving power. For a sensible object placed directly upon the sense organ is not felt; rather, it is joinable to it by means of something else—as in sight, when color is perceived, which is at a distance from the organ of seeing. It is joined by means of light; and although color is received in the eye, it is the **likeness** <gloss>original: "species"</gloss> of the color that is received. 

But in the vegetative soul, the power is joined directly with its object, as the nutritive power is with food or the augmentative with that which it grows; nor is the "likeness" of the food joined with the body to be nourished, but rather the substance of the food itself. Likewise, that distinction which is called the "motive power" is not in plants as it is in animals. For although in all reproduction and growth there is movement from one place to another, it is not, however, a progressive movement, but a movement of expansion or contraction; in the sensitive or rational [soul...]

<summary>This page details the three powers of the vegetative soul (nutrition, growth, and reproduction) and begins to contrast them with the sensitive soul, which requires "distance" and perception to function.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's powers, moving from the vegetative functions (nutrition, growth, reproduction) to the sensitive functions found in animals. It specifically addresses why animals possess the power of movement and begins a detailed classification of the five external senses.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...is in the rational soul: because a good, whether it is a true good or an apparent one, moves the appetite when it is apprehended. And because that same good, once apprehended, is not immediately joined to the being, the power of **progressive motion** <term>progressive motion<note>The ability of an animal to move itself from one location to another to achieve a goal.</note></term> from place to place was given to it so that it might seek that good and unite with it.

And if it is objected that not every animal moves from place to place—such as conches and oysters, which only have the motion of expansion and contraction like plants—it must be said that the motion of those animals is not solely expansion and contraction. Rather, it is also a motion that in some way follows the apprehensive power. This is evident because it occurs with sensible pleasure or pain; for these feelings follow the senses, as Aristotle <note>original: "Ar[istoteles]"; the "Philosopher" frequently cited in medieval texts.</note> says.

Furthermore, even if there is expansion in both [plants and these animals], the expansion in one is different from the other. For in plants, the expansion of parts occurs according to the quantity of growth. In conches, however, it occurs according to the diffusion of **spirits** <term>spirits<note>original: "spirituum"; in medieval physiology, "animal spirits" were subtle fluids that carried sensory and motor information through the body.</note></term> around the heart. But this does nothing to prove that there is more motion in animals than in plants; rather, it shows that there is a motion there that follows apprehension in some way. Also, there is no objection regarding those animals, since they are "imperfect animals," as the Philosopher understands them.

Of the apprehensive powers, some are **external** and some are **internal**. 

An external apprehensive power is said to be that which apprehends a thing from the outside, while the object is present in its underlying matter. There are five such senses: namely, **Sight**, **hearing**, **smell**, **taste**, and **touch**. These agree in that they are passive powers of the soul and are receptive of **species** <term>species<note>In this context, the "species" is the sensible form or likeness of an object that travels through a medium to the sense organ.</note></term> just as matter is. Therefore, for this reason, their objects are called "passible qualities" <term>passible qualities<note>Qualities that act upon the senses to produce a sensation or "passion."</note></term> which cause an impression in the sense. 

And note that for these senses to perceive in actuality, four things are required: two on the part of the sense itself—namely, the spirit and the organ—and two from the outside—namely, the object and the **medium** <term>medium<note>The substance, such as air or water, through which a sensory signal travels.</note></term>. Thus, in the process of abstraction, the form of the object is first in the medium, second in the organ, and third in the spirit. And when it is in the spirit, it easily reaches the soul. First, therefore, a change occurs in the medium, then in the particular sense organ itself, and finally, a judgment follows in the **common sense** <term>common sense<note>original: "sensus communis"; the internal faculty that gathers and unifies data from the five individual senses.</note></term>.

An internal apprehensive power is said to be that which apprehends from within, either the thing itself or its image. For example, the **imagination** receives from the common sense, and the **intellect** from the imagination, as will be seen below.

Of the senses, two are more necessary: namely, **taste** and **touch**. Taste is for discerning good food from that which is harmful; touch is for discerning those things that corrupt the body from the outside from those that preserve it. Thus, we flee from what is burning because it causes corruption. 

Smell, hearing, and sight, however, exist for the well-being of the animal. And perhaps for this reason, nature has ordained it so that in those two senses which are more necessary, the medium is internal, so that there can be no failure on account of the medium. The other three have an external medium. Hence, it often happens that the sense of sight does not see because the medium is not illuminated. 

How these three senses contribute to the well-being of the animal is manifest. For two of them—namely, sight and hearing—are necessary for obtaining **knowledge** <note>original: "scientia"</note>, according to the two ways by which knowledge is acquired. For everything that anyone knows, they know either by learning or by discovery. Therefore, to obtain knowledge through teaching, hearing is necessary to receive directly from the teacher. But as for obtaining knowledge through...

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...discovery, **sight** is especially necessary because it sees such diverse things—beautiful and ugly, the stars in the heaven, and so on. One wonders, and from wonder proceeds the path to **philosophizing** <note>original: "philosophari"; a reference to the Aristotelian idea that "philosophy begins in wonder."</note>; by inquiring into the natures and causes of things. Although it also serves that way of acquiring knowledge which is through teaching, this is mediated through writings. Furthermore, it should be known that among all the senses, sight contributes most to knowledge, insofar as it is generated through the **abstraction of the form** <term>abstraction of the form<note>The process by which the sense takes in the appearance or "species" of an object without taking in its physical matter.</note></term> from objects. This is evident in the lower powers: for **imagination** <note>original: "fantasia"</note> is named from the act of sight, and the **common sense** <term>common sense<note>The internal faculty that integrates data from the five external senses.</note></term> says: "I see that I see." Likewise, through hearing we learn the names of things, but through sight, we recognize the differences between things. 

**Smell**, however, is the least necessary among all the senses; nonetheless, it contributes to the health of the animal. For the animal flees when it perceives the corruption of the air. The corruption of air, as the physician says, harms the animal more than corrupt food. Likewise, it contributes to knowledge occasionally or accidentally. For when fragrant things are smelled, they comfort the brain, and this comfort does much to allow for better reasoning.

### On the five senses in particular: and first on sight. Chapter 12.

Therefore, we must speak of each individual sense. And first, regarding sight. The object of sight is **color**. For color, as **the Philosopher** <note>Aristotle.</note> says, is that which changes the sight according to the act of the light. Yet many things are seen which do not have color, such as the sky, the earth, water, and many others. Hence we believe that the **lucid** <note>original: "lucidum"; that which is bright or filled with light.</note> is properly the object of sight, and color is not the object except by reason of the light which is there. For **light** <note>original: "lux"</note> in its own subject is a luminous body; light in a **diaphanous** <note>A medium that allows light to pass through, like air or glass.</note> or transparent medium is **illumination** <note>original: "lumē"; medieval thinkers often distinguished between *lux* (light at its source) and *lumen* (light as it travels through space).</note>. In a polished, smooth surface it is **splendor**, but in a mixed body it is **color**. And thus it is defined: Color is the extremity of the transparent in a bounded body. A **ray**, however, adds direction to light, because a ray is directed light; just as virtue adds to grace, because virtue is grace ordered toward an act or work.

The medium of sight is the transparent and pervious. Wherefore note that there are certain **solid bodies** that are opaque on the surface, such as the earth, which it is impossible for the sight to penetrate or pass through. Likewise, there are certain bodies that are transparent on the surface but opaque according to their solidity, such as gold and silver, which are similarly impenetrable to sight. Likewise, there are certain bodies that are transparent both in their surface and in their depth, such as air, water, and crystal. And this transparent and pervious body is the medium of sight.

Its instrument or organ, which is more properly called the **eye**, is that to which the **optic nerve** <term>optic nerve<note>original: "neruus obticus"</note></term> reaches, extending from the brain to the pupil. And therefore the **visible spirit** <term>visible spirit<note>original: "spūs uisibilis"; in medieval physiology, a subtle fluid that flowed through the nerves to carry sensory information.</note></term>—which is in that nerve branched out to the pupils of the eyes—receives the form of color from the **crystalline humor** <term>crystalline humor<note>The lens of the eye, which medieval scholars believed was the central organ where the act of seeing actually occurred.</note></term> which is in the pupil. And note that in order for the pupil to be able to receive any color, it is necessary that it have no color in actuality, but be potentially any color. For if it were actually of some color, it could not receive any other except that one. Hence, when the pupil has been strongly changed by an excellent white, such as the white of snow, immediately even if that is removed...

### On the power of hearing and its object. Chapter 7.

The object of **hearing** is sound, which is produced in the air from the collision of two bodies against each other. From that collision, a tremor is produced in the air, which continues until it reaches the ear. Hence, two things are required for the generation of sound: namely, that there be contact of a hard body against another hard body with air as the medium. And it must happen with a blow or violence; for if it did not happen with violence and the bodies were not hard or firm, they would not sound. 

As **Aristotle** <note>original: "Arist."; the text refers to his work *De Anima* (On the Soul).</note> gives the example of a sponge and hair: [they do not sound because they lack firmness]. And again, if the contact did not happen with violence, there would be no sound, as is evident. But when an object is moved violently with a speed of motion, finding a body that resists it, it creates a tremor that continues to the ear. Thus, the sound once made spreads out in the manner of a sphere <note>original: "ad modum spe[re]"; the text describes sound waves expanding like a bubble.</note>, the center of which is the place of the strike. 

Whence a voice is heard from every side, as the **Philosopher** <note>A standard title for Aristotle in medieval texts.</note> says. It generates a tremor that is always weaker in a circle until it fades away. A likeness of this appears in the throwing of a stone into water. The medium of hearing is the air continued to the air placed within the cavity of the ear. Indeed, a double air is required for hearing to occur: namely, the exterior air in which the sound is made, and the interior air, connatural to the ear, contained within the cavities <note>original: "folles"; literally bellows or bags.</note> of the ears. The organ of hearing is the **eardrum** <term>timpanum<note>The tympanic membrane.</note></term>, to which is directed a nerve extended from the brain containing the **audible spirit** <term>spiritus<note>In medieval physiology, "spirits" were subtle fluids or gases that moved through the nerves to carry sensory information to the brain.</note></term>. It is characteristic of this sense that its sensible object is always in the medium, and therefore it lacks its own abstraction—namely, that which is taken from the object itself.

### On the object of smell. Chapter 8.

The object of **smell** in the fragrant thing is the odor; in the medium, it is called smoke or vapor, which heat releases from the odoriferous thing. This is the reason why a fragrant thing often rots more quickly, such as an apple. For the more subtle humors that preserve the thing are drawn out through the process of smelling. The medium of smell is air. The organs are two **small fleshy parts** <term>carunculae<note>Referring to the olfactory bulbs at the base of the brain.</note></term> descending from the brain, similar to two nipples, through which the odorous spirit arrives and creates the sensation.

### On the object of taste. Chapter 9.

The object of **taste** is flavor, which is in the flavorful thing. When a flavorful thing is joined with the moisture of saliva, it is transformed by it, and through the spongy flesh on the surface of the tongue—which is the internal medium—it delivers its flavor to the spirit which is in the internal nerve. 

Hence it happens that if the salivary moisture is infected with the bitterness of **bile** <note>original: "colere"; refers to yellow bile, one of the four humors, which was thought to cause a bitter taste during illness.</note>, the taste judges the food to be bitter, even if the food is sweet. And in this, the taste is not actually deceived, because it truly perceives bitterness; and in truth, there is bitterness present. But it is deceived in this: that it places the bitterness in the food where it is not, [rather than in the tongue where it is]. Therefore, the medium of taste is twofold: internal and external. The internal medium is the spongy flesh of the tongue. The external medium is the salivary moisture. The organ of taste is the nerve descending from the brain to...

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...the tongue, which in some animals is divided; and because of this, they have two tongues. In others, however, it is united or single, and these have only one tongue.

### On Touch and its Object. Chapter 10.

**T**he act [of sensing] can be named from the thing that feels, and thus it is one sense; or from the things that are felt. And thus it perceives four things. First: hot and cold, wet and dry. From these come hard and soft, rough and smooth, heavy and light. However, there is no single "nearest genus" <note>In logic and philosophy, a "genus" is a general category. While "color" covers all specific colors, there isn't one single word that covers all the different physical feelings of touch.</note> for these qualities, just as color is the genus of all colors; and because they do not fall under one genus in this regard, touch is [considered] multiple senses. Thus **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian polymath whose works on the soul heavily influenced medieval European thought.</note> says that there are five or eight external senses. Therefore, the object—or rather, the objects—are hot and cold, etc. Its medium, however, is the flesh covering the nerves. The organ of touch is the nerve, branched in many ways; it is like a garment clothing the whole body, and therefore touch is a general sense. For it exists in every part of the body, and even in the organs of the other senses; however, it is most active in the pads of the fingers, especially the index finger.

### On the Media in General. Chapter 11.

**M**edia <note>The "medium" is the substance through which a sensation travels, such as air for sound or flesh for touch.</note> are indeed necessary for sensation to occur, because a sensible object placed directly upon the sense organ is not felt. For sensation happens through the reception of the *species* <note>original: "speciei"; in medieval philosophy, a "species" is the "likeness" or "image" of an object that travels to the sense organ without the physical matter of the object itself.</note> or likeness of the object, not according to its essence. For if the sense received the essence of its object, there would never be a sensation of opposites. Thus, it would not be possible to see white and black at the same time, or to touch hot and cold [together], which is clearly false. For it is established that the eye, being unaltered and remaining in the same state, receives both whiteness <note>original: "albedinem"</note> and blackness <note>original: "nigredinem"</note> while seeing; this would not be the case if it received the physical essence of that object. For in that way, opposites would exist in the same thing according to their essence. Because nothing is received by the sense except the *species* of the object, a medium is necessary in every sense.

### On the Objects of the Senses in General. Chapter 12.

**R**egarding the objects of the senses, it must be said that the object of a sense is the "sensible" thing which is perceived by that sense. Note, therefore, that there are things sensible *per se* <note>Essentially or by their own nature.</note> and things sensible *per accidens* <note>Incidentally or by association.</note>. Something is sensible *per accidens* when the sensible object of one sense is said to be perceived by another. For example, when it is said that "sweetness is seen in an apple"; since sweetness is the object of taste, it is said to be "seen" *per accidens*—that is, through something else, namely through the reddish color of the apple. In itself and properly, however, sweetness is not perceived except by taste. Of things sensible *per se*, some are "common" and some are "proper." A "common sensible" is that which is perceived by multiple senses; this happens through the nature of the "common sense" <note>A central faculty in the brain that coordinates data from the five individual senses.</note> existing within them. Examples include magnitude (length, width, and depth), motion and rest, time, and place. These are the common sensibles and their properties. For these properly belong to the common or interior sense, about which more will be said later. However, because the common sense is not separate in reality from the particular senses, these [are perceived] by the particular [senses]...

<summary>This page analyzes the mechanics of touch, the necessity of a physical medium for all senses, and the distinction between proper, accidental, and common objects of perception.</summary>
<keywords>touch, Avicenna, species, medium, common sense, proper sensibles, accidental sensibles, Aristotelian philosophy</keywords>
