<meta>The previous page discussed the physical senses, concluding with the sense of touch and the media through which sensations travel. This page shifts from the "internal" world of the soul and senses to the "external" world of the atmosphere, specifically the nature and location of vapors as part of a study on meteorology.</meta>

<header>On Meteorology.</header>

**L**et us return, then, to the vapor from which things composed of elements <note>original: "elemẽtata"; referring to physical substances formed by the mixing of the four basic elements: earth, water, air, and fire</note> are formed: both those generated in the heights and those in the bowels of the earth. It must be known, therefore, that every vapor comes from either water or earth. For air itself is not "vaporizable." For it is either thinned <note>rarefied</note> beyond its own nature—and then it immediately becomes fire—or it is thickened further, and then it is no longer vapor, because vapor is generated through thinning and rarefaction; instead, the air then becomes water or earth. This double vapor, therefore, is the material cause of all those things generated either on high or below.

There are four locations of generation. Specifically: the **upper region**, which because of its proximity to fire is called by the proper name "Heat" <note>original: "estus"</note> by the philosophers. And the **middle zone** or region which is called the "place of cold." And the **lower part of the air**, which is above the earth and possesses some heat and some cold, which is called the "vapor of the earth."

The upper part of the air, being pure and thin, is intensely hot due to its proximity to fire. And because of the rays of the stars and their light, which are closer to it, it is luminous, more tranquil, and subtle; however, it receives its nearby light not from the neighboring fire, but from the proximity of the stars. And because the shadow of the earth is so contracted and diminished there, the sun's rays remain in it longer; therefore, that part is a place of inflammation by its very nature.

The lower part, however, since it is around the earth and above it, possesses cold from itself because of the water and the earth. Yet it becomes warm from the percussion, reflection, or even the refraction of rays against the solid, flat surface of the earth and water; that part does not become inflamed, but rather "breathes out" its own vapor. Its nature prevents it from being burned or set on fire, yet it exhales vapor from itself, drawn out by the reflection of the rays. This last or lower part which is around us touches the solid element which is around the polished plane and surface, such as water; and therefore this reflection of rays is great, whence also a greater light is generated.

The region of the air, however, taken as being equidistant from these two, is exceedingly cold for the opposite reason. For it has neither heat from the nearby fire, nor from the reflection of rays, because rays do not reflect off transparent or "diaphanous" substances, but only off the solid and the flat—and even more so if it is polished, like the surface of water, a mirror, or something else smooth and flat. And this region is more shadowy and dark, because it lacks the cause of both types of illumination; and therefore it is stormy, and there are generated tempests, snows, and hailstones. And this is the **habitation of demons** and aerial powers, to whom that murky place has been assigned. Whence the saints say that they stir up winds and thunders and such things to terrify men.

The region which is in the middle, between that one just mentioned and the place of habitation (that is, the earth), is temperate in its cold and heat as far as its own nature goes, receiving a distinction from both; accidentally, however, it is intemperately hot, because it is near to or overcome by the lower region at certain times. And it is intemperately cold according to how it nears the upper region at other times. Thus, let this be the determination regarding the matter of vapors in general, and regarding their location.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of atmospheric science (meteorology), moving from the general locations of the "four regions" of air to the specific "efficient causes" (heat and cold) that create weather and geological phenomena.</meta>

# On Meteorology

The efficient cause of atmospheric phenomena <note>original: "impressionum"; in medieval science, an "impression" refers to any phenomenon produced by the elements in the air or earth, such as rain, lightning, or minerals</note> in specific is manifold, but in general it is only twofold: namely, heat and cold. Heat itself is twofold: specifically, the heat of the sun, which raises all vapor, and the heat of a place, which disperses or ignites that which has been raised. The heat of the sun, as far as it suffices for our present purpose, is twofold: scorching and raising. Scorching occurs for two reasons: one is direct positioning <note>the sun being directly overhead at the zenith</note>. The other is nearly direct, occurring when the sun is in the summer signs, especially around the summer tropic, such as **Gemini, Cancer, Leo, and Virgo**. At that time, the rays of the sun fall upon the regions below as if in a perpendicular line falling from the center of the sun; they are reflected back upon themselves and are made infinite in number, trapping a scorching heat against the earth. Therefore, in those places, nothing "breathes out" <note>original: "expirat"; refers to the process of evaporation</note> from the earth, but everything is scorched before it can be raised. However, in nearby places that approach further toward the north <note>original: "aquilonem"</note> in latitude: the further they recede from the path of the sun, the more obliquely they are viewed, and the angles of the rays are widened. Consequently, the heat becomes less—less scorching and more effective at calling forth the breath of evaporation. Thus, according to the latitude of the climates <note>term: "climatum"; geographical zones or bands of latitude</note> from the path of the sun, it is easy to understand the proportion of heat, whether it be more or less scorching.

Cold is also determined to be twofold: for there is a constricting cold, more powerful at constricting than the sun is at raising or calling forth vapor. This type of cold prevents all vaporization by constricting the pores of the earth and the surface of the water, so that the sun's ray falling upon them cannot draw anything up. There is also a temperate cold, which indeed restrains the sun so it does not burn, but does not prevent it from calling forth vapor. In the world, the cause of this is the latitude of regions from the path of the sun, according to the distance from the equinoctial toward the southern <note>original: "meridionalem"</note> region. For when the sun is near the winter tropic—that is, in **Sagittarius, Capricorn, or Aquarius**—it is greatly removed from the northern climates. Therefore, the rays of the sun view the inhabitants of those climates only very obliquely; for this reason, cold triumphs in those places, and evaporation is cut off by the contraction of the surfaces of the earth and water. This must be understood proportionally regarding the locations and the causes that produce vapors.

From these divisions of vapors, places, and causes, everything that is to be generated on high or in the depths is determined, once the following classification is made. For everything generated in the depths or on high is generated either from dry vapor only, or moist vapor only, or from both. If it is from dry vapor only, then the material of every such atmospheric phenomenon will be the "vapor of the earth" <note>often referred to as "exhalation" or smoke in Aristotelian physics</note>. Since the earth is a solid element, naturally cold and dry, it retains the heat that enters it more strongly. Because it is solid, it is ignited more easily; because it is dry, the heat becomes a more sharply penetrating force; and because it is dry, it is not easily blunted. Therefore, with this vapor divided through three locations according to whether it is hotter or less hot, there are caused from it the atmospheric phenomena—

<summary>This page explains how the sun's heat and the earth's cold act as "efficient causes" to create or prevent evaporation based on latitude and the zodiac signs. It introduces the distinction between "moist vapor" and "dry vapor" (earthly exhalations) as the primary materials for all atmospheric and geological phenomena.</summary>

<keywords>meteorology, efficient cause, scorching heat, evaporation, latitude, zodiac signs, dry vapor, Aristotelian physics, climate zones</keywords>

# On Meteorology

...at least eleven appearing in the air. And regarding others, if any others appear, the judgment will be similar.

->### On those things generated from dry vapor alone. Chapter III.<-

**F**or this [vapor] is intensely hot and dry: it passes through two regions of the air—namely, the lower and the middle—because the cold of those regions cannot transform <note>original: "conuertere"; here meaning to condense or turn the dry vapor into liquid</note> it. It leaps over to that region which is called the zone of heat <note>term: "estus"; the uppermost part of the atmosphere according to medieval physics, bordering the "sphere of fire"</note> and there it creates five types of phenomena. 

Now, the vapor is either equally subtle <note>fine or thin in consistency</note> in all its parts—making it ready for ignition by the heat of that place—or it is unequal. If it is unequal, it either has many earthy parts at its base that are capable of expansion, or it does not, having only a few. If it has many earthy elements below, then by necessity it will expand there. Moreover, an abundance of earthy material resists being raised to the same level as the other parts. Meanwhile, the fiery heat expands the vapor by thinning it out <note>original: "rarificando"</note>, and the hotter parts continually stretch upward into a point; this makes the shape like a pyramidal column. Because of the great distance from the ground, it will seem to be divided from the upper part, like a plumb line <note>term: "ppendiculum"; a weight (plummet) on a cord used by builders to find a vertical line</note> that has a heavy mass at the bottom and a string extending continuously upward. This substance, ignited in this way by the heat of the place and by fire, is called by philosophers **perpendicular fire**. 

If, however, the vapor is unequally subtle and hot, but is sudden <note>or "thin"</note> and lacks the many earthy elements that could expand for the reason mentioned above, then the whole mass will be ignited for that same reason. It is called a **lance** <note>original: "lancea"</note> because of the long, thin shape that extends itself and stands upright, having a point at the top, just like the perpendicular fire. 

If, on the other hand, the vapor is equally subtle and quite thin, being equally ready for ignition, then it is ignited equally in all its parts. In this case, it is either equally dispersed by the heat in every dimension, and thus it becomes wide and somewhat round, floating on the surface of the air. This is what the common people call a **burning candle** <note>original: "candela accensa"</note>. For although it is wide across its surface, it nevertheless appears round and spherical because of the distance from our eyes. 

Or, the vapor is equally subtle, but it is not equally extensible except in one direction because of the small amount of material—like a horn which, when extended in one direction, is contracted in another. In that case, this ignited vapor is called a **rising Assub** <note>term: "Assub"; a Latinized version of the Arabic word for a shooting star or meteor</note>. 

Or, it is almost equally subtle, but not well-connected or holding together; rather, one part of it is somewhat distant from another, and it appears as one rising Assub after another, as if sparks were flying out of a furnace. 

And these five "impressions" <note>phenomena</note> agree in this: they all fly upward into the fire, and none of them descend downward. But if the vapor is not intensely hot and dry, then it cannot penetrate the middle region of the air, which is very cold <note>medieval scientists believed the middle layer of the air was freezing, which is why clouds turn to snow or hail there</note>. In that case, another five impressions are caused from that vapor. For either that vapor is hot and dry and well-connected, with its parts lying strongly together, or it is scattered. If it is scattered, then because of the heat it possesses, when it is rising in the lower air...

<meta>This page continues the medieval scientific explanation of atmospheric phenomena (known as "impressions"). Having previously discussed how "dry vapors" (exhalations of hot, dry air from the earth) rise, it now describes the specific visual forms these vapors take when they ignite in the upper atmosphere, such as meteors and "flying dragons."</meta>

# Meteorology

It encounters a cold cloud. This coldness causes one part to rub against another, and from this friction, it catches fire <note>In medieval physics, friction between opposing qualities—like hot vapor and cold clouds—was believed to generate fire</note>. Because the parts are scattered, the fire appears divided. And because it flees from the cold cloud it touches, it appears to come out in bursts, just as if lit flax were blown from a tube. 

But if the vapor is quite continuous and is expelled by a touching cold cloud, then the vapor is either compact, bundled, and strong, or it is modest and not very compact. If it is not very compact or large, then it falls continuously; however, it does not divide because of its steady fall. It looks like a long falling star that leaves a trail of its fall behind it; this is called by philosophers a "candle lighting another candle" <note>original: "candela accendens aliam candelam"</note> continuously through its descent. 

If, however, there is a great deal of bundled material, then it takes on three different forms according to the different things it meets in the air. For if it meets nothing, it is ignited by its own friction and will stay in the air for some time; this is what people walking at night say is a "light preceding them" <note>Likely referring to *ignis fatuus* or "will-o'-the-wisp," though here described as an upper-air phenomenon</note>. 

But if it should encounter something in the air, either it is pushed downward from above—which is called by the common people a "falling star" and by philosophers a "descending <term>assub</term>" <note>original: "assub"; an Arabic-derived term used in medieval Latin to describe meteors or shooting stars</note>—or a cold cloud is on one side and heat is on the other. In this case, because of its affinity for the heat, it is pulled toward it and catches fire there. The first part, however, smokes before it ignites; it widens in the middle and bends because of the ignition of one part and the movement of the other side toward moisture and cold. This creates what is like a "flying dragon" <note>original: "draco volans"</note>, because the front part on fire looks like the dragon’s breath. The middle part is bent because it is pulled by the lead part, curving itself like a snake. It always flies toward the side where heat surrounds it, because "like attracts like and opposites repel." 

Furthermore, if the vapor is dry, thin, and moderately flammable, it reaches the middle region of the air but does not penetrate it, and then a third type of <term>assub</term> is generated. However, it is expelled by the cold of that place because many clouds are beneath it. Thus, falling into a cloud, it is hidden, and penetrating the cloud, it appears again. Philosophers call this the "assub that sometimes appears and sometimes does not." Let these things be said regarding the generation of dry vapors, which leave their impressions in the three high regions. Thus, the number of eleven impressions of the air is clear, all of which are generated in the heights from dry vapor.

->### On those things generated from moist vapor only. Chapter IV.<-

Now, however, we must move on to those things that are generated from moist vapor alone, such as dew, frost, snow, rain, and hail—as well as the large drops or droplets that sometimes fall in summer. It must be known that moist vapor, since it is watery, is not flammable because it contradicts fire in two qualities: namely, in its power of cold and its power of moisture. Although it may actually be warm and dry in a temperate way...

<meta>This page marks a transition from a discussion on Meteorology to a treatise on Psychology (De Anima), specifically concerning the nature of the senses and the soul. <header>On the Soul.</header>

...they perceive through [the senses]. Thus, we speak of seeing magnitude, length, width, depth, and brevity <note>Here "brevity" refers to physical smallness or short extent</note>. We also speak of both touching and seeing the motion of a thing, its rest, and whether it is one or two [number]. Others are in a high or low place, which are differences of location; these are perceived both in themselves and by accident. For an object is twofold: principal and secondary. 

Principal objects are multiplied according to the number of the senses. For the object of sight, which is essential, proper, and principal, is color and the differences of colors. The object of hearing is sound and the differences of sounds. The object of smell is odor and the differences of odors. The object of taste is flavor and the differences of flavors. The object of touch consists of the elemental qualities: such as heat, cold, moisture, and dryness. 

Secondary objects, however, are those which do not affect the sense primarily, but rather as a consequence. For example: heat, cold, moisture, and dryness affect the sense of touch primarily, and so they are called principal; however, soft and hard, rare and dense, heavy and light, rough and smooth are perceived as a consequence, and therefore are secondary. 

The reason why there are five senses can be gathered from the different modes of sensing. For sensing happens in two ways: either across a distance, or by application <note>original: "applicationem" – meaning direct physical contact</note>. Likewise, sensing by contact happens in two ways: either the sense is applied to the object along the surface, which is how sensing works in touch; or it is according to depth, which is how it works in taste. Whence a hard, tasteable object is broken and chewed so that the taste of the inner depth of the object may reach the sense. 

Sensing at a distance happens in three ways. It is either in respect to the sense and the object in one direction, and thus sensing happens in sight. Or it is in every direction, and thus sensing happens in hearing. Or it is in several directions, but not every direction, and thus sensing happens in smell.

### On the internal apprehensive powers of the sensitive soul. Chapter 13.

<image-desc>Large decorative red initial 'S' with swirling floral flourishes.</image-desc>

There follows the internal apprehensive powers of the sensitive soul, which are five according to <term>Avicenna</term> <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian philosopher whose "Canon of Medicine" and psychological theories were foundational to medieval European universities</note>. The first is the <term>common sense</term> <note>original: "sensus communis" – This is not "good judgment" in the modern sense, but a faculty that gathers data from all five external senses into one unified experience</note>, or "phantasia." The second is imagination. The third is the imaginative power (also called the "cogitative" or "formative" power). The fourth is the "estimative" power <note>The faculty that allows an animal to perceive "intentions," such as a sheep perceiving a wolf as "harmful" even without prior experience</note>. The fifth is the "memorative" power, or memory. 

The necessity of the common sense is proved by the fact that any particular sense only judges its own object: just as sight judges color and its differences, hearing judges sound and its differences, and so on for the others. But it is clear that we have a common judgment regarding all things. For we judge that a color is not a sound, nor a smell, and so forth. Therefore, it is necessary that we have some internal common faculty of judgment that judges all sensible things, which is called by the philosophers the "common sense." Likewise, there are certain "sensibles" that are perceived by multiple senses and in themselves, such as magnitude, motion, rest, and so on. But it is clear that this does not happen through their own [specific] nature, because according to...

<meta>This page continues the investigation into how the soul perceives the physical world. While the previous page categorized objects of the senses into principal (like color or sound) and secondary (like hardness or weight), this page explores the "Common Sense"—the internal faculty that unifies these different signals into a single experience.</meta>

### On the Soul. ###

...they differ in those ways. However, they do not converge on anything except through the nature of the **common sense** <note>original: *sensus communis*—the internal faculty that unifies different sensory inputs, like seeing and smelling an apple at the same time</note> existing within them or directing them. Therefore, the nature of sensation is twofold: namely, the particular external senses and the common internal sense. 

Furthermore, during sleep, the powers of the external senses are quieted, yet it is certain that they still operate through an internal power. There is, therefore, a single power of internal sensation which is also "common," insofar as the specific senses return to it during sleep and flow from it—as if from a fountain or a guiding principle—when we are awake. 

Likewise, it is clear that we perceive <term>sensory forms</term> <note>The mental images or "shapes" of things we have perceived</note> even when the objects are absent, not just when they are present. Since we clearly do not see these with our external senses, we must see them with an internal sense. It is evident, then, that there is an internal power for grasping sensory information, which is the "common sense." 

When this power is viewed as a part of our nature that does not obey reason, it is called **imagination** <note>original: *fantasia*</note>. Therefore, the common sense is a power of the <term>sensitive soul</term> <note>The part of the soul responsible for feeling and movement, shared by humans and animals</note> located in the first cavity of the brain. It receives into itself all the forms that are impressed upon the five senses and reflects them back. This power is the center of all the specific senses, which branch out from it like limbs from a trunk. 

From the common sense, the **imagination** receives the "species" or "idols" <note>original: *species siue idola*—the mental representations or likenesses of things</note> of objects even when those objects are no longer present. According to **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician and philosopher whose work on the soul deeply influenced Western thought</note>, the common sense has three functions: 

The first function is to reflect upon the actions of the individual senses. This is why a person can say, "I see myself seeing" or "I feel myself feeling." In this mode, it focuses on the object of only one sense at a time. 

The second function is to distinguish between the inputs of different senses. Through this, the mind is able to judge that "this is not that"—for instance, recognizing that "white" (a sight) is not the same as "sweet" (a taste). An individual sense cannot do this on its own because it is limited to its own specific objects. To distinguish between two things, one must first be able to grasp both. 

While these two are its primary functions, according to Avicenna, its third function is to grasp an object in the place where it is now and where it was previously. For example, it perceives a drop falling from a roof to the ground as a single continuous line, even though the drop cannot be at the top and the bottom at the same moment. 

Avicenna calls this the "formal" or "form-giving" sense. Others prefer to call the common sense "formal" because of its specific ability to perceive the **common sensibles**: which are **motion**, **rest**, **number**, **shape**, and **size**.

<summary>This page defines the "Common Sense" as the internal power that unifies the five external senses, allows for self-awareness of perception, and distinguishes between different types of sensations like color and taste. It cites Avicenna to explain how we perceive continuous motion and "common sensibles" like shape and magnitude.</summary>

<meta>Following the diagram of the brain's ventricles on the previous page, this text defines the specific psychological functions—the "internal senses"—located within those chambers. It moves from the "common sense" discussed previously to the faculties of imagination and cogitation.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>
<note>original: *De anima*</note>

### What Imagination Is. Chapter 14 ###

<image-desc>Large decorative rubricated initial 'S' in red ink with white-line interlacing patterns and flourishes extending into the left margin.</image-desc>

The second power—alternatively called a virtue or a faculty—is **imagination**. According to **Avicenna**, <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician and philosopher whose theories on the "internal senses" dominated medieval European thought.</note> it is a power situated at the rear of the front cavity <note>The anterior ventricle of the brain.</note> of the brain. Its role is to retain what the **common sense** <note>original: *sensus communis* — the faculty that gathers and unifies data from the five external senses (sight, hearing, etc.).</note> receives from the five senses; these images remain in the imagination even after the external senses have ceased their work. For the "common sense" requires the physical presence of the object to perceive it, but the **imagination** does not.

He means to say, therefore, that the function of the common sense is to *apprehend* the forms of all sensible things, while the power called imagination is meant to *retain* them. These two are almost like a single power; they do not differ in their object or their location, but only in their mode of action. One receives, the other keeps. That which receives is not the same as that which retains. Furthermore, the power of imagination holds the forms of sensible things but does not judge them—it does not say, "this is white" or "this is black," and so on.

One can see this difference—between receiving and retaining—in **water**. Water has the power to receive the impressions of shapes (like ripples or reflections), yet it lacks the power to retain them. Therefore, there must be a distinct power for receiving sensory data and another for retaining it. Thus, **imagination** is distinct from the **common sense**. This is the opinion of Avicenna. To others, however, it seems that imagination is simply the turning of the internal sensory power toward an image. Both views can be true depending on how one defines the term "imagination."

### What the Imaginative Power Is. Chapter 15 ###

<image-desc>Large decorative rubricated initial 'T' in red ink, styled with a circular flourish and internal white-line patterns.</image-desc>

The third power is called the **imaginative**, the **cogitative**, or the **formative**. According to Avicenna, this is a power situated in the middle cavity <note>The second ventricle.</note> of the brain, which is able to join one thing found in the imagination with another, or to divide them as it wishes.

Avicenna explains this power in this way: we know with absolute certainty that it is in our nature to compose sensory images with one another or divide them, based on agreement, disagreement, or the likeness of the forms we see. It is necessary, then, that there be a power within us that performs these operations.

This power is called **cogitative** <note>original: *cogitatiua* — the power of thought or reflection.</note> insofar as the intellect commands it. However, it is called **imaginative** <note>original: *imaginatiua* — here used in the sense of "fantasy."</note> insofar as it is commanded by the "animal" power <note>The vital spirit or biological soul.</note>. When it is busy composing and dividing, it is called **formative**. It is this power that builds "castles in the air," <note>original: *castra in spera aeris*</note> and creates fantasies like a **chimera** or a wolf with horns.

This power follows the previous ones in order. The common sense passes or gives data to the "formal power"—which is the imagination—to be stored, much like a warehouse for whatever the external senses deliver. The imagination stores and retains these, for it has the power to multiply sensory forms. Then the **cogitative** (or imaginative, or formative) power turns toward the forms stored in the imagination to compose or break them down, since they are subject to it. It performs this composition and resolution often, whether in sleep or while awake, by comparing one perceived thing to another. For example, the **particular sense**... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page explains the medieval psychological functions of the brain, distinguishing between the "common sense" (perception), "imagination" (storage), and the "cogitative power" (the ability to combine or manipulate mental images, such as imagining mythical creatures).</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, imagination, common sense, cogitative power, formative power, faculty psychology, brain ventricles, medieval philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the internal senses, specifically how the imagination and "common sense" can lead to errors in judgment, before transitioning to a new chapter on the nature of sleep and waking states.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...or the **common sense** apprehends the "redness" in copper. The "redness" is a **thing sensed by itself** <note>original: *sensatum per se* — an object's primary quality, like color or sound, which a specific sense is designed to perceive directly.</note>, while the copper itself is a **thing sensed by accident** <note>original: *per accidens* — something perceived indirectly; you see the color "red" (direct), but your mind concludes it is "copper" (indirect/accidental).</note>. Likewise, gold is sensed by accident. Therefore, that which is sensed by itself leads and moves the mind toward that which is sensed by accident. Thus, once the "redness" is perceived, the mind judges it to be gold; turning toward it and compounding the image of the red, it says: "this is gold."

Sometimes there is an error in this, and sometimes not. An error occurs when the mind recalls something that is not actually present beneath that direct sensation which it is currently apprehending. If, however, the thing recalled is truly what lies beneath the direct sensation, there is no error.

This power is called "simple" because it often operates without reasoning. However, it can also operate alongside reasoning, and in those cases, there is sometimes a **fallacy of the accident** or a **fallacy of the consequent**. 

An **accident** <note>In logic, a fallacy where a general rule is applied to an exceptional case, or a property is confused with the essence.</note> occurs when a direct sensation, like "redness," is apprehended and moves the faculty to combine it with "gold." A **consequent** occurs when the "redness" moves the mind to recall not only gold, but also copper. This happens because a direct sensation can be seen to exist in many different things. The **imaginative power** <note>original: *imaginatiua uirtus*</note> composes these various things together and claims one is the other.

There is a difference, then, between this and the preceding type of deception. In the first kind, there was only a recollection of a single subject—the thing sensed by accident—which is not actually present in reality, even though it is present in the way it moves and excites the mind by way of the "redness." In this second kind of deception, however, there is a recollection of *both* subjects: the one that is actually present in reality and moving the senses, and the one that is present only in the mind’s movement. 

For this reason, **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BCE), referred to here as the ultimate authority on natural logic.</note> says that an "accident" pertains to only one thing, while a "consequent" pertains to many. Therefore, I understand this to mean "in several subjects," although some understand "in several consequences." Thus, a **logical error of the accident** <note>original: *paralogius accidẽs*</note> is taken as a single subject across all terms, as is clear when it is said:

*Every bath is artificial.*
*Water is a bath.*
*Therefore, water is artificial.*

In this case, "bath" and "artificial" are the same in the subject. Similarly, "water" and "bath" are the same in the subject. In the "consequent," however, different subjects are always assumed, as is clear to anyone who examines it. 

And note that some call this second power—which moves from one **mental image** <note>original: *fantasmate* — a "phantasm" or appearance held in the mind.</note> to another—the **fantastic power**; however, according to the transformations that occur in dreams, they call it the **imaginative power**.

---

### On Sleep and Waking. Chapter 16. ###

<image-desc>Large decorative red initial 'A' at the start of the section.</image-desc>

Before we speak of the other internal sensitive powers, something must be said about **sleep** and about the **vision** <note>i.e., dreams or mental images.</note> that occurs during sleep. It should be known, therefore, that according to **Avicenna**, **waking** is the state in which the soul commands the senses and the external powers, and moves voluntarily toward action. **Sleep**, however, is the privation of this state, in which the soul turns away from external things toward internal ones. 

Hence it is also defined thus: **Sleep is the rest of the animal powers, accompanied by the intensification of the natural powers.** <note>In medieval science, "animal powers" (from *anima*, soul) refer to thought and movement, while "natural powers" refer to digestion and growth.</note> 

This turning from external to internal things happens for several reasons: either due to weariness, or due to solitude, or due to the "disobedience" of the physical organs. 
* **Due to weariness:** when the **animal spirit** <note>original: *spiritus animalis* — a refined substance believed to flow through the nerves to facilitate sensation and movement.</note> is dissolved or weakened by excessive exercise of the body, it flees to the interior, and the mental powers follow it. 
* **Due to anxiety...** <note>original: *solicitudinẽ* — can also mean intense concentration or mental care.</note>

<summary>This page explains how the mind misidentifies objects (like mistaking copper for gold) through logical fallacies of the imagination, and begins a new chapter defining sleep as the retreat of the "animal spirits" from the external senses to the inner mind.</summary>
<keywords>imagination, common sense, logic, fallacy of the accident, Aristotle, Avicenna, sleep, waking, animal spirits, medieval psychology</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the logic of sensory errors to the physiology and psychology of sleep, continuing the exploration of how the mind processes internal images.</meta>

<header>### On the Soul.</header>

Indeed, it happens that the very cares of many anxieties heat the brain, causing a moist resolution <note>original: *humorosam resolutionem* — a breakdown or release of bodily fluids</note> in that area from which sleep arises, provided that the heating is not so excessive as to dry out the brain. Sleep also happens for another reason: because food and **humors** <note>The four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to control health and temperament.</note> combine within, and they require the **animal spirits** <note>original: *spiritus animalis* — in medieval medicine, a subtle substance that flows through the nerves to enable movement and sensation.</note> to come to them with all their natural heat so that the heat can achieve a perfect digestion. Furthermore, sleep occurs because of the "disobedience" of the bodily organs, when those organs are soaked and blocked by vapors and foods that spread through them, and the animal spirit refuses to move the organs due to the great amount of moisture.

Regarding the **visions** that occur in sleep, we should note the different types according to **Augustine**, who says the following in his book *On the Spirit and the Soul*. Regarding those things that people seem to see while sleeping, there are five kinds: namely, the **Oracle**, the **Vision**, the **Dream**, the **Nightmare**, and the **Phantasm**.

An **Oracle** <note>original: *Oraculum*</note> is when some holy and grave person—such as a priest or even God—appears in sleep and announces what will or will not happen, or what should be done or avoided. A **Vision** <note>original: *Visio*</note> is when someone sees something exactly in the way it later appears and occurs. A **Dream** <note>original: *Somnum*</note> is veiled in figures <note>symbolic imagery</note> and cannot be understood without interpretation. A **Nightmare** <note>original: *Insomnium*</note> is when that which exhausted a person while awake forces itself upon them while they sleep, such as a concern for food or drink, or certain studies, arts, or illnesses. For according to the studies one has practiced, so does one dream; the images of familiar arts appear impressed upon the presence of the mind during sleep. Also, according to the diversity of illnesses, different dreams occur, and according to the diversity of **manners and humors**, dreams also vary. For those of a sanguine temperament see certain things; those of a choleric temperament see others; and those of a melancholic temperament see yet others. The first see varied and red things; the others see white and black things.

A **Phantasm** <note>original: *Fantasma*</note> is when someone has barely begun to sleep and still thinks they are awake; they seem to see forms rushing at them or wandering about, differing and varied, whether joyful or turbulent. In this category is the **phialoes** <note>A term related to the "incubus" or sleep paralysis.</note>, which common belief holds to invade those who are resting, weighing them down and burdening them with its mass. This is nothing other than a certain "smokiness" or vapor ascending from the stomach or heart to the brain, where it oppresses the power of the soul.

Note, therefore, the number of these differences in dreaming, which are categorized according to the ways of seeing: for one sees either the truth or a falsehood. If it is **truth**, it is seen either veiled in figures and hidden, or it is manifest. If it is seen in the first way, it is a **Dream**. If it is seen as manifest, it is either with a sense of awe—which happens from a great imagination of some holy person, and thus is an **Oracle**—or it is known as a future event without such an appearance, and thus is a **Vision**. If, however, what is seen is **falsehood**, either it is a falsehood regarding the thing seen, and thus it is a **Nightmare**, or it is a falsehood regarding the act of seeing, and thus is a **Phantasm**, as when the sleeper thinks they are seeing while they are awake. **Jerome** posits other differences of sleep...

# On the Soul.

...commenting on the book of **Daniel**, saying: There are six manners or kinds of dreams. Two are such as no holy person can avoid: those arising from excessive emptiness of the stomach <note>i.e., hunger</note> and those from over-indulgence. The third is from nocturnal illusions. The fourth is from a prior thought followed by a vision. The fifth is through the revelation of the **Holy Spirit**, which happens in many ways: relating to our head, **Christ**, or to the **Church**, or to its members, with a revelation following. The sixth is from prior cares and worries. 

And note that for the **imaginative power** to work—by the act of composing and resolving images—whether in sleep or in waking, it is necessary for the soul to rest from external action. This is so that the mental images <note>original: *idola* — literally "idols," here meaning the phantom forms or pictures in the mind</note> or the forms <note>original: *species* — the sensory "blueprints" of objects</note> retained in the imagination, or recalled by the power of memory, may appear. This is why those who are always wandering about and immersing their minds in external actions learn the **liberal arts** <note>The traditional core curriculum of medieval education, including grammar, logic, and rhetoric.</note> more slowly. Whence **Aristotle** says in the seventh book of the *Physics*: "By sitting and resting, a person becomes knowing and prudent." 

This is also why, in those who have eaten and drunk well, because of the thick and many vapors ascending to the brain, the imaginative power cannot immediately operate clearly and purely, except weakly and confusedly. This is also why those full of wine do not dream, especially at the beginning of sleep, for the same reason. Later, however, when the fumes are refined and allow the forms to appear, they are able to dream. For the same reason, children do not dream: because they eat much and frequently, and from the milk, many thick vapors ascend to the brain. 

Regarding the operation of the imagination, **Averroes** <note>Ibn Rushd, the 12th-century philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were central to medieval university learning.</note> says that it sometimes happens that in sleep it seems to a person as if they feel through the five senses, without there being any external sensible object present. This happens through a movement contrary to that which occurs in wakefulness. In wakefulness, external sensible objects move the senses, and the **common sense** <note>original: *sensus communis* — the faculty that compiles data from all five senses into a single, coherent perception.</note> moves the imaginative power. In sleep, however, the imaginative power carries the "intention" <note>original: *intentionem* — the mental representation or concept of an object</note> which it received from the outside. Or, when it returns from the power of memory, it moves the common sense, and the common sense moves the particular powers of sense. 

Thus it happens that a person perceives sensible things even though they are not outside, because their "intentions" are in the instruments of the senses; they move them indifferently, whether those intentions come from the outside or from within. Something similar to this happens to those who are fearful or sick because of the intensity of the imagination in those states. For the movement of the imaginative power is intensified in sleep because it is released from the bond of the **cogitative power** <note>The faculty of active, conscious thinking.</note>. Because of the weakness of this cogitative power in the sick and the fearful, such an occurrence happens to them. 

And note that the common sense, because of its habit of receiving from the particular senses, also easily receives returning forms from the memory during sleep. Regarding this, an example is given by authors of a candle that is often lit and often extinguished <note>The text likely refers to how a wick stays hot and easily catches fire again, just as the common sense is "primed" to receive images.</note>...

Which, if it is near another that is lit, easily catches fire. So in the present case: for the **mental image** <note>original: *species* — refers to the sensory "blueprint" or form of an object</note> descends to the **common sense** <note>The *sensus communis*, a faculty where the medievals believed all five external senses were unified.</note>. And because of this, the thing is then believed to be present. If, however, it is asked why it is not similarly directed toward the higher part—namely, **reason**—it must be said that sometimes it is so. For it happens sometimes that good verses are made in sleep, and good **syllogisms** <note>Logical arguments consisting of two premises and a conclusion.</note>, yet rarely; because these do not happen through the composition of one imaginable thing with another; on the contrary, there is a certain **reasoning** <note>original: *ratiocinatio*</note> there. Whence the opinion is that then people do not sleep entirely, but weary nature rests and does not use the senses, yet uses reason concerning those things in which it is occupied.

### On the estimative power: what it is, where it is, and what it does. Chapter 17.

Consequently, we must speak of the **estimative power** <note>The *virtus extimativa* is the faculty that allows an animal to perceive "intentions" that are not visible to the eye, such as danger or utility.</note>. The estimative power is, as **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on the soul were foundational to medieval European philosophy.</note> says: a power situated in the top of the middle cavity of the brain, apprehending the **intentions** <note>original: *intentiones* — the non-physical qualities of an object, such as "threat" or "friendliness."</note> of sensible things, as in a sheep judging that the wolf must be fled from and that it should live with the lamb. It is a transcending power because its apprehension is not of the form of sensible and material things, but of immaterial things: for goodness and ill-will, the fitting and the unfitting, the useful and the harmful, in themselves are not material forms, nor do they fall under the external sense; yet they are accidents of sensible things. Of these is the estimative power, which is the fourth of the internal sensible powers. Note therefore that the estimation of useful and harmful things happens in three ways. 

The first way is from a natural cause <note>original: *causa maternali* — literally "maternal cause," referring to innate or instinctive knowledge.</note>, as happens in a child: who, when lifted up to stand and estimates himself falling, immediately clings to someone; and when someone's eye must be cleaned of **rheum** <note>original: *lipiditate* — likely a transcription error for *lippitudine*, meaning eye discharge or inflammation.</note>, he immediately closes it before he knows what is happening to him. And so the sheep fears the wolf naturally, and animals the lion, and birds the hawk, because they estimate them as harmful. 

The second way is through experience: as happens when an animal has had pain or delight from some thing. The form of which is imprinted in the formal power, or **imagination**, and the **intentions** of utility and harm are stored in memory; as soon as it appears externally, that thing will be judged in the estimation of delights or harm. Hence it is that dogs are frightened by sticks and stones because they estimate them as harmful, and are enticed by bones because they estimate them as delightful. 

The third way is by way of similarity and an associated property, as when a thing has some form joined with the intention of estimation in some sensible thing; as happens in an apple when it is of a scarlet color, which signifies it is ripe and sweet, and therefore when we see a scarlet apple, we estimate it to be sweet.

### On the sensible memorative power. Chapter 18.

The **memorative power** <note>The faculty of memory.</note> is a power situated in the posterior cavity of the brain, retaining what the estimative power apprehends concerning the intentions of sensible things. The comparison of the estimative power to memory, according to **Avicenna**, is such as the comparison of the imagina...

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the "internal senses" of the soul, specifically focusing on the relationship between the estimative power, memory, and the active process of recollection (reminiscence). It draws heavily on Avicenna’s psychological framework.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

[...of the estimative] power to the **common sense** <note>The *sensus communis*, the faculty that unites different sensory inputs into a single perception.</note>: because just as the **imagination** <note>original: *imaginatio*</note> retains and is the storehouse of the **forms** <note>original: *formae* — the visual or structural "blueprints" of objects.</note> of sensible things which the common sense apprehends, so the **memory** <note>original: *memorativa*</note> is the storehouse preserving the **intentions** <note>original: *intentiones* — the non-physical qualities of an object, such as its usefulness or danger, which cannot be "seen" but are perceived by the estimative power.</note> of sensible things which the **estimative power** <note>The faculty that allows animals to judge situations (e.g., a sheep sensing that a wolf is dangerous).</note> apprehends.

However, for a greater clarification of the power of memory, we must ask: What is the difference between **memory** and **reminiscence**? And is recollection or reminiscence found in irrational animals? To this it must be said, according to **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the primary authority for this text's psychology.</note>, that memory exists in other animals; but **reminiscence**—which is the active effort of the mind to seek out what has been forgotten—is found in man alone. For to know that something was once present to oneself which was later erased belongs only to the **rational power**. But if it belongs to any power other than the rational, it could belong to the estimative power, but only insofar as it is decorated and informed by rationality.

The difference, therefore, between memory and reminiscence is that memory is the retention of **species** <note>The mental "images" or "forms" of things.</note> or sensible intentions; but reminiscence is the recovery of forms erased from memory through forgetfulness. This is done through those things which turn the mind back to the act, and similar methods—just as if we have forgotten a person whom we saw, we return in our minds to the place and the time, and we grasp those details through which we remember the person whom we saw in such a place, at such a time, doing such things.

It must also be noted that there is a difference between **recollecting** and **learning**. For some have proposed that learning is nothing other than reminiscing <note>A reference to Plato’s theory of *anamnesis*, the idea that all learning is actually the soul "remembering" knowledge it had before birth.</note>. These two agree in this: that in both there is a movement from the unknown toward knowledge. But they differ in this: that recollection is the seeking of unknown things so they may be known in the present or future, which were also known in the past. **Learning**, however, or instruction, is the extension of the soul to knowing unknown things which were *not* known before.

Furthermore, for greater clarity, it is asked why some men learn things more easily than they can remember them, while for others it is the opposite. And universally, why some find recollection easier than memory, and others the opposite. To understand these things, it must be noted according to Avicenna that those who are of a **dry temperament** <note>original: *siccae complexionis* — In the medieval theory of the four humors, "dryness" was associated with the ability to "fix" or hold an impression, like clay hardening, but it made the mind less flexible for movement.</note> are strong in retaining by memory and weak in recollecting. For dryness is a suitable disposition for the retention of impressions, yet it is unsuited for movement; and therefore a dry temperament suits memory, but not recollection.

Those, however, who are of a **warm temperament** <note>original: *calidae complexionis*</note> recollect easily. For these are the ones who perceive **hints** <note>original: *nutus* — subtle triggers or signs.</note> more readily. Hints work through the movement of sensible things, and he who is more perceptive of hints will be quicker to recollect. Also, warmth of temperament is a most suitable disposition for movement.

Those who are of a **moist temperament** <note>original: *humidae complexionis* — moisture was thought to make the "material" of the brain soft and easy to impress, but also easy to erase.</note> are easy to teach. For moisture is a most suitable disposition for the ease of receiving impressions, which suits easy learning; yet it is the opposite disposition to a strong memory. For it is necessary for memory to be a material from which that which has been impressed is deleted with difficulty; and for this, a dry temperament is needed. Note, therefore, that those with good memories are of a contrary disposition to those who learn well. For those with good memories are those whose souls do not have easy movement, nor do they scatter...

...their thoughts. For that soul which has many and manifold movements and many thoughts does not remember well. For the disposition of good recollection is the opposite: good recollection requires manifold movements and agile thoughts. Likewise, those of a **dry complexion** <term>complexio: the physiological balance of the four "humors" (fluids) in the body which was believed to determine health and personality.</term> are good at remembering, which is a state that is difficult for receiving impressions; and therefore it is the opposite of the state for easy learning <note>The author suggests that a "dry" brain holds onto information well like etched stone, but is harder to "write" on initially.</note>. Good memory, therefore, results either from a dryness of complexion or from focus upon one or a few things, and not many. This is why children, although they are "moist," nevertheless retain things firmly. For their souls are not occupied with many things like the souls of mature persons, but are fixed upon one thing. In young men, however, on account of their heat and their agile movements, the memory is weaker, even if they are of a dry complexion. In the elderly, it happens that on account of the humor <note>In this context, an excess of phlegm or moisture associated with aging.</note> which prevails in them, they do not remember those things which they see.

### On the Comparison of These Powers to One Another. Chapter 9.

And note that of the internal apprehensive powers, some apprehend **sensible forms** <note>original: *formas sensibiles* — the raw data of the senses, like the shape or color of an object.</note>, such as the **common sense**, the **imagination**, and the **imaginative** or **cogitative** power. Some apprehend the **intentions** <note>original: *intentiones* — the non-physical qualities of an object, such as whether it is "dangerous" or "useful," which are perceived by instinct rather than sight or touch.</note> of sensible things, such as the **estimative** and **memorative** powers. 

Furthermore, some both apprehend and act: such as the common sense, the cogitative, and the estimative. Some, however, apprehend but do not act: such as the imagination and memory. Also, some apprehend primarily, and others secondarily. In the apprehension of sensible forms, the **common sense** apprehends primarily, along with the exterior senses; but the power of imagination and the imaginative power apprehend secondarily. Similarly, in the apprehension of sensible intentions, the **estimative power** <note>The faculty that allows an animal to "estimate" a situation, such as a sheep fearing a wolf.</note> apprehends primarily, and the **memorative power** apprehends secondarily. Let these things said concerning the apprehensive powers of the sensitive soul suffice.

### On the Motive Powers of the Sensitive Soul. Chapter 20.

Having spoken of the internal apprehensive powers of the sensitive soul, we must next speak of the **motive powers**. Now, the sensitive motive power operates in two ways. For one is motive by **natural motion**, while the other is by **animal motion**. And by the "sensitive motive power through natural motion," I mean that which does not move according to apprehension, nor is it subject to the command of reason: such as the **vital** or **pulsative power**. This power is in the heart as its organ, and it is the force moving through inspiration and respiration, being the principle for tempering the heat of the heart and the body. 

And note that this force does not belong to every animal, but only to breathing animals that possess lungs. For this kind of force is not in fish, nor in imperfect animals, worms, or turtles. And the reason for this is because ani...

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on memory and the internal senses, explaining why different ages and temperaments remember differently, before transitioning to the "motive powers" like the heartbeat and breathing.</summary>
<keywords>memory, humors, complexion, common sense, imagination, estimative power, motive powers, vital power, respiration, physiology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "complexions" or physiological balances of the body and transitions into the mechanics of biological heat, before beginning a new chapter on the psychology of physical movement.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...a perfect and breathing animal is exceedingly hot. Moreover, this **natural heat** <term>calor naturalis: the internal "fire" believed by medieval scholars to drive digestion and life functions.</term> performs two functions. It breaks down the substance of the body as far as its physical matter is concerned, and it alters the nature of the body from its own quality as far as its accidental properties are concerned. Therefore, in the presence of intense sensible heat, two consequences follow: a loss of substance and a loss of balance. Against these harms, there are two preservative forces: one that restores, and another that regulates. Consequently, two preserving powers are employed: the **nutritive power** <term>virtus nutritiva</term>, which repairs what is lost through heat, and the **pulsative or vital power** <term>virtus pulsativa: the power associated with the heartbeat and the cooling of the "vital spirits."</term>, which regulates the heat. 

Because heat in fish is weak—as is evident by the fact that they survive in a cold element, which is water—and similarly in "imperfect" animals <note>In the Aristotelian tradition, "imperfect" animals usually refers to invertebrates or those that do not breathe air.</note>—which is detected by their slowness of movement and because they do not have the structure of a heart (though they have something proportional to a heart)—it follows that the heat in them does not cause a loss of balance; for this reason, they also lack the pulsative power.

### On the Motive Power of Animal Motion. Chapter 21.

Consequently, we must speak of the **motive power** <term>virtus motiva</term> of animal motion. By "animal motion," I mean that which occurs through a preceding apprehension <note>original: *apprehensionem* — the act of perceiving or noticing something.</note> of good or evil. In an animal, this perception is followed by an **appetite** <term>appetitus: a drive or desire, not necessarily for food, but a general impulse toward or away from an object.</term>, which is either a movement toward something or a movement away from it—that is, flight—as is seen in the perception of good or bad food. It is necessary, therefore, that there be some power other than the sensory-perceptive ones to move the animal. For the powers of perception do nothing but perceive or judge whether something is good or bad, or form an estimation of it. 

Philosophers call this the **appetitive power**, while theologians call it **sensuality** <term>sensualitas: the part of the soul that deals with sensory desires and animal instincts.</term>, which is strictly the power of movement. Furthermore, it is called "sensuality" insofar as it exists in a human being and is ordered by reason; because of this order, sin <note>original: *peccatum*</note> can exist within it—namely, the first movement of venial sin. 

We can generally distinguish between these motive powers. For some are "motive" because they dispose the animal toward movement; these are "motive" in a relative sense, such as the **imagination** <note>original: *fantasia*</note>, the **common sense** <term>sensus communis: the internal faculty that collects data from the five external senses into a single experience.</term>, and the **estimative power** <term>virtus estimativa: the instinct that allows an animal to "estimate" whether something is harmful or helpful, such as a sheep fearing a wolf.</term>. For both the estimative and the imagination move the appetitive power insofar as they represent the "image" <note>original: *speciem*</note> of good or evil, or what is suitable or unsuitable. 

However, the estimative and imagination prepare for movement in different ways. Imagination prepares for movement based on the representation of sensible forms (the physical shapes or colors of things). The estimative power, however, acts based on the presentation of **sensory intentions** <term>intentiones sensibiles: non-physical qualities like "danger," "utility," or "pleasure" that the senses alone cannot see.</term>. We call "sensible forms" things like color, flavor, and such. "Sensory intentions," however, are the useful, the harmful, the suitable, the unsuitable, the pleasant, and the painful. 

The motive powers that are the actual causes producing movement are "motive" in a simple sense; these are subdivided into the **appetitive**, the **effective**, and those that move the limbs to execute the motion. The appetitive powers are those that command the movement. The affective powers are those that produce and execute the internal spiritual movement. The motive powers of the limbs perform the external, bodily movement. Note, therefore, that just as...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s faculties, transitioning from the physical maintenance of the body (nutritive and vital powers) to the psychological drivers of movement and emotion. It draws heavily on the Aristotelian-Avicennian tradition of "faculty psychology," which categorizes human impulses into specific "powers" located in physical organs.</meta>

# On the Soul.

**Avicenna** <note>referring to the 11th-century Persian polymath Ibn Sina, whose psychological theories were foundational to Western scholasticism</note> says: There are two parts of the **appetitive power** <term>virtus appetitiva: the soul's faculty for desiring or avoiding things based on what it perceives.</term>: the **concupiscible power** and the **irascible power**.

The **concupiscible power** <term>vis concupiscibilis: the "desiring" faculty, focused on seeking pleasure and physical needs.</term> is the power that commands one to move or to approach those things which are thought to be necessary or useful, driven by an appetite for pleasure.

The **irascible power** <term>vis irascibilis: the "spirited" or "fighting" faculty, focused on overcoming obstacles to achieve a good or avoid an evil.</term>, however, is that which commands one to move in order to repel from oneself what is thought to be harmful or destructive, driven by an appetite for victory <note>original: "appetitu vincendi," suggesting a drive for mastery or overcoming resistance.</note>.

To understand the difference between the irascible and concupiscible powers, note that "**the good**" is spoken of in two ways: namely, the **simple good** and the **expedient good**. 

The **simple good** is here called the "delightful," such as food. The **expedient good** is here called the "arduous and difficult," which is nonetheless called good because it is useful, such as medicine or a **surgical incision** <note>original: "incisura"</note> for the sake of the body's health. Therefore, the concupiscible power is the appetite for the delightful good—specifically the delightful good of the flesh and the temporal world—while the irascible power is the appetite for the **arduous, expedient good**.

And it should be known that since the concupiscible power is directed toward the delightful good, it possesses these acts: **Lusting** <note>original: "concupiscere"</note>, **desiring**, **rejoicing**, **being glad**, **loving**, and **cherishing**. And since opposites are naturally suited to occur [even] regarding God, there are acts within this same concupiscible power that are contrary to these, such as: **loathing** (which is the opposite of lusting), **abominating** (the opposite of desiring), **grieving** (the opposite of rejoicing), **being sorrowful** (which is contrary to being glad), **hating** (contrary to loving), and **contemning** (contrary to cherishing).

The acts of the irascible power, since they concern the arduous and the difficult, exist according to a twofold disposition: namely, **weakness** and **strengthening**.

**Weakness** is the disposition that follows from the "discomforting" <note>original: "disconfortatione," a loss of vital strength or courage</note> of the irascible power. **Strengthening** is the disposition that follows its "comforting" or fortification. 

According to **strengthening**, acts occur by the command of the irascible power. Some are directed toward a good; others against an evil. 
*   **Toward a good:** not according to just any reason, but according to the reason of excellence or honor; according to this, the acts of the irascible power are: **ambition**, **hope**, **pride**, **contempt** [of obstacles], and **dominance**. 
*   **Against an evil:** the acts according to the strengthening of the irascible power are: **daring**, **anger**, and **rising up**.

According to **weakness** or "discomforting," movements are made by the irascible power by way of flight <note>original: "per modum fuge," meaning avoidance or psychological retreat.</note>. And this happens in two ways: for some are movements of flight from a good, and others are flight from an evil. 
*   Movements of **flight from a good** are not concerning just any good, but the **arduous** or excellent good. According to this, the movements are: **poverty of spirit**, **despair**, **humiliation**, and **reverence**. 
*   By way of **flight from an evil**, the acts according to the weakness of the irascible power are: **penance** <note>original: "penitentia," here meaning regret or remorse</note>, **impatience**, and **fear**.

The **organ** of the irascible and concupiscible powers is the **heart**. For just as the brain is the principal organ of the sensitive apprehensive power <note>the faculty of perceiving through the five senses</note>, so the heart is the organ of the **motive power**, as the philosophers say. 

A sign of this is the **dilation** and **restriction** of the heart, and the recall of blood and **spirits** <term>spirituum: the subtle, vaporous fluids believed to carry life and sensation through the body.</term> from the exterior parts to the interior, or their emission from the interior to the exterior, during the movements of the concupiscible and irascible powers. When...

It identifies the heart as the physical seat of these emotional movements, evidenced by the physical expansion and contraction of the organ.</summary>

-># On the Soul.<-
->f iii<-

...for these movements are produced by a strengthened power: then there occurs a dilation of the heart and an emission of blood and **vital spirits** <note>original: "spuum," abbreviation for "spirituum." In medieval medicine, "spirits" were subtle vapors that carried life and sensation through the body.</note> from the heart to the exterior parts. This happens slowly when the **concupiscible power** <term>the faculty of desire for pleasure</term> is strengthened, but impetuously when the **irascible power** <term>the faculty of spirited anger or aggression</term> is strengthened. Conversely, when these powers are weakened, there is a constriction of the heart and a recall of the spirits. If the concupiscible power is weakened, this happens slowly; but if the irascible power is weakened, it happens impetuously. From these different movements of the concupiscible and irascible powers, it is understood that the organ of the **motive power** is the heart.

### On the affective powers. Chapter xxii.

<image-desc>Ornamental red initial letter 'D' with fine penwork flourishing inside and around the character.</image-desc>Regarding the **affective powers**, it must be understood that they are perhaps the same in substance as the appetitive powers, but they differ in their definition, as **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina, the Persian philosopher whose work "De Anima" heavily influenced this text.</note> says. For insofar as the appetitive powers are directed toward an object, they become affective or "desiring" powers. It should be known, therefore, that **appetite** is a movement commanded by the irascible and concupiscible faculties. **Affect**, however, is an internal movement that follows the apprehension of good and evil. 

Hence, the affections are multiplied according to four differences, according to both the saints and the philosophers: namely, **joy** or gladness; **sorrow** or sadness; **desire** or hope; and **dread** or fear. Their number is determined thus: two arise from the comprehension of a **present good** (joy or gladness) and a **future good** (desire or hope). Two arise from the comprehension of a **present evil** (sorrow or sadness) and a **future evil** (dread or fear).

### On the external motive power. Chapter xxiii.

<image-desc>Ornamental red initial letter 'C' with fine penwork flourishing inside and around the character.</image-desc>Consequently, we must speak of the **external motive power** which follows the affective powers. This power is infused into the muscles and fibers, contracting the ligaments joined to the limbs and relaxing them. It is subdivided into the **general motive power** and the **particular motive power**. 

The general motive power is rooted in the back part of the **brain** and the head, from which the motor nerves arise, just as the sensory nerves arise from the front part. These motor nerves branch out through the whole body and reside in the fibers and muscles that are inserted into the limbs; these serve as the organs for the general motive power of the body. 

The **particular motive power** is further subdivided into the **contractive**, the **progressive**, the **operative**, and the **vocal**. I call "contractive" that which is the principle of the movement of contracting and extending the limbs of an animal, which does not result in the movement of the whole animal from place to place. For as Avicenna says, animal movement is twofold: there is the movement of the animal from place to place, and there is the movement of contracting and extending the animal's limbs. It is impossible for an animal to have the sense of touch and not have within itself the power of movement in some way. For how would it be known to have the sense of touch unless one could see in it some movement of fleeing from a contrary thing? From this, one can understand that these are indeed animals, just as we see in **shellfish and snails** <note>original: "conchilibus et conchis"</note> that there are movements of constriction, pulling back, and extension in their parts, although...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the motive powers of the soul, moving from the internal impulses (desire and anger) described on the previous page to the external physical movements they command, and begins a new inquiry into the rational soul.</meta>

-># On the Soul.<-

...they do not depart from their place: and in this we recognize that they feel and are **animals**. The **progressive power** <term>locomotive faculty; the ability to move from place to place</term> is the sensory principle of traveling from one place to another. The organ of this power is different, according to the diversity of the animals. For some walk with feet, as **walking creatures** <note>original: "gressibilia"</note>. Some are carried by wings in flight, as **flying creatures** <note>original: "uolatilia"</note>. Some are pulled along by their ribs and bellies by crawling, as **reptiles** <note>original: "reptilia"</note>. And some are driven by fins in swimming, as **swimming creatures** <note>original: "natalia"</note>. Furthermore, the **operative power** is the sensory principle of working, as in those having a hand. The organs of this power are the hands. In those not having hands, however, there is something in place of hands, such as the mouth or the feet, as seen in bees and spiders. The **vocal power** is the principle of emitting voice. This is not the same as the **pulsating power** <term>the heartbeat</term>. For the pulsating power moves always and by necessity, not according to mental perception; it moves by a certain motion according to the dilation and constriction of the heart, by breathing in and breathing out. The vocal power, however, is not always active by necessity, but voluntarily and according to perception. This is the power that indicates affections, according to what **the Philosopher** <note>Aristotle, often referred to simply as "The Philosopher" in medieval texts.</note> says: that "voices are signs of those passions which are in the soul." The organ of this power is the lung and the **vital artery** <note>original: "uitalis artaria." In this period, the trachea was often called the 'rough artery' and thought to carry vital air.</note>. Thus, only those animals possessing a lung emit voices. Let these things said regarding the powers of the soul common to us, beasts, and plants, suffice.

## On the virtues or powers of the rational soul. Chapter xxiv.

There follows the discussion of the powers of the **rational soul** <term>the human soul, capable of reason</term> and things human. This is first divided into the **apprehensive** and **motive** powers, or into the **speculative** and **practical intellect**. But because the knowledge of powers comes from the knowledge of their organs, their objects, and their acts, we must first speak of the organ. Second, of the object. Third, of the distinction of the intellectual power, where its acts will also be touched upon.

## Whether the intellectual power has a specific determined organ in the body. Chapter xxv.

Someone might ask at the beginning about the organ of the intellectual power: that is, in what part of the body it exists and operates. For if it operates in no part of the body, it does not seem to be in the body. But if it operates in some part of the body, it will have some part in the body as its own determined organ. But against this: every power operating through an organ operates according to the property and the capacity of that organ only. For instance, the power of sight, which operates through an organ (the eye), operates according to the property and capacity of that organ. Thus, it does not judge flavor or sound, but color, because color pertains to the nature of the organ of sight alone, not flavor or sound. However, no physical organ has the capacity or disposition for **incorporeal things** <note>abstract concepts or spiritual realities that have no physical body.</note>. Therefore, no power operating or knowing through a physical organ is capable of knowing anything except physical things. If, therefore, the intellectual power were to understand through a physical organ, it would understand only physical things...

<summary>This page concludes the study of physical powers shared by all animals, such as locomotion and voice, before transitioning to the unique properties of the human rational soul and the philosophical debate over its immateriality.</summary>
