--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul's faculties, shifting focus from the physical and sensory powers discussed previously to the unique nature of the intellective or rational power.</meta>

-># On the Soul.<-

...and are not spiritual things. Likewise, the operation of the **intellective power** <term>the faculty of understanding and reason</term> is always through abstraction from matter and from the conditions of matter; but every operation is according to the nature of the power from which it proceeds. Therefore, the intellective power is abstractive from matter; consequently, it has no assigned corporal and material organ in the body.

Furthermore, no power that is "incorporated"—that is, restricted to a specific part of the body—or operating through a physical organ, is capable of knowing itself. This is because it cannot reflect back upon itself since it is bound to matter. For this reason, the eye does not see itself, nor does the imagination imagine itself. Since, therefore, the intellective power is self-knowing—for it understands itself when it reflects upon itself—it is neither incorporated nor operating through a physical organ.

Furthermore, everything that is known or understood exists in the knower according to the nature of the knower, and not according to the nature of the thing known, as is evident in all things generally. Everything that is received is in the receiver according to the nature of the receiver, and not according to the nature of the thing received <note>original: "Omne quod recipitur est in recipiente secundum naturam recipientis." This is a fundamental principle of Medieval philosophy: the mind processes information based on its own capacity, not just the raw data of the object.</note>. Since, therefore, corporal and material things exist in the intellect in an immaterial way, the intellect must be immaterial, and therefore simple <note>In this context, "simple" means not composed of parts, unlike physical matter.</note>.

Likewise, **Aristotle** <note>original: "Arz."</note> says in the third book of *On Animals*: "The intellect and intelligence come from without, and it alone is divine, because its operation has no communication with corporal operation in any way." Likewise, in the third book of *On the Soul*: The sense cannot perceive after an intense sensation; for instance, the sense of hearing is dulled by loud sounds, nor can the eyes see or the nose smell in the presence of the strongest colors or odors. But the intellect, when it understands a "greatly intelligible" <note>An exceptionally profound or complex concept.</note> thing, does not understand lower things any less, but even more so. Moreover, the sensory faculty does not exist without the body, but the intellect is separate; therefore, the intellect is immaterial and simple. This must be granted. It must be said, therefore, that the intellective power is not in the body in such a way that it is restricted to a part of the body, because it is the "act" or "perfection" of no specific part of the body—unlike the visual power of the eye or the auditory power of the ear, etc. Rather, it is whole within the whole body; this power is perfect, as is clear from what has been said.

### On the object of the intellective or rational power. Chapter 26.

Following this is the discussion of the object of the intellective power. And since the power itself is immaterial, its object will be immaterial; that is, an "intelligible form," namely, one abstracted from matter and the conditions of matter. Regarding **species** <term>mental representations or concepts</term> or abstracted forms: some are abstracted by their own nature, such as all spiritual things. Others, however, are abstracted through the action of the intellective power itself—that is, through mental consideration, not in physical reality—such as the species or likenesses by which physical things are known. Those forms abstracted from matter and all conditions of matter by their own nature are called "innate." Such is the form by which God is known, and the angel, and the soul itself, and those things which are in the soul as in a subject...

It concludes by defining the objects of thought as immaterial forms, some naturally spiritual and others abstracted from the physical world by the mind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on how the soul understands different types of reality, moving from the way it knows spiritual beings (God and angels) to the complex process of "abstraction," by which it understands the physical world. The word "cto" at the beginning completes the word "abstracto" (abstractly) from the preceding page.</meta>

-># On the Soul.<-

...abstractly: such as the powers, sciences, and virtues. But the form through which God is known is a likeness or image of the **First Truth** impressed upon the soul by the Creator. For this reason, <note>original: "Ioannes Damascenus"</note> John of Damascus says that the knowledge of God’s existence is implanted in all beings by nature itself; for the image of the First Truth impressed upon the soul leads it toward the knowledge of Him whose image it is. 

However, the form by which the soul itself or an angel is known is the rational soul itself. When the soul uses itself as a likeness—referring back to that which it was made to imitate (the reality above it)—it knows God in an acquired manner. Or, it uses itself as a likeness by referring to another whom it was not made to imitate but with whom it shares an imaginative likeness; in this way, it knows an angel as its peer <note>literally "beside itself"</note>. 

And this is what <note>original: "Aug."</note> Augustine says: "The soul knows God above itself, itself in itself, and the angel alongside itself." To know those things that are within it—such as its powers, virtues, and sciences—it uses these as likenesses to know them. Hence, the thing being known and the "form" through which it is known differ only in definition <term>logical distinction</term> and not in physical reality. 

Now, forms that are "abstracted through consideration" are the forms by which bodily things and those things grounded in bodies are known. Since the nature of the intellect is superior to bodily things, it possesses a power to comprehend bodily forms in a wondrous way. For it **abstracts** them: first from the senses, then from the imagination and all material conditions—namely, shape, position, and the like. Thus, having stripped away all conditions of matter and individual substance, it receives them as abstract and universal concepts, common and immaterial, such as **genera**, **species**, **differences**, **properties**, and **accidents** <term>the categories used to classify all knowledge in the Middle Ages</term>. 

This abstraction does not happen by a physical act, but through mental consideration. According to <note>original: "Auice."</note> Avicenna, the order of abstraction for a bodily form should be viewed in this way: 

The **exterior sense**, such as sight, does not receive the form as it exists in matter, but rather its likeness; yet it only comprehends it when the physical matter is present or the form exists within that matter. 

The **interior sense**, or imagination, abstracts the form with a greater degree of abstraction because it apprehends it even when the physical matter is absent. Nevertheless, the imagination does not divide the form itself from the "accidents" of matter—such as its specific shape, location, and the like—under which it comprehends it. 

**Estimation** <term>the faculty that senses non-physical qualities, like a sheep "estimating" that a wolf is dangerous</term>, however, transcends that level of abstraction. It comprehends forms that are the "intentions" <term>perceived meanings or qualities</term> of sensible things, which do not have a direct physical likeness to material forms—such as goodness, malice, the fitting, or the unfitting. Yet, estimation does not grasp these forms entirely stripped of all material accidents, because it apprehends them as particulars, according to their own nature, and in comparison to the imagined sensory forms, as is clear from what was said before. 

The **intellective power**, however, apprehends the bodily form and strips it from matter, from all circumstances of matter, and from individuality itself; thus it apprehends the form as naked, simple, and universal. For example, it apprehends "man" as a concept that is predicated of many as one common nature...

<summary>This page explains how the soul acquires knowledge: it knows God and itself through an internal divine image, but knows the physical world through a step-by-step process of "abstraction" that strips away material details to find universal truths.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the human mind understands the physical world by "abstracting" or stripping away physical details to reach the pure essence of a thing. The first word completes "abstrahit" (abstracts) from the previous page.</meta>

# On the Soul.

...the intellect abstracts it <note>the form or essence of a thing</note> from every quality, position, location, and singularity. For unless it were stripped bare in this way by mental consideration, it could not be understood as a "common" concept that could be applied to all individuals. This, then, is the difference in the levels of bodily abstraction: First in the **senses**; second in the **imagination**; third in the **estimative power** <note>the faculty that perceives "intentions" not found in the senses, like a sheep sensing a wolf is dangerous</note>; and fourth in the **intellect**.

Note, however, according to **Avicenna** <note>original: "Avice."; refers to the Persian philosopher Ibn Sina, a major influence on medieval thought</note>, that many "dispositions" happen to material forms because of the matter they are joined to, which they do not possess by their own essence—that is, simply by virtue of being forms. These include things like being a particular individual, or having a specific quality, quantity, or position. For example, the "human form" is a substance and a nature in which all individual members of the human species agree equally, and it has a single definition. Yet, because this form is received into an individual—into this person or that person—its existence is multiplied. This multiplication does not belong to the form by its own nature. 

For if it were part of "human nature" itself to be multiplied, then the term "human" could not be spoken of except in reference to a specific individual <note>literally "a single number"</note>. If the humanity of this person existed solely because it is humanity, it could not belong to anyone else. It follows, therefore, that this multiplication and division into individuals happens to humanity because of its relationship to **matter**. 

Besides this, something else happens: when a form is in matter, it acquires certain modes of quality, quantity, position, and place, all of which are distinct from the nature of the form itself. For if humanity, simply by being humanity, determined these <term>accidents</term> <note>non-essential characteristics like height or hair color</note> for itself, then every human would have to be identical to every other in these features. Since they all share in humanity, they would all be of the same quality, size, and so on. It follows, then, that the human form does not have these traits from its own essence, but they happen to it because of its composition with matter. Therefore, when the form is understood without these traits, that is the truest understanding of the form itself.

Note also that the difference in how we understand things arises from the level of abstraction within the forms themselves. 
*   **First**, some forms are simply abstracted from matter and from all conditions of matter; these are spiritual forms, which philosophers call **metaphysical**. 
*   **Second**, some forms are abstracted from matter but *not* from the "conditions" of matter <note>such as shape or number</note>, and are therefore only partially abstracted. These are **mathematical** forms, such as magnitude and number. For a line or a geometric figure does not require "golden" matter or "wooden" matter to be what it is, yet it does require a specific position and arrangement; thus, they are not abstracted from the conditions of matter in their own right. Similarly, a number does not require specific physical things (like two or three objects), yet it involves multiplication or division, which are conditions of matter, as has been shown. 
*   **Third**, certain forms are "unabstracted" in themselves, such as **physical** or natural forms (like fire, earth, flesh, etc.); these are only abstracted through mental consideration, following the order mentioned before, by separating them from matter and its conditions. 

The first type of forms <note>metaphysical</note> are understood only by intellectual vision. <note>Large initial "I" is missing or faded</note> [I]n fact, that vision, as **Aristotle** <note>original: "Ari."</note> says, comprehends things that have no images other than themselves. The second type of forms <note>mathematical</note>, however, are understood...

<summary>This page explains the process of "abstraction," where the intellect understands the true essence of a thing (like "humanity") by mentally stripping away individual traits caused by matter. It categorizes knowledge into three levels: metaphysical (spiritual), mathematical (shapes and numbers), and physical (natural elements).</summary>
<keywords>abstraction, Avicenna, Aristotle, matter, form, accidents, metaphysics, mathematics, physics, intellect</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sensory abstraction. The previous page ended with "th..." which likely leads into "they are apprehended" (apprehenduntur) at the start of this page.</meta>

...they are apprehended through imaginary vision, which is the **common sense** <note>the faculty that combines input from the five senses into a single experience</note>, or an interior power. Through abstraction from the conditions of matter—such as position, site, and the like—they are apprehended by intellectual vision, which belongs to the intellectual power. The third kind of forms, however, are grasped by bodily vision, which is the particular or exterior sense; and through abstraction from matter, they are apprehended by imaginary vision. Then, through a fuller abstraction from all conditions of matter, they are apprehended by intellectual vision.

## On the distinction of the apprehensive intellectual power. Chapter 27.

Next, we must speak of the distinction of the apprehensive intellectual power. It must be known, therefore, that there is a **passive intellect** <note>original: "intellectus passiuus"</note>, which is corruptible and is called "material" by **Aristotle** <note>original: "Ari."</note>. And there is an incorruptible and separable intellect. We will speak of these in this order: first, concerning the passive intellect and its acts; second, concerning the separable intellect and its acts. 

The passive intellect, therefore, is the lower force of the intellectual part joined to the sensible part; it receives the **intelligible forms** <note>original: "species intelligibiles"; the mental "blueprints" or essences of things</note> within the **phantasms** <note>original: "fantasmatibus"; mental images derived from the senses</note>. It was necessary for this power to exist so that the separable intellect might understand the forms abstracted from those phantasms. And this power is what connects the acts of the intellectual power with the acts of the sensible power; for it brings from the phantasms the intelligible forms that are to be abstracted in a material way. This power, moreover, never belongs to the **rational soul** <note>original: "animae rationalis"</note> except while it is joined to the body. It is also called by another name, "rationality"—from which the middle cell of the brain is called "logistic" or "rational" <note>Medieval medicine believed the brain was divided into three "cells" or ventricles; the middle one was thought to be the seat of logic</note>. It is called rationality or reason because it is able to take universals from particulars, although not the universal through the mode of the universal.

## On the separable intellectual power. Chapter 28.

Differences in the **separable intellect** are assigned according to two modes. One mode is according to the difference of nature, by which the intellectual power is distinguished into the **possible intellect** <note>the mind's capacity to receive knowledge</note> or the **agent intellect** <note>the mind's active power to create knowledge</note>. 

The other mode is according to the difference of order, and according to this, there are two distinctions of the intellectual power: namely, the **superior** and **inferior intellect**, or the superior and inferior reason. Regarding the first division: note that the nature of the intellect is twofold: one is like matter, the other like form. One is "possible," like a **blank tablet** <note>original: "tabula nuda"; often called a "tabula rasa," a clean slate upon which nothing is yet written</note> without any picture—that is, capable of receiving all pictures, having none in actuality, but capable of all; and this is the [possible] intellect. The other is "agent," like light. For there is an intelligible light of the primary power impressed upon us by nature, always active like a light always shining; of which the Psalm says: *The light of thy countenance, O Lord, is signed upon us* <note>Psalm 4:6</note>. And it relates to manifesting the intelligible forms just as light relates to colors, as the philosophers say.

<summary>This page defines the different "powers" of the human mind, distinguishing between the passive intellect, which deals with sensory images (phantasms), and the active or agent intellect, which acts as a "light" to reveal abstract truths. It uses the famous "blank slate" (tabula nuda) analogy to describe the mind's potential for learning.</summary>
<keywords>passive intellect, agent intellect, rational soul, phantasms, abstraction, Aristotle, tabula rasa, common sense, brain anatomy, medieval psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the intellectual powers of the soul, moving from the passive intellect discussed on the previous page to a detailed classification of the "possible" and "active" intellects, drawing heavily on the Aristotelian tradition via Avicenna and Saint Augustine.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

## On the possible intellect. Chapter 29.

Concerning the **possible intellect** <note>original: "intellectus possibilis"; the capacity of the human mind to receive and store knowledge</note>, it is certain that it exists. For unless there were a possibility for receiving **intelligible species** <note>original: "species intelligibiles"; the mental "blueprints" or essences of things</note>, it would be impossible for the intellect to exist without always actually thinking. Therefore, we must now examine its different states. It is clear that the potential for understanding exists in children, in adults who are learning, and in educated adults who are not currently thinking about what they know, as well as in those who are actively thinking—though it exists in each in a different way. 

Just as the power of writing exists in a child, in an adult learning to write, in a writer who has mastered the art but is not currently writing, and in a writer in the act of writing. 
1. In the first, there is **natural power** <note>original: "potentia naturalis"</note>. 
2. In the second, there is **disposed power** <note>original: "potentia disposita"</note>. 
3. In the third, there is **perfected power in habit** <note>original: "potentia perfecta in habitu"; knowledge held in the memory but not currently being used</note>. 
4. In the fourth, there is **power in habit and in use**. 

According to this model, there are four different types of possible intellect according to **Avicenna** <note>an influential Persian philosopher (Ibn Sina) whose works on the soul were foundational for medieval European scholars</note> and others. 
*   The first is the intellect that has only the possibility of knowing; it is like **material potential** <note>original: "potentia materialis"</note> similar to **prime matter** <note>original: "materie prime"; the most basic, formless substance that has the potential to become anything</note>, which has no form of its own but is the subject for all forms. 
*   The second is the **disposed intellect** (or an intellect having a disposition), which is when someone holds "principles" in their mind—that is, propositions that are believed not because they were learned elsewhere, but because they are self-evidently known. For example: "every whole is greater than its part," or "if you take equals from equals, what remains are equal." 
*   The third is the **perfected intellect**, when the intellect already possesses the conclusions of those things that follow from the principles, but it is not currently turned toward them through active consideration. 
*   The fourth is the **intellect in act**, when it is already actively considering. 

The first is called the **material intellect**. The second is called the **intellect in habit**. The third is called the **acquired intellect** <note>original: "intellectus adeptus"</note>. The fourth is called the **accommodated intellect** <note>original: "intellectus acomodatus"</note>. These, indeed, are the levels of the speculative possible intellect.

## On the active intellect. Chapter 30.

Concerning the **active intellect** <note>original: "intellectus agens"; the "light" of the mind that makes potential thoughts become actual thoughts</note>, Avicenna proves its existence in this way: The human soul is an intellect first in potentiality, and afterwards it is an intellect in effect. However, nothing moves from potentiality to effect except through a cause that leads it from potentiality to effect. But the cause that gives the **intelligible form** is nothing other than an "intelligence in effect"; therefore, an active intellect necessarily exists. 

Now we must see whether the active intellect is separate from the substance of the soul or if it is a specific power within the soul. And if it is separate, we must see whether it is a **created intelligence** (which is an angel) or **uncreated** (which is God). 

That it is the uncreated intelligence, which is God, is proven by **Augustine** in his book of *Soliloquies*. He says that just as we notice three things in this material sun—that it shines, and that it illuminates—so in the most serene God we must understand three things: that He understands, and that He makes all other things understand. Thus, God is the intellect that makes things understood; therefore, He is the active intellect. Likewise, in the first chapter of **John**: *He was the true light that enlightens every man,* etc. <note>John 1:9</note>

<summary>This page defines the "possible intellect" and "active intellect," explaining how the human mind moves from a blank slate to active thought through four developmental stages and the illuminating power of a higher cause, identified here as God.</summary>
<keywords>possible intellect, active intellect, Avicenna, Augustine, material intellect, acquired intellect, potentiality, actuality, intelligible species, cognition</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the intellect, transitioning from the definition of the "possible intellect" to a debate over the "active intellect." It explores whether this light of understanding comes from God, from angels, or is a natural part of the human soul itself.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

This illumination, however, is not for knowing things perceived by the senses, but for knowing **intelligible things** <note>original: "intelligibilia"; abstract concepts or essences that the mind understands rather than what the eyes see</note>. Therefore, the Son of God will be the light that makes these intelligible things manifest; thus, He is the **active intellect** <note>original: "intellectus agens"; the power of the mind that "activates" knowledge by abstracting universal truths from sensory experiences</note>. 

However, some argue that the active intellect is a created intelligence, namely an **angel**. This is proven by the words of **Blessed Dionysius** <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential early Christian theologian whose writings on the celestial hierarchy shaped medieval views on angels</note>, who says: "All angels are revealers." Furthermore, all revelations pertain to them, as is evident from the Apostle in the first chapter of **Revelation** and in **Daniel** chapter 8. But it is certain that this kind of manifestation, which occurs through angels, is not made to the sensory power. It must, therefore, be made to the intellectual power, and this occurs only through **irradiation** <note>original: "irradiationem"; a "beaming down" of spiritual light or influence</note>. This is why they are called "angels of light," a form into which Satan sometimes transforms himself. Therefore, the angelic intelligence is the active intellect.

Likewise, the light of the First Intelligence <note>God</note> is "inaccessible," according to the first letter to **Timothy**, chapter 6: "God dwells in inaccessible light." Therefore, it will not be through that light that we understand. It is necessary, then, for the "seer" and the "light" to be proportional to one another. Thus, the divine intellect will not be the active intellect in relation to the human soul. But since there is a proportion between the light of an angelic intelligence and the vision of the human intellect—and vision occurs in a proportional light—it follows that the angelic intelligence will be the active intellect in relation to the human **possible intellect** <note>the capacity of the human mind to receive knowledge</note>.

On the other hand, it is argued that the active intellect is not separate from the substance of the soul, but is rather a specific capacity of the intellectual part. This is proven by **Psalm 4**: "The light of your countenance, O Lord, is signed upon us." Therefore, in the highest part of the soul, there is a "signing" of the light of the divine likeness. We possess, therefore, a light impressed upon us by the Creator for the purpose of knowing intelligible things; and this is what we call the active intellect. 

Furthermore, since there are physical eyes that see through a light existing within themselves—such as the eye of a **cat** <note>original: "chaci," likely a variant of "catus"; medieval thinkers believed certain animals saw at night because their eyes emitted their own internal light</note> and the eye of an **owl** which sees in the dark and does not need external light—then all the more should the intellectual eye see through a light existing within itself. Therefore, the light by which we understand will not be separate from the substance of the soul.

I respond without prejudice <note>a standard scholarly disclaimer meaning the author is offering a reasoned opinion rather than a final dogma</note>: There are certain things which, as **Augustine** says, are above the human intellect; some are beside it; some are within it; and some are below it. For he says that the soul knows God **above** itself, angels **beside** itself, itself **within** itself, and whatever is contained in the circuit of the heavens **below** itself. 

It must be said, therefore, that in order to understand those things which are above it—such as the things understood about the divine essence and the Trinity of persons in a divine manner—the soul requires an irradiation from that very light of the First Truth and Essence upon its highest power, which is called the **mind** or **intelligence**. Regarding this, Augustine says that no nature stands between it and the First Truth which forms it. Thus, in respect to the Supreme Truth and those intelligible things that entirely exceed the human intellect, God is called the "active" power. This kind of illumination is an "infusion of grace" for the contemplation of divine things. 

However, to know those things which are beside it—such as the angelic essences, their powers, orders, and operations—the soul requires angelic revelation or instruction. In this operation, an Angel could be called the "active intellect" insofar as it instructs the human intellect. But this does not concern the highest "face" of the human intellect, which is eternal...

<summary>This page debates whether the "active intellect" (the power that enables human understanding) is God, an angel, or a natural part of the human soul. The author concludes that the source of illumination depends on the subject being studied: God illuminates divine mysteries, while angels assist in understanding celestial hierarchies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the source of intellectual light, concluding the previous argument that this light is not merely external but involves the internal powers of the soul. It transitions into a specific explanation of how the human mind processes information, drawing heavily on the theories of St. Augustine and the Persian philosopher Avicenna.</meta>

<header>### On the Soul.</header>

...of the eternal and uncreated: but rather an inferior power, according to how **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), whose "divine illumination" theory was central to medieval psychology</note> distinguishes between intelligence, intellect, and reason. **Intelligence** is the uncreated spirit <note>original: "spiritus increata"; referring to God</note>. **Intellect** belongs to the spiritual creature <note>Angels or the human soul</note>. **Reason** belongs to the study of bodily natures. 

To know those things which are within the soul—such as the soul itself, its powers, its habits, its branches of knowledge, and its virtues—the soul does not need an external light; instead, it knows them by an **innate light**. For it knows that it exists, that it can reason, that it feels, that it knows something, and whether a thing is just or unjust, by **turning toward itself**. And for this reason, Augustine says that the soul knows itself within itself. 

To know those things that are below itself <note>physical objects in the world</note>, the soul does not need the light of an intelligence that exists outside of itself. Since the human intellect is superior to physical things, and even to those non-physical things residing within a subject—such as powers, habits, dispositions, and affections—therefore, to grasp these things, the illumination of a **separate substance** <note>such as an angel or a detached celestial mind</note> is not necessary. Instead, an internal light suffices: namely, the **active intellect** <note>original: "intellectus agens"; the part of the mind that actively processes raw sensory data into abstract thoughts</note>, which is the power of the highest part of the soul being discussed here.

Note, therefore, according to **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on the soul were essential to medieval university curricula</note>, that the operation of the active intellect is to illuminate, or to diffuse the light of intelligence over the "sensory forms" existing in the imagination or the **estimative power** <note>original: "extimatione"; the faculty that allows one to perceive "intentions" not found in the five senses, such as a sheep perceiving the "danger" in a wolf</note>. By illuminating them, it **abstracts** them from all material circumstances. Once they are abstracted, it joins or orders them in the **possible intellect** <note>original: "intellectu possibili"; the "blank slate" part of the mind that receives and stores knowledge</note>. 

Just as by the operation of physical light, the appearance of a color is in a way "drawn out" and joined to the pupil of the eye, so too is the operation of the possible intellect. First, it turns itself through consideration toward the forms held in the imagination. When these are illuminated by the light of the active intellect and are abstracted—stripped bare of all the circumstances of matter—they are imprinted upon the possible intellect. As stated above, the possible intellect is then brought into action and "formed"; it is then called the **formed intellect**. 

Following this, the operation of the possible intellect proceeds in stages: 
1. First, regarding **quiddities** <note>original: "quiditates"; the essence or "what-ness" of a thing, such as "human-ness"</note>. 
2. Second, regarding the first **complexions** <note>original: "complexiones"; the joining of simple concepts into a statement or proposition</note>, which are basic principles. 
3. Third, regarding **conclusions**. 

For example: first, the mind knows what a "whole" is. Second, it knows the proposition or "complexion" which is a self-evident principle: such as **"every whole is greater than its part."** Third, it understands the conclusion that follows: namely, that a continuous whole is greater than its part; and so it goes for other things. 

By the path of **induction**, therefore, the universal form itself is gathered, having been abstracted from individual things, and by this, the possible intellect is formed. Once formed, the operation of the possible intellect is perfected in two ways: 
* One way is, so to speak, **inductive**: when it turns its attention toward sensory forms so that a "form" may be abstracted by the action of the active intellect’s light. 
* The other way is, so to speak, **syllogistic**: when it turns toward the abstract concepts <note>original: "species intelligibiles"</note> it has already received in order to infer a new conclusion. 

In this, it differs from the senses. For the sense of sight receives images through the action of light even if the person does not turn their attention toward them; nevertheless, a human judges without turn...

<summary>This page defines the "active intellect" as an internal power of the soul that "abstracts" universal truths from sensory data, citing Augustine and Avicenna. It describes the process by which the mind moves from perceiving individual objects to understanding self-evident logical principles and syllogistic conclusions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the soul acquires knowledge. It moves from the role of the active intellect to the "possible intellect" and then categorizes the higher powers of the mind using a framework that blends the theology of St. Augustine with the faculty psychology of the Persian philosopher Avicenna.</meta>

<header>### On the Soul.</header>

...[illumina]tion. The **possible intellect** <note>original: "intellectus possibilis"; in medieval psychology, this is the "blank slate" part of the mind that has the potential to receive and store knowledge</note>, however, neither receives an intelligible form nor makes a judgment without a "turning toward" <note>original: "conversio"; the act of the mind focusing its attention on a specific mental image</note>. These are the stages of operation for the possible intellect regarding things that come to us through the service of the senses. There is, however, another mode of operation regarding forms that do not require **abstraction** <note>original: "abstractio"; the process of stripping away physical details to reach a universal concept, like "circularity" from a physical "plate"</note>, such as the virtues of knowledge and other things that exist within the soul as their subject. For these species are not abstracted from the **phantasms** <note>original: "phantasmatibus"; mental images derived from sensory experience</note> of sensible things, because the particulars of these universals are not themselves sensible. But since they exist within the soul where they can exist according to their true nature, they do not need separate species by which they might be known, for they are not distinct from the things themselves. To know these, nothing is required except the turning of the possible intellect toward them, along with the illumination of the **active intellect** <note>original: "intellectus agentis"; the "light" of the mind that makes concepts understandable</note>.

### On the division of the powers of the soul according to their order. Chapter 31.

It follows that we should speak of the difference in the intellectual power of apprehension according to the order of its reason. It should be known, therefore, that the intellectual power is subdivided into a **superior part** and an **inferior part**. The superior intellectual part is so named by **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD)</note> when this highest power among the apprehensive forces is turned toward knowing **uncreated truth** <note>God</note> according to the **image**—or likeness—of the Trinity impressed upon it by the Creator.

According to **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on the soul heavily influenced Latin Scholasticism</note>, the image of the Trinity is defined by three things: **Memory**, **Intelligence**, and **Will**.
*   **Memory** is called that power insofar as it preserves the likeness of the first truth, through the likeness of truth impressed by the Creator.
*   **Intelligence** is that which contemplates the first truth through that same impressed likeness.
*   **Will**, as it is understood here, is the power insofar as it loves the first truth through a fixed appetite for it placed there by the Creator.

Or, to put it another way: **Memory**, as it is used here, is that power of the superior intellect by which a rational creature can reflect upon its own **efficient principle** <note>The "cause" that brought it into being—God</note> through the fact that it did not always exist; thus it is certain that it came from Him. **Intelligence** is the impressed power of the superior intellect by which it can contemplate the first truth as being present, like a light present to itself and the cause of its contemplation. **Will** is the power of the superior intellect that is directed or inclined toward that first truth as its final goal.

Note, therefore, that according to this power, certain **judgments of truth** regarding the principles of the sciences are impressed upon us. For example: "of anything, either the negation or the affirmation is true, and never both at once"; and "the whole is always greater than its part," and similar things. These are said to be manifest to the nature of any learner through the light of the likeness of the first truth, according to that part impressed upon us. Regarding this, the fourth Psalm says: *The light of your countenance is signed upon us, O Lord.*

Note also that this power is the same in substance as the **active intellect**. But insofar as this power is considered as a certain nature, it is called the active intellect; however, insofar as it is apprehensive, it is called the **intelligence** or the **mind**. The inferior power of the intellect, if considered as a nature, is called the **possible intellect**; and its acts are those described above.

<summary>This page explains the hierarchy of the human mind, distinguishing between the possible intellect (which processes sensory data) and the superior intellect (which contains innate truths and reflects the Trinity through memory, intelligence, and will). It argues that fundamental logical principles are "impressed" upon the human soul by God as an internal light.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the intellective power of the soul, building on the distinction between the active and possible intellects. It transitions from how the soul receives forms to how it categorizes its own cognitive processes, specifically focusing on how we find new knowledge (investigation) versus how we process or share existing knowledge (judgment and interpretation).</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

If it is considered as an apprehensive power <note>original: "uirtus apprehensiua"; the faculty of the mind that grasps or takes in information</note>, it is called the **speculative intellect** <note>original: "intellectus speculatiuus"; the mind when it is engaged in pure contemplation of truth for its own sake</note> or **reason**.

### On the act of the intellective power. Chapter 32.

Note, therefore, that the principal act of this power—that is, the apprehensive intellect—is **to understand** <note>original: "intelligere"</note>, and it does this through itself. However, besides this, there are auxiliary acts insofar as this power is directed toward things perceived by the senses. According to this, the power is divided based on a fourfold function: **inventive** (or investigative), **judicative**, **memorative**, and **interpretative**.

The inventive and judicative powers are distinguished only by discovery and judgment, following two different paths of understanding. One is the path of inquiry, which seeks truth for its own sake. The other is the path of judgment, which examines truth received from another. One is the way of **experiencing**, and the other is the way of **learning**. To experience is to act by oneself; to learn is to receive from another. 

Alternatively, discovery is the "way of intention" <note>original: "uia intētionis"</note> following the mode of **composition** <note>building individual thoughts into a larger idea</note>, while judgment is the "way of resolution" <note>original: "resolutionis"</note> following the mode of breaking ideas down into their components. Or, the way of discovery is when one moves from the knowledge of an **effect** to the knowledge of its **cause**. The way of judgment, however, is when the effect is known through knowledge of the cause.

The **memorative** and **interpretative** powers are distinguished as follows: the memorative power retains the "species" <note>original: "spēi"; the mental representation or "look" of a concept stored in the mind</note> that has been known. The interpretative power, however, is what provides or shares that knowledge. 

Or they can be distinguished another way: the investigative and memorative powers concern the person’s relationship to themselves. Investigation (or discovery) is for seeking out the unknown, and memory is for retaining what has been discovered. Thus, **thinking** <note>original: "cogitare"</note> serves the inventive power, while memory follows after the act of understanding. 

Likewise, the judicative and interpretative powers concern the person's relationship to others. Judgment is used in receiving knowledge from someone else; interpretation is used in transferring knowledge to someone else. Thus, one of these—judgment—serves the truth that is to be understood. The other—interpretation—comes after the act of understanding.

The specific acts of the **inventive** or **investigative** power are: to exercise ingenuity, to experience, to apprehend, to conceive, and to reason. 
*   **Ingenuity** <note>original: "ingenium"</note> is the extension of the intellect toward the knowledge of unknown things. 
*   **Experience** <note>original: "experiētia"</note> is the certainty of things established through the senses. 
*   **Apprehension** <note>original: "apprehēsio"</note> is the simple grasp of the intellect, such as the concept of "man" or "animal." 
*   **Conception** <note>original: "cõceptio"</note> is the complex grasp of the intellect, such as "man is an animal" or "man is capable of laughter." 
*   **Reasoning** <note>original: "ratiocinatio"</note> is the argumentative process of the intellect, such as "if man is an animal, then man is a sensing being." 

The number of these acts is clear: for investigation either extends itself to know the unknown—which is **ingenuity**—or it follows the path of knowing through the senses—which is **experience**—or it follows the path of knowing through reason. This path of reason is threefold:
1. There is a path toward the knowledge of simple things (such as individual words, or rather the things signified by words); this is **apprehension**.
2. There is a path toward the knowledge of complex things (such as statements or propositions); this is **conception**.
3. There is a path toward the knowledge of a conclusion; this is **reasoning**.

The acts of the **judicative** power are the same as those mentioned, but they function according to a different path. For understanding is reached either through one’s own inquiry or through another person, as in judgment or formal instruction. Nevertheless, insofar as the judicative power is joined to sensible things, specific acts can be assigned: namely...

<summary>This page categorizes the functions of the human intellect into four main powers—investigation, judgment, memory, and interpretation—and defines the logical steps of human thought from simple apprehension to complex reasoning.</summary>
<keywords>speculative intellect, reason, investigation, judgment, memory, interpretation, ingenuity, experience, apprehension, conception, ratiocination</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the intellective powers, moving from the process of discovery and judgment to the specific states of belief (doubt, opinion, and knowledge), the functions of memory, and the nature of human speech. It concludes by introducing the intellective motive power, which drives the soul toward the good.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...to discern insofar as it is joined to the **imagination**; to doubt and to form an opinion insofar as it is joined to the **cogitative power** <note>original: "cogitatiue"; the faculty that processes and combines particular intentions or thoughts</note>; and to know. 

But **doubt** is the acceptance of both sides of an issue along with the fear of being wrong. **Opinion** is the acceptance of one side with the fear of the other. **To know**, however, is the complete act of the intelligence. 

And this is the order: first is discernment, which is to judge between things. Then, if judgment is lacking, there is doubt. If judgment makes progress, it becomes faith or opinion. When judgment finally reaches the understanding of truth, it becomes **knowledge** <note>original: "scientia"</note>. Thus, doubt is a vacillation between the true and the false; opinion is a belief in what is true; but knowledge is the certain comprehension of truth.

The act of **rational memory** is threefold. The first is to retain intelligible forms <note>original: "species intelligibiles"; the mental concepts or "stamps" of ideas</note>. The second is to represent them. The third is to restore lost or forgotten forms; and this is **recollecting** <note>original: "reminisci"</note>, which is how it differs from sensory memory. 

Note that memory is threefold. For there is a memory that is the power to preserve a sensory form. There is also a memory of an intelligible form. And there is also a memory that is the power to preserve the **divine likeness** implanted by the Creator, which was discussed above regarding the "image" <note>referring to the *Imago Dei*, the belief that the human soul mirrors God's nature through memory, intellect, and will</note>.

Regarding the **interpretative power**—which is the power of indicative intelligence—there are two acts: namely, **thinking out** <note>original: "excogitatio"</note> and **signifying**. "Thinking out" is the shaping or forming of a thought for the purpose of expressing it outwardly. "Signifying," on the other hand, is the demonstration of that formed knowledge through signs or words. 

And because signs in practice are often confused and ambiguous to the intellect when one tries to share them with another, words were provided for the manifestation of understanding as distinct and proper signs. Therefore, **speech** <note>original: "locutio"</note> was created to signify understanding. Speech, then, is the outward demonstration through words of the **interior word** conceived in the mind—that is, the outward showing of a thought. 

This power is the same as the **vocative** power—that is, the emission of the voice. For the "vocative" power belongs to the senses and to irrational creatures. But the power of "speech" belongs to rational beings. Hence, neither **magpies** <note>original: "picae"; often used as examples of animals that mimic sounds without meaning</note> nor people who are sleeping are said to truly "speak." For to speak—or to say something—is to utter with **intention**. 

Therefore, because a magpie or a sleeper, even if they utter a meaningful sound, intends nothing by it, they do not truly speak. Speaking and the power of speech belong only to one who utters with intention; and such a being is a rational animal—that is, a human being. The power of speech, as far as intention is concerned, belongs to the intellective part of the soul. But as far as the physical utterance is concerned, it belongs to the sensitive part. For these two things are required for what we call "speaking": namely, to utter with intention.

### On the intellective motive power. Chapter 33.

Next, we must discuss the **intellective motive power** <note>original: "uirtus intellectiua motiua"; the faculty that moves the soul toward a goal or action based on understanding</note>. For just as we have an intellective power ordered toward the **true**, we also have an intellective power ordered toward the **good**. 

Note that there is a motive power toward the good that acts as **nature** <note>as an innate, automatic drive</note>. And there is a motive power that acts through **apprehension** <note>through conscious awareness and choice</note>. 

The motive power toward the good as nature, simply speaking, is threefold. For there is a motive toward the **highest good**, which is called **synderesis** <note>original: "sinderesis"; the spark of conscience that always inclines the soul toward the good</note>. And there is a motive toward the **lowest good**, which is called **sensuality** <note>original: "sensualitas"; the drive toward physical and bodily pleasures</note>. And there is a motive toward a **middle good**, which...

It also introduces the soul's motive powers, distinguishing between the innate drive for the highest good (synderesis) and the lower drive for physical pleasure (sensuality).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the classification of the soul's powers, specifically moving into the "motive powers" (the drives that cause action). It follows from the previous page's introduction of the intellective motive power, which seeks the "good."</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

...is called the **natural will**. For there is a higher good—namely, the **rational good**, which is called the **honorable good** <note>original: "bonum honestum"; a good that is worthy and moral in itself, regardless of its utility</note>. And there is the **simple good**, which draws us by its own power and attracts us by its own dignity. And there is a lower good—namely, the **bodily good**: the **pleasurable good** of the flesh, which is an **apparent good** <note>original: "bonum apparens"; something that seems good on the surface but may not be beneficial</note>, or a good in a certain respect. And there is a **middle good**, which is the **natural good**, such as being and living. To understand and to feel, and whatever things are substantial to nature. Therefore, there is a **natural appetite** <note>original: "appetitus naturalis"; an innate drive or desire</note> fixed and implanted in the soul for the honorable good, and for virtues and virtuous works. For there is also an ingrained appetite for the good that is pleasurable to the flesh, such as those regarding food and the pleasures of sexual union. There is also an ingrained appetite and natural love for the [middle] good—namely, for being, living, feeling, and understanding. Therefore, **synderesis** <term>synderesis: the innate "spark" of conscience that provides the basic principles of morality and inclines the soul toward the good</term> is a naturally moving power that inclines the rational soul toward the honorable good and pulls it away from what is dishonorable. **Sensuality**, however, is the power that naturally moves and inclines [the soul] toward the good that preserves nature. **Will**, however, is the power naturally moving toward the substantial good of nature. Thus, the motive power is distinguished as a "nature" that acts without apprehension <note>that is, acting instinctively rather than through conscious thought</note>. Likewise, the motive power that acts through apprehension is distinguished [as follows]. Because one motive power is determined toward the **unchangeable good**. And another is determined toward the **changeable good**. And another is an indeterminate motive power that relates to both. The motive power determined toward the unchangeable good is the **highest part of reason** or intelligence, which possesses a likeness of the highest goodness impressed by the Creator. According to this, the **image of the Trinity** <note>original: "imago trinitatis"; the Christian theological concept that the human soul mirrors the Three-in-One nature of God</note> is determined in the soul through mind, knowledge, and love. For the **Mind** <note>original: "Mens"</note> is the power that preserves the likeness of the highest goodness implanted by the Creator. **Knowledge** <note>original: "Noticia"</note>, however, comes through the contemplation of it. **Love** <note>original: "Amor"</note> comes through the desire for it. And just as the highest part of the apprehensive, intellective power is the image of the Trinity through the implanted likeness of the First Truth, so the highest part of the intellective motive power is the image of the Trinity through the implanted likeness of the Highest Goodness. From this impression of goodness, the dictates of the **natural law** <note>original: "legis naturalis"</note> are born. For example: "What you do not wish to be done to you, do not do to others." And again: "Whatever you want men to do to you, do also to them" <note>Matthew 7:12</note>. The motive power determined toward the changeable good is, of itself, the **sensory motive power**. Yet it can be considered in two ways: in itself, or in relation to reason or the intellect. Insofar as it is in that relation, it is subject to reason itself, as is evident in the **concupiscible** and **irascible** powers <note>the "concupiscible" power desires simple goods like food, while the "irascible" power fights for difficult goods or against obstacles</note> and the power that moves the limbs. Insofar as it is in itself, it is determined and ordered toward the sensory good, which is changeable. However, the motive power that relates indeterminately to both goods—namely, the unchangeable or the changeable—is the power of reason below the highest part, or **free will** <note>original: "liberum arbitrium"</note>, about which it says in Ecclesiasticus 15: "He left him in the hand of his own counsel." And further on: "Before man is life and death, good and evil; whatever pleases him shall be given to him." The "hand of counsel" is the power of judgment. Life...

...and the good of the soul consists in the turning of free will toward the unchangeable good, which is God. Death, however, and the evil of the soul consist in turning away from the unchangeable good and turning toward a changeable good <note>original: "bonum commutabile"; referring to worldly things that pass away, as opposed to the eternal nature of the divine</note>. This power is called by the philosophers the **practical intellect**—that is, the "operative" intellect—because it is the principle and source of things to be done. ### Giles of Rome, On the Rule of Princes, Book One, Part Three: On the Twelve Passions of the Soul.

<image-desc>A large rubricated initial letter 'Q' in red ink begins the following paragraph.</image-desc>

There are twelve passions of the soul: namely love, hatred, desire, aversion <note>original: "abhominatio"; a turning away in loathing</note>, delight, sadness, hope, despair, fear, boldness, anger, and mildness. But note that "mildness" sometimes signifies a passion opposite to anger, and sometimes a middle virtue between anger and the passion of mildness. This [overlap in meaning] is due to a lack of specific vocabulary. Therefore, the "sufficiency" or the specific number of passions can be understood this way: passions do not properly exist except in the **sensitive appetite** <term>sensitive appetite: the part of the soul shared with animals that reacts to physical sensations and perceptions</term>. For the **intellectual appetite**—because it is not in the body, or rather, it is not a power attached to a physical organ—is removed from passions of this kind. The sensitive appetite is thus divided into the **irascible** and the **concupiscible** <term>irascible vs. concupiscible: the "concupiscible" appetite seeks simple pleasures and avoids simple pains; the "irascible" appetite deals with goods or evils that are difficult to attain or avoid</term>. The first six passions belong to the concupiscible appetite. Therefore, the number is determined thus: every movement of the soul pertaining to the concupiscible appetite is taken either in respect to a good or in respect to an evil. If in respect to a good, this happens in three ways. For when a good is perceived: first, it pleases; second, we tend toward it; third, once it is obtained, we rest in it. Regarding the first, it is **love**. Regarding the second, it is **desire**. Regarding the third, it is **joy** or **delight**. If it is in respect to an evil, this is also threefold, for when an evil is perceived: first, it displeases in itself. Second, it is refused or rejected. Third, if it is held, it causes sorrow. Regarding the first, it is **hatred**. Regarding the second, **aversion**. Regarding the third, **pain** or **sadness**. The other six passions differ from the ones mentioned above. Because the passions of the concupiscible appetite regard good or evil taken simply or absolutely; but the passions of the **irascible** appetite regard good or evil as **arduous** <note>original: "ut arduum"; meaning a good that is difficult to reach or an evil that is difficult to overcome</note>. They are taken, therefore, either in respect to an arduous good or in respect to an arduous evil. If in respect to an arduous good, this is twofold: either because we tend toward it—this is **hope**. For hope is nothing other than to tend toward some arduous good; for concerning goods taken simply, there can be love or desire or delight. However, hope does not exist concerning any goods unless they have the character of being arduous. Therefore, if we tend toward an arduous good, it is called hope. But if we fall short of such a good, it is **despair**. Hope, therefore, and despair are taken in respect to a good. The other passions of the irascible appetite are taken in respect to an evil. For evil can be considered in two ways: either as future or as present.

<summary>This page concludes a treatise on the soul's relationship with God and begins Giles of Rome’s analysis of the twelve passions, categorizing them based on the soul's "desiring" (concupiscible) and "struggling" (irascible) appetites.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, Giles of Rome, passions of the soul, sensitive appetite, concupiscible, irascible, practical intellect, hope and despair, love and hatred</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the twelve passions of the soul, specifically focusing on the logical order and relationship between them. This text is part of Book One, Part Three of Giles of Rome’s "On the Rule of Princes," which was often appended to or studied alongside Albertus Magnus’s works on the soul.</meta>

<header>On the Soul.</header>

If [an evil] is considered as something in the future: this occurs in two ways. Either we confront it, and this is **boldness** <note>original: "audacia"</note>; or we flee from it, and this is **fear**. If, however, the evil is present: this also occurs in two ways. Either we rise up from this to take vengeance, and this is **anger**; or we flee from such vengeance, and this is **mildness** <note>original: "mansuetudo"; here used to describe the passive reaction to a present harm rather than the virtue of gentleness</note> as a passion.

The order of the aforementioned passions can be considered in two ways: either individually or by combination.

By combination, **love** and **hatred** are in the first place: because all other passions take their origin either from love or from hatred. For whoever is moved by any passion is so moved either because they love something or because they hate something.

In the second place are **desire** and **aversion** <note>original: "abhominatio"; literally "abomination," meaning a strong turning away from something loathsome</note>. For desire immediately rests upon love. When we love something, we either desire to have it (if we do not possess it) or we desire to be preserved in possessing it (if we already have it). Aversion, however, immediately rests upon hatred: because as soon as we hate something, we feel aversion toward it.

In the third place are **hope** and **despair**: because they are taken with respect to a good, and they precede fear, boldness, anger, and mildness.

In the fourth place are **fear** and **boldness**. For since something is a future event before it is a present one, fear and boldness—which are taken with respect to a future evil—precede anger and mildness, which are taken with respect to a present evil.

The final passions are **delight** <note>original: "delectatio"</note> and **sadness**: because they follow after the other passions. For whatever other passion a person may feel, they end in being either delighted or saddened.

Taken individually, they ought to be ordered in this way: Love is prior to hatred. Desire prior to aversion. Hope prior to despair. Fear [prior to] boldness. Anger prior to mildness. Delight prior to sadness. This is because a passion taken with respect to a "good" is always prior to a passion taken with respect to an "evil." For the appetite, of itself and primarily, tends toward the good. If it flees from evil, this is a secondary consequence, insofar as fleeing evil has the character of a "good" <note>Scholastic philosophers argued that avoiding a bad thing is itself a "good" act, so the movement toward good always comes first in the soul's logic</note>.

Therefore, **love** is the first movement and the first passion. **Hatred**, however, takes its origin from love. For unless we loved something, we would hold nothing in hatred. It is because we love the good and justice that we hate and feel aversion toward thieves and the unjust. Furthermore, **desire**, which rests on love, precedes **aversion**, which rests on hatred.

**Hope**, truly, precedes **despair**. For if the first thing willed is the good, then whatever is willed is willed under the logic of being a good. The passion that more closely joins us to the good as such precedes other passions. And because "tending toward the good" is to be joined to the good, and being joined to the good is prior to failing in it, **hope** (by which we tend toward the good) precedes **despair** (by which we fail in it).

**Fear**, however, precedes **boldness**. For just as the appetite by its nature tends toward the good—and therefore the passion tending toward the good is prior to the passion that fails in it—so also fleeing evil has the character of a good. Therefore, fear, through which we flee evil, is prior to boldness, through which we confront it. And so it is...

<sig>g ii</sig>

<summary>This page establishes a logical hierarchy for the twelve passions, arguing that they all stem from love and hatred, and that passions directed toward 'the good' always precede those directed toward 'evil.'</summary>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the passions of the soul, moving from the basic pairs of love/hatred and desire/aversion to identifying the "four principal passions." It explains how these emotions flow from one to another based on whether an object is good or evil, and whether it is in the future or the present.</meta>

# On the Soul

In the category of passions, [delight] is prior to **mildness** <note>original: "mansuetudine"; referring back to the end of the previous page where mildness was defined as a passive response to present evil</note>. For mildness is not properly a passion itself, but rather a failing or cessation of passion. Furthermore, **delight**, which regards the good, is prior to **sadness**, which regards evil. 

Of these passions, four are considered the **principal** ones: namely **hope**, **fear**, **joy**, and **sadness**. This is evident when they are compared to the other passions, to their objects, and to the substance—that is, the powers—of the soul.

First, it is evident because all passions of the soul seem to be ordered toward these. Those passions regarding the good appear to be ordered toward hope and joy; those regarding evil toward fear and sadness. This is because a passion regarding the good begins first with **love**, then proceeds to **desire**, and ends in **hope** while the good is still in the future. Finally, it results in **joy** and **delight** when that good is present and has been obtained. 

Similarly, regarding evil, the process begins with **hatred**, proceeds to **flight** or **abomination** <note>original: "abhominationem"; a strong turning away from something loathsome</note>, and ends in **fear** if the evil is in the future. Finally, it ends in **sadness** or **pain** if that evil is actually present.

Second, the same is clear from the timing of the objects. Every passion is taken in respect to a good or an evil, which are considered as being either in the future or in the present. 
* If a good is in the future, it is **hope**. 
* If it is present, it is **joy** or **delight**. 
* If an evil is in the future, it is **fear**. 
* If it is present, it is **pain** or **sadness**.

Third, the same is clear from the powers of the soul. The *concupiscible* power <term>the part of the soul that naturally desires simple pleasures and avoids basic pains</term> tends toward the good in itself—that is, the good as good. The *irascible* power <term>the "spirited" or "combative" part of the soul that deals with obstacles</term> tends toward the good as something *arduous* or difficult. 

Since a thing is "most good" when it is already obtained (which causes delight or joy), and "most evil" when it is already present (which causes pain or sadness), it follows that **delight** and **sadness** are the principal passions of the concupiscible power. **Hope** and **fear**, however, are the principal passions of the irascible power. For since the irascible power tends toward the good as something difficult, it is considered most difficult when it is still in the future; this is the realm of hope. Likewise, a future evil is feared as something that is not easily avoidable.

Besides these [twelve] passions, the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> in the second book of the *Ethics* seems to enumerate six others: namely **zeal**, **grace**, **nemesis** (which is indignation at the prosperity of the wicked), **mercy**, **envy**, and **shame** (or modesty). However, all of these are reduced to the passions mentioned before. 

For **zeal** <note>original: "zelus"</note> is reduced to **love**, because zeal is nothing other than an intense love. The things we love intensely are either physical or spiritual. If they are physical things—because such things cannot be possessed by one person if they are possessed by another—zeal regarding them is usually defined as: *an intense love that does not allow for a partner in the thing loved.* This is why some people are said to be **jealous** <note>original: "zelotipi"</note> regarding a person, if they do not wish to have any partner in their affection for them. Therefore, an intense love for physical things is seen to be a "private" love...

->ii g<-

<summary>This page identifies hope, fear, joy, and sadness as the four principal passions, explaining how they arise from the soul's desiring and combative powers. It also begins to categorize other emotions, like zeal and envy, as variations of these primary movements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the classification of the passions of the soul. Having established the four principal passions (hope, fear, joy, and sadness), the author now explains how secondary passions—such as zeal, grace, shame, envy, and mercy—are actually specific variations or "species" of those primary ones.</meta>

# On the Soul

...and blameworthy: not allowing a partner in the thing loved <note>This continues a discussion on the exclusive nature of certain types of love, likely romantic or possessive love</note>. But in respect to intellectual goods and in respect to virtues: if the love is intense, it is laudable and, as it were, shared. For never would anyone be virtuous, nor would they properly love the virtues, if they did not wish to have a partner in them. This kind of **zeal** <note>original: "zelus"</note> in respect to honorable things is defined by **the Philosopher** <note>A standard medieval reference to Aristotle</note> in the second book of the *Rhetoric*. He states that it is a sadness regarding such goods, not because they are present in another, but because they are not present in oneself. Therefore, zeal is reduced to **love**.

In the same way, **grace** <note>original: "gratia"; here meaning favor or the inclination to be "gracious"</note> is produced from love, making someone gracious to another. For grace, as the Philosopher treats it and insofar as it is a passion, is nothing other than a certain movement of the soul by which someone is inclined to bestow benefits.

**Shame** <note>original: "verecundia"</note> is reduced to **fear**. For one can fear in two ways: namely, corruptions and dishonors. He who dreads corruptive evils <note>Physical harms or death</note> is called fearful. But he who dreads base and dishonorable things is called shameful. Shame is nothing other than the fear of dishonor, or the fear of being without glory. Shame is also customarily called **blushing** <note>original: "erubescencia"</note>, because those feeling shame commonly blush, just as those feeling fear turn pale. For from the fact that someone believes they are losing their life—which is an internal good—they fear; and in fearing, the blood retreats to the internal organs, and the exterior parts remain pale. But from the fact that someone believes they are losing glory and honor—which are external goods—they feel shame; and the blood returns to the exterior and the face turns red. Therefore, the fear of something corruptive and of losing internal goods, because it does not have a specific name, retains the common name and is called "fear." But the fear of losing honor has a special name and is called "shame" or "blushing." Thus, it is reduced to fear.

**Envy**, **mercy**, and **nemesis** (or **indignation**) are reduced to **sadness** as if they were species of it. For someone can be saddened either by the evil of another or by their good. If one is saddened by another's evil, and especially if they believe that person suffers it undeservedly, this is **mercy**. For as it is said in the second book of the *Rhetoric*: Mercy is nothing other than a certain sadness regarding a visible corruptive or distressing evil in respect to one who is unworthy of receiving that evil. 

If, however, there is sadness not for the evil of another but for their good, this can happen in two ways. Either it is for the good itself, and this is **envy**, which as it is said in the second book of the *Rhetoric*, is nothing other than a certain sadness over the apparent attainment of some goods by **equals** <note>original: "similes"; Aristotle argues envy occurs between people of similar status or profession</note>—not so that one might gain something, but because of them. For envy is pain at the apparent good of someone, not because the person themselves lacks that good, but because the other whom they envy excels in that good. And envy exists most especially among equals. Whence "potters envy potters" and "blacksmiths envy blacksmiths," and so on for others. 

If, however, there is sadness at the good of another, not in just any way, but because it is held unworthily by them, this is **nemesis** or **indignation**. For according to the Philosopher in the second book of the *Rhetoric*: Nemesis or indignation is to be saddened by that...

<summary>This page analyzes complex emotions—zeal, grace, shame, envy, and mercy—by categorizing them as specific forms of the primary passions, love, fear, and sadness. It notably explains the physiological difference between the paleness of fear and the blushing of shame based on the type of "good" being threatened.</summary>
