# On the Soul

...who unworthily seem to prosper. Because actions and habits must be carefully considered according to the diversity of the passions:

->Egidius’s treatise on the passions ends here.<- <note>original: "Finit egidius de passionibus." Egidius likely refers to Giles of Rome, a prominent medieval philosopher.</note>

### On the Intellectual Power

From the collection of what has been said concerning reason or the intellect: that which is highest within it regarding movement is called **synderesis** <note>The innate spark of conscience that inclines a human toward the good and away from evil</note>. Generally, as has been said regarding knowledge, the **active intellect** <note>original: "intellectus agens"; the faculty that processes sensory data into abstract concepts</note> stands in the universal. But it should be noted that the "active" [intellect] is so called insofar as it abstracts species <note>mental images or concepts</note> from the imagination and composes them in the **possible intellect** <note>original: "intellectus possibilis"; the faculty that receives and stores knowledge</note>. 

**Synderesis**, however, as was said, is a motive power. **John of Damascus** <note>original: "Dama."; an 8th-century theologian influential in medieval scholasticism</note> calls it the **natural will**; but they differ in this way: synderesis only regards rational goods. The natural will, as John of Damascus himself wishes, regards natural, rational, and vital things. 

Following that and above that part of reason called the **"man"** <note>original: "vir"; a traditional metaphor for the higher, contemplative reason</note> is the intellectual part by which one contemplates divine or eternal things in the way that is possible. This differs from synderesis because synderesis does not deliberate at all, whereas this part deliberates well. Likewise, just as synderesis is affectively drawn toward the good, so it is a detester of evil; hence it always "murmurs" against evil. This intellectual part, however, does not regard evil, because its only object is the eternal, where there is no evil.

And if it is said that it ought to be ordered according to this, it must be said that it is not, because this part deliberates and knows that it desires one thing and always remains in the universal. After that is the part of reason called the **"man,"** which they extend so far that it comprehends the very **Trinity** of which we have already spoken, and especially insofar as it is the **image of the uncreated Trinity** <note>original: "imago trinitatis increate"</note>; regarding which a brief statement was made, as the brevity of this treatise requires. 

After that is the **"woman"** <note>original: "mulier"; a metaphor for lower reason or the faculty oriented toward worldly affairs and the senses</note>, who is joined to sensuality; and she often behaves differently from it, for she possesses the enticement, according to which she always sees the "apple" being offered to her <note>An allusion to the Fall of Man in Genesis, used here to describe how the lower reason is tempted by sensory pleasures</note>. 

**Augustine** <note>original: "Augu."</note> maintains that these two parts differ only according to their office <note>their function or role</note>, and that there is only one power. In this power—or in these two parts—is the **will** properly speaking, in which **consent** <note>original: "consensus"</note> resides. 

But consent is twofold. One is in the true logic of truth; and thus it is knowledge, and thus it is the work of reason—namely, the perfection of the true according to its cognitive power. For the "true," insofar as it is true, is the perfection or the "good" of the intellect, to which the will consents—or rather, assents—because the good is the object that perfects the intellect. For perfection, or the object, properly belongs to the cognitive power when it is joined to it. 

The perfection or knowledge of the soul itself is **science** <note>meaning "organized knowledge" or "demonstrable truth"</note> through the speculative way; and then reason, insofar as it is in the cognitive power, is called **intellect**; but they differ in the manner already stated. 

There is also consent to the **good** under the logic of the **true**. I say "under the logic of the true" because the intellect judges a thing to be good or evil and stops there, proceeding no further. If, however, it proceeds further and judges it to be good so that it may be *done*, then it is the motive will; in the first way, it is merely cognitive. Likewise, if it does not yet judge it only to be good...

<sig>iii</sig>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the passions and introduces the intellectual faculties of the soul, distinguishing between higher reason (contemplating the divine) and lower reason (managing sensory world), using the metaphors of "man" and "woman." It explores how the intellect recognizes truth and how the will provides consent to what the mind perceives as good.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, synderesis, active intellect, possible intellect, John of Damascus, St. Augustine, higher reason, lower reason, will, consent, Trinity, Giles of Rome</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philosophical discussion of the soul's intellectual faculties, specifically defining the mechanics of free will. It then transitions to the book's colophon (publication details) and the beginning of the "Tabula" or table of contents for the entire volume, which covers natural philosophy and Aristotle's Physics.</meta>

# On the Soul

...that it may be done; but in judging, the soul is affected and desires that thing as if it were already done. Whether it tends toward the deed itself, it is then called **free will** <note>original: *liberum arbitrium*</note>, since free will encompasses both reason and the will, as spoken of in the first manner. Therefore, free will resides in both judgment and desire. 

It must therefore be said that consent regarding a thing which is done or not done is a cognition of the reason; that it *be* done or *not* be done belongs to the will. For this is the process: Reason judges a thing to be good or evil, and in this judgment the "consent of reason" agrees, insofar as it is within the cognitive power. Then follows the "consent of the will," which says "let it be done" or "let it not be done," and then it is called the consent of the motive reason. 

Moreover, the consent of both toward the true and the good is called free will, or the "consent of the free choice." Hence, free will includes in its definition both reason and will. For free will is a **faculty of reason and will** <term>facultas rationis et voluntatis: a standard scholastic definition used to explain how humans choose between paths</term> by which good is chosen when grace assists, and evil is chosen when grace is absent.

### Table of this Work
The first part or book of which briefly explains the books of **Physics** <note>Referring to Aristotle's *Physics*, the foundation of medieval natural science</note> in 17 chapters.

**Preface:** Where, having first set forth the general division of philosophy, and intending to treat only of natural philosophy, the author declares in how many ways "nature" and "the natural" are spoken of, and what its subject is. Also into how many parts this work is rationally divided and ordered. Chapter 1.

**What a body is**, and its various types. And which three things are the primary principles of natural things. Chapter 2.

**Concerning matter** <note>original: *materia*</note>: what it is, how it is understood, by how many names it is called and why, and its various types. Chapter 3.

**Concerning privation** <note>original: *privatio*; in Aristotelian philosophy, this is the absence of a quality or "form" that a thing is naturally capable of having</note>: its notification and division. Chapter 4.

**Concerning form**, which is threefold: namely Physical, Mathematical, and Metaphysical. Physical form is defined and divided; three opinions on the origin of forms are recited; and by how many names it is called. Chapter 5.

**Concerning the "per se" causes** <note>Essential or direct causes, as opposed to accidental ones</note> of natural things. And concerning the difference between a **principle** and a **cause**; how many causes there are, how they are compared to one another, and in how many ways something is called the cause of another. Chapter 6.

**Concerning accidental causes**: namely, **fortune and chance**. Chapter 7.

**Diverse opinions concerning motion**; concerning the "plenum" and the "vacuum" <note>the debate over whether empty space can exist</note>; the dense and the rare, the fast and the slow; and incidentally, concerning the **motion of an angel**. Chapter 8.

**Definitions of motion** and its measure. Concerning the difference between continuous things...

# On the Soul

...contiguous <note>original: *tigua*, likely a fragment of *contigua*</note> things and consequences: and from where unity or plurality arises. **Chapter 9.**
Divisions and differences of motion. **Chapter 10.**
On rest: what it is and its various types. **Chapter 11.**
On place: what it is and its various types, and in how many ways something is said to be in a place. **Chapter 12.**
On time: what it is. **Chapter 13.**
In how many ways something may be in time, and the differences of time. **Chapter 14.**
On eternity, where a threefold duration is distinguished regarding substance and operation; and regarding the beginning and end or the lack thereof: what eternity is and its properties. **Chapter 15.**
On the "now" <note>original: *nunc*, refers to the instantaneous point of the present</note> of eternity. **Chapter 16.**
On the aeon <term>aevum: in medieval philosophy, a measure of time for beings that do not change, such as angels, sitting between human time and divine eternity</term> and aeiternal beings. **Chapter 17.**

### Chapters of the second book: namely, on the heavens and the world.

How the things to be said follow from what has been said: and the order of the discussion is proposed. **Chapter 1.**
That there is only one mode of the body and not several, proven by Aristotle <note>original: *Ar.*, shorthand for Aristoteles</note>. **Chapter 2.**
That the heavens are finite: where it is shown in how many ways "finite" and "infinite" are defined: that no body is infinite, nor are the heavens, which are the most noble: containing all things, incorruptible, active, not passive, spherical, and of circular and finite motion. **Chapter 3.**
That all things are within the heavens and nothing is outside them. **Chapter 4.**
That the heavens are neither an element nor composed of elements <term>elementatum: a substance formed from the mixing of the four basic elements</term>. **Chapter 5.**
On the nobility of the heavens. **Chapter 6.**
On the motion of the heavens. **Chapter 7.**
On the number of the heavens according to the philosophers and the theologians. **Chapter 8.**

### Chapters of the third book: namely, on the elements.

How the things to be said follow from what has been said and the placement of the elements: and the order of the discussion is proposed: and the meanings of this name "element": and how elements are contrary to each other and how they are not. **Chapter 1.**
In which the opinions of the ancients regarding the elements are presented. **Chapter 2.**
On the primary qualities of the elements: which and how many they are, and their properties: what is homogeneous and heterogeneous. **Chapter 3.**
On the generation and corruption <note>In this context, "corruption" means the breaking down or decay of a substance</note> of the elements and things composed of elements. **Chapter 4.**
On other motions of the elements, namely: alteration, growth, decay, change of place, contact, mixing, confusion, and composition. **Chapter 5.**

### Chapters of the fourth book: namely, on inanimate things composed of elements or on "meteors."

After the continuation concerning the fivefold mixing or composition of the elements, by which five types of mixtures or elements result: which fivefold division is reduced to a twofold one with further subdivisions, beyond which is a singular union. **Chapter 1.**
<note>original: *methaurorum*, referring to Aristotle's "Meteorology," which studied atmospheric phenomena and minerals</note>

->May, 1937<-

# Albertus Magnus: Philosophy for the Common Man
## Venice, Georgius Arrivabenus, 31 August 1496

Printed with gothic types, in quarto <note>A book size where each sheet of paper is folded twice to create four leaves.</note> form, 37 lines on a page. It has 54 leaves, the last blank. The size of a leaf is 206 X 147 mm., while the printed text measures 157 X 108 mm. There is a woodcut title-page, with type-set inscriptions. There are also three other wood-

<column-break/>

cuts, one a full-page reproduction of a human head. The text begins with a handsome woodcut initial, and there are other initials in red and blue. Bound in the original boards, covered with sheepskin.
Hain *506; B.M.C., vol. V, p. 386. <insert>St A 269</insert>

---

This is a compendium consisting of five books on “physics” <note>original: "physica"</note>, “on the heavens and the world” <note>original: "de coelo et mundo"</note>, “on the elements” <note>original: "de elementis"</note>, “meteorology” <note>original: "methaurorum"</note>, and “on the soul” <note>original: "de anima"</note>. Since it gives in a small compass a variety of subjects, the volume has been called *Philosophia pauperum*, which perhaps may be translated as “Philosophy for the common man.” The five essays are included in Volume V of Borgnet’s edition of Albert’s works.

The treatise on physics discusses such subjects as matter, form, movement, rest, place, time, eternity, and so on. The second treatise undertakes to prove that the world is one; that the skies are finite; that all the extant bodies are located in the sky and none outside of it; and that there are several celestial spheres. The treatise on the elements is sometimes entitled “On Generation and Corruption” <note>original: "De generatione et corruptione"</note>, as generation and corruption are its main subjects. In the fourth treatise, the strange word “meteorology” <note>original: "methaurorum"</note> means meteorology. Here the phenomena of rain, snow, fog, thunder, lightning, earthquakes, and the various winds are described. Finally, the treatise on the soul scrutinizes the characteristics of that mysterious substance. Albert maintains that man has three kinds of soul — “vegetative” <note>original: "vegetabilis"</note>, “sensory” <note>original: "sensibilis"</note>, and “rational” <note>original: "rationalis"</note>. The last chapters are concerned with the intellect, which has a “higher” and a “lower” level.

As in his other works, the author first states the opinions of authorities. In case of difference, on almost every point Aristotle decides the argument.

The last five pages of the volume contain passages on the passions of the soul from the *On the Government of Princes* <note>original: "De regimine principum"</note> by Aegidius de Columna <note>A 13th-century philosopher and theologian also known as Giles of Rome.</note>. (*For a description of this work see the December 1933 issue of* MORE BOOKS.)

Bought in December 1936.

<summary>A bibliographical description of a 1496 edition of Albertus Magnus’s Philosophia Pauperum, detailing its physical characteristics and its role as a summary of Aristotelian natural science.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, Philosophy for the Common Man, incunabula, Georgius Arrivabenus, Venice, natural philosophy, Aristotle, Aegidius de Columna, On the Government of Princes, history of science</keywords>
